- **[Charts as Epistemic Actions for Experience-Driven Agentic Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30072)**  `arXiv:2610.30072`  
  _Xinzhe Wang, Xudong Weng, Zang Li, Shaolei Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data agents built on ReAct-style loops now execute complex analytical workflows over structured data, yet their intermediate reasoning still runs almost entirely through text. Human analysts instead move between numbers, tables, and charts, reading visual evidence to expose trends and anomalies that text summaries drop. We ask whether charts can serve as internal epistemic actions inside a data agent's reasoning loop, and how to teach an agent to use them. We first build \probe{}, a suite of 200 controlled tasks under four matched interface conditions, and find a persistent gap between what agents can read and what they choose to use. Agents score as well from self-generated charts as from numeric output, yet plotting access alone does not translate into consistent use. Task slices show that charts lead on temporal, pattern, and anomaly reasoning, while text leads on precise reading and conditional verification. We then propose \method{}, which distils two families of experience from conditioned trajectory rollouts. One family records how to select and verify analytical operations, and the other records when to invoke text, charts, or both. A trajectory judge scores outcomes and labels intermediate steps, and a validation gate admits only experience that improves held-out performance. On three held-out benchmarks with four LLM backbones, \method{} raises analytical accuracy and visual evidence quality over matched baselines, and backbones that never drew charts begin using them, all without touching model parameters.
  </details>

- **[Which Differences Matter? Controlled Tests of Rule Transfer, Joint Reliability, and Information Use in Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30045)**  `arXiv:2610.30045`  
  _Tianyou Lai, Wentao Yue, Zhiwei Zuo, Qilei Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models map stated rules and observed states to discrete answers. Aggregate accuracy leaves unresolved which rule and information changes guide those answers. We connect controlled input changes to transfer, joint reliability, and directed information use through executable arithmetic rules. Semantic rule separation and crossed rule and state tests make transfer and joint success separately measurable. We compare equivalent wordings, calibrate joint correctness through marginal-preserving reassociation, and alter tables to define alternative answer targets. Adaptation gains increase with nearest-source answer agreement; fully conflicting quartets yield small pooled gains and different gain signs across source splits. Equivalent rewrites have opposing task-level effects, linking transfer gains to rule presentation. Joint-score departures depend on which rule and state associations the reference preserves. A decision service changes answers more across equivalent wordings than across identical-text repeats and usually follows the answer targets implied by altered table values or mappings.
  </details>

- **[Robust Soft Skill Adaptation via Behavioral Localization in Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30041)**  `arXiv:2610.30041`  
  _Zijian Jiang, Xilu Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text skills can extend the capabilities of tool-using agents, but long skill descriptions increase computational costs. Soft prompts provide a compact alternative, yet learning from entire successful trajectories may also introduce unnecessary behavioral changes. We find that the useful effect of a text skill is highly concentrated in a small subset of trajectory positions. Based on this observation, we propose Behavioral Localization, which compares the frozen agent with and without the text skill to identify positions where the skill both increases the likelihood of successful tokens and meaningfully changes the next token distribution. We use these localized positions to supervise a short soft prompt. Experiments on SpreadsheetBench with a frozen Qwen3.6-35B-A3B agent show that the top 10\% of trajectory positions capture 96.12\% of the total positive skill gain. In the shared prompt setting, selective supervision improves success from 30.36\% with full trajectory to 36.79\%. Under task-specific conditions, using the top 10\% localized positions achieves 57.38\%, outperforming both the full text skill and the on-policy OPCD baseline. These results show that a small set of behaviorally relevant trajectory positions can provide effective supervision of soft skills.
  </details>

- **[GuideEvolve: Self-Evolving Agent Framework with Expert Guidance and 3Ts Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30017)**  `arXiv:2610.30017`  
  _Meihong Jia, Yasemin Timar, Jonathan Taws_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study introduces GuideEvolve, a self-evolving agent framework incorporating expert guidance and three autonomous regulatory mechanisms, collectively termed the 3Ts: Think Fast and Slow, Throwback, and Termination by choice. The framework addresses critical limitations in extant self-evolving agent paradigms through dual innovations: (1) a task-specific expert-evaluation-driven evolutionary pipeline, and (2) the integration of 3Ts mechanisms to facilitate cognitive optimization, performance recovery via temporal rollback, and adaptive termination protocols. Empirical validation across the MGSM and MMLU benchmarks demonstrates that GuideEvolve attains performance metrics comparable to current state-of-the-art models (89.8% accuracy versus Gödel Agent's 90.6% on MGSM; 89.1% versus 87.9% on MMLU), while exhibiting notably reduced variance (25-59%) and enhanced robustness. Furthermore, the framework achieves a 100% evolution success rate, superior to the baseline's 82%, diminishes required optimization iterations by approximately 50% (from 30 iterations in baseline to 15.1 and 13.4 across tasks), and provides improved stability for production deployment.
  </details>

- **[TIDE: Tree-Induced Fine-Grained Diagnostic Evaluation from Model Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29985)**  `arXiv:2610.29985`  
  _Jiayi Chen, Hui Li, Yujiong Shen, Qiyuan Peng, Yanxin Li, Chenhao Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark performance is the dominant standard for comparing large language models (LLMs), typically summarized by a single aggregate score, such as accuracy, pass rate, or rubric score. This aggregate score provides limited information about a model's underlying capabilities, yet understanding these capabilities is crucial for model development and evaluation. To this end, we propose Tree-Induced Diagnostic Evaluation (TIDE), which recasts diagnostic evaluation as behavior-driven decision-tree induction to perform fine-grained capability diagnosis. TIDE recursively builds a hierarchical capability tree from benchmark samples and model scores: at each node, an LLM judge proposes candidate splits and performs sample-to-group classification, and behavior-based information gain selects among them. We apply TIDE to five models across four benchmarks to demonstrate its effectiveness, and find fine-grained capability contrasts invisible at the aggregate level: Claude Opus 4.6 achieves 100% on microeconomic market reasoning where GPT-5.4 scores 83.3%, but trails GPT-5.4 on contract law reasoning with 77.8% versus 88.9%. We hope TIDE can be extended to past and future benchmarks so that the rich behavioral signal in evaluation results is fully leveraged for understanding model capabilities.
  </details>

- **[UserSWE: Evaluating Coding Agents in Multi-Turn Conversations with Dynamic Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29822)**  `arXiv:2610.29822`  
  _Zhan-Yu Wu, Ji Luo, Alyssa Wu, Junjielong Xu, Richeng Xuan, Zhichao Hu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As coding agents become more capable, developers increasingly use them to fix bugs through multi-turn interactions. Existing benchmarks do not adequately cover this scenario. Most present a complete issue report in a single turn without interaction. Their evaluation can produce both false positives and false negatives, accepting incorrect patches or rejecting correct ones. We introduce UserSWE for evaluating coding agents' ability to fix repository-level bugs through multi-turn interactions with dynamic users and execution-grounded verdicts at every turn. Its construction pipeline turns repository history and existing single-turn benchmarks into multi-turn tasks. At each turn, information pools constrain what the simulated user can reveal, while a persona chain sampled from a transition model calibrated on 20,859 development sessions governs how it speaks. Tests run after every turn, and a pair of agents from different model families writes and gates each verdict, using test results as primary evidence and model judgment only for aspects the tests cannot resolve. We evaluate eleven agents on 72 tasks from 42 repositories and find that agent rankings change with the interaction budget. Claude Fable 5.1 resolves the most tasks in one turn, GPT-6-Astra resolves the most by the eighth turn, and Claude Opus 5 leads at most intermediate budgets. These results indicate that single-turn performance is insufficient to characterize interactive coding ability.
  </details>

- **[COMET: A coordinated multi-layer runtime monitoring framework for human-supervised AI-enabled safety systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29816)**  `arXiv:2610.29816`  
  _Niclas Flehmig, Mary Ann Lundteigen, Shen Yin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Incorporating AI systems into safety systems requires monitoring to avoid silent faults and ensure safe and reliable operation. Single monitors often do not provide actionable information for human supervisors, who act as the system's adaptive controller. We present COMET, a coordinated multi-layer runtime monitoring framework for human-supervised AI-enabled safety systems in which each monitor is anchored in a different fault mode. Monitor outputs are combined in a coordination layer and mapped by a communication layer to a traffic-light display. In this work, COMET consists of three monitoring layers: signal integrity, physics consistency, and the AI system's competence envelope. The framework is evaluated on an open dataset of compressed-air leaks, where an AI system detects leaks from acoustic emissions, with held-out conditions simulating covariate shift, semantic shift, and sensor faults, events expected during runtime but unknown in their exact appearance. All monitors are frozen after development and tested zero-shot, and the framework is compared against no-monitoring and single-monitor setups. The bare AI system fails silently and confidently under all three shift types, whereas COMET reduces the trusted-wrong-answer rate to zero on covariate-shifted no-leak data and on three of four sensor faults, at a nominal false-alarm rate of $\approx 1\%$. Single monitors come close in detection but cannot attribute the fault mode. COMET converts their anonymous detection flags into diagnoses. Residual silent misses under semantic shift define the boundary of passive monitoring. Runtime monitoring frameworks for AI-enabled safety systems must therefore combine quantitative fault detection and isolation with qualitative, human-oriented feedback.
  </details>

- **[TraceForge: Distilling with Your Own Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29680)**  `arXiv:2610.29680`  
  _Yan Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using an LLM to generate training data for another is among the most general ways to train one, and it remains common practice. Some frontier teams have closed that route for their own models: what comes back is an answer, or a short summary, without the detailed reasoning trace behind it. That is a little ironic: they were themselves built on distilling human knowledge, and distilling an open-source model is still entirely routine; the restriction applies to their own outputs alone. Recent work recovers the hidden reasoning by replaying encrypted blocks across models. It somehow works, but it breaks the providers' terms of service, so it is not something a product can be built on long-term.We built the alternative. We take the context and the teacher's answer as input, and generate the reasoning trace ourselves, in the style of the student's own model family. Training is two-stage, supervised and then adversarial: a generator learns to regenerate the family's own reasoning traces from their context and answer, and a critic learns how real a reasoning trace looks. Applied to a teacher's answer, the generator writes one in the family's style. An independent judge rates how likely traces are to be genuine on a scale from 0 to 1: ours average 0.68, real traces 0.66, and traces selected by the critic from four candidates 0.82. These are authenticity scores, not correctness rates.We test the idea on five benchmarks with everything else held fixed. On general tasks the five training corpora are about the same; on coding, the generated reasoning beats Fable 5's own thinking content. With the same 5,000 questions and the same teacher answers, HumanEval+ improves by 3.7 points and MBPP+ by 7.1 at the final checkpoint, and the MBPP+ lead holds at every checkpoint. We suspect that reasoning written in the student's family style is easier for it to learn from.
  </details>

- **[From Association to Decision Value: Testing Paired Instruction Dependence for VLA Corpus Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29646)**  `arXiv:2610.29646`  
  _Nurmukhambet Serikbayev, Abdul Basit, Muhammad Shafique_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Choosing among robot demonstration corpora is a ranking problem: an upstream diagnostic must preserve which candidates produce better downstream policies, not merely correlate with average performance. We study paired instruction dependence, the rate at which a demonstrator changes its first decision when only the goal changes. Across 136 independently seeded quadruped-navigation corpora, predictions developed on 56 corpora are evaluated prospectively on 80 held-out corpora. The audit is reliable and associated with downstream paired correctness, and lowers mean absolute prediction error when added to a correctness-aware baseline, but the primary proper-log-score increment is +0.059 nats (95% corpus-bootstrap CI [-0.027, 0.133]) and the prespecified top-20% comparison does not establish an incremental selection gain. An exploratory response decomposition suggests a structural source of this predictive-ranking mismatch: under the tested predictor specification, separating both-correct and both-wrong responses yields larger held-out gains than their unsigned switch statistic. Seed-to-seed agreement indicates that the held-out endpoint retains recoverable rank signal. These results show that counterfactual response structure can be reliable and predictive while providing limited incremental decision value in this setting when compressed into an unsigned statistic, motivating diagnostics that preserve outcome-relevant structure and are validated against the decisions they are intended to support.
  </details>

- **[Parallel Thinking for Large Language Model with Path Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29501)**  `arXiv:2610.29501`  
  _Jiaru Qian, Yihong Dong, Xue Jiang, Chun-Wah Hsu, Zhi Jin, Ge Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel thinking has emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by exploring multiple reasoning paths simultaneously. However, we identify that parallel thinking via Reinforcement Learning (RL) typically suffers from the mode collapse of reasoning paths, where parallel thinking trajectories degenerate into similar ones. This homogeneity undermines the fundamental motivation of parallel thinking, that is, maintaining multiple hypotheses concurrently to prevent premature loss into a suboptimal solution. In this paper, we propose Diverse Parallel Thinking (DPT), a simple yet effective RL approach for parallel thinking designed to encourage path diversity. DPT rewards the model to develop distinct reasoning strategies by penalizing the prefix similarity of parallel thinking paths, thereby enhancing a more comprehensive exploration of the reasoning space. We conduct various experiments and analyses across multiple reasoning benchmarks to demonstrate the effectiveness of DPT. Results show that DPT successfully outperforms previous parallel thinking methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance over baselines. We validate path diversity as a key element to improve parallel thinking. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RubricOpt: Self-Revision of Semantic Rubrics for Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29491)**  `arXiv:2610.29491`  
  _Wei Chen, Yubing Wu, Shigui Li, Delu Zeng, Jingfeng Liu, Junmei Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A model's failures can reveal gaps in the behavioral criteria guiding its training. We introduce RubricOpt, a framework that lets a policy revise these criteria within preference optimization. The policy diagnoses its own rubric-free outputs, retains requirements already covered by the rubric, and edits clauses that leave recurring failures unaddressed. A shared semantic representation connects task demands, preference-data contrasts, and rubric clauses, while a deterministic gate checks evidence references and fixed semantic anchors. The rubric then guides Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) during training, with preference pairs, labels, and the objective held fixed, and is removed at evaluation. Our analysis relates the local effect on rubric-free success to semantic alignment and a residual beyond the measured factors. Experiments on capability and open-ended generation benchmarks show that rubric conditioning can improve outcomes over ordinary DPO, with gains and regressions varying across models and datasets. A matched continuation isolates the contribution of revision from additional training and finds mixed incremental effects for the tested edit, whose feedback overlaps the evaluation set. RubricOpt thus brings the behavioral specification itself into the policy's feedback loop.
  </details>

- **[PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29467)**  `arXiv:2610.29467`  
  _Jian Zhong, Zeyu Liu, Pingchuan Cao, Rongduo Han, Shunye Tang, Guohuan Xie, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon dialogue agents need to keep user memories stable enough for personalization while adapting when the user genuinely changes. Existing memory systems often append every observation or overwrite old entries by heuristic rules, which makes transient noise hard to distinguish from real belief drift. We introduce PreCoMem, a memory consolidation framework inspired by predictive coding that represents memory as confidence-weighted beliefs about the user's state. Its core signal, Effective Surprisal, combines semantic deviation and contradiction evidence, then downweights this signal when retrieval is diffuse and unreliable. According to effective surprisal, a two-threshold gate maps each turn to one of three deterministic updates: MAINTAIN reinforces supported beliefs, PROFILE stores ambiguous cues as low-confidence hypotheses, and CORRECT softly decays reliably contradicted facts. The PROFILE stage acts as an explicit waiting room, preventing premature commitment to noisy evidence. Experiments on LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, and PersonaMem-v2 show consistent state-of-the-art accuracy; our new DiMoBench confirms that PreCoMem adapts to non-stationary belief drift without over-reacting to noise. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Events, Not Pixels: Where World Models Get the Return Wrong](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29435)**  `arXiv:2610.29435`  
  _Haoyue Liu, Xiaoying Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models are increasingly used for planning, where a planner ranks candidate actions by returns imagined by the model. However, these models are judged mainly by how well they render frames, even though a visually faithful rollout can still assign the wrong return. To identify where this mismatch arises, we introduce a transition-level diagnostic that replaces imagined transitions with real ones and measures the reduction in return error against a budget-matched random control. Our analysis yields two findings. (1) Event transitions, the few steps where a reward is earned or an episode ends, are rendered as well as other steps (median pixel-error ratio 0.85), yet restoring them removes a median of 70% of the return error. In contrast, restoring transitions with the largest MSE, SSIM or LPIPS error removes less than a random choice. Across the 44 Atari-100k model--collection pairs that pass our reliability checks, including three released families, event patches outperform random patches in 39 (sign test p=1.4 x 10^{-7}), with a median gain of +44 percentage points. (2) The remaining error is structured: a complementary patch separates errors at true events from those elsewhere, while an eight-step timing tolerance recovers few events missed by our deterministic models. This structure also guides repair: a rule recorded before four repair outcomes were known predicts when a diffusion model repairs these failures. Event patches improve plan ranking, and the same concentration appears on control tasks with rare reward events.
  </details>

- **[Solving Go under Positional Superko: Sound Proof Transfer and New Board Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29414)**  `arXiv:2610.29414`  
  _Sami Kharma, Florian Schintke_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Positional superko (PSK) forbids recreating any earlier whole-board position. It is part of the Chinese rules as written and of the rules used in computer Go. Yet almost all solved Go boards were computed under a simpler basic-ko rule set. PSK makes solving hard: legality depends on the whole game history, and this breaks the three tools that make solving tractable, namely transposition tables (the graph-history interaction problem), static life-and-death recognition, and safe move pruning. We present Sanko, a parallel depth-first proof-number solver that restores all three soundly. Its core is a cheap proof-transfer theorem: a stored proof stays valid on a new path unless a board that the proof's winner must create has already occurred. Sanko proves 5x5 Go in 16 minutes with six threads, where the previous state of the art needed 5.25 hours with six processes, and in about a minute and a half on one 192-thread machine. It also establishes the first PSK values of twenty-three further boards, eleven of which were unsolved under any rules. Superko changes game-theoretic values: 4x7, a full-board win under basic ko, is worth only 12 points under PSK, and we pinpoint the move at which the previously published optimal line fails. Unlike earlier solutions of empty Go boards, we release the solver source together with the command line and outcome of every run behind our values and measurements, so that all reported values can be reproduced.
  </details>

- **[From Prompts to Posteriors: Can LLM Agents Perform Bayesian Inference in Context?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29331)**  `arXiv:2610.29331`  
  _Linus Filbry, Luckeciano C. Melo, Yarin Gal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied across the scientific discovery pipeline, from synthesizing literature to generating hypotheses to informing experimental design. A unifying principle behind these tasks is the need to continuously update beliefs as evidence accumulates, for which Bayesian inference provides a natural framework. However, existing work assumes that LLMs cannot carry out principled Bayesian belief updating in context, instead relying on simplifications and heuristics. To challenge this assumption, we present a principled evaluation of Bayesian reasoning capabilities in LLMs, analyzing both the model's internal consistency as well as its ability to recover an externally determined posterior. Specifically, we build an evaluation framework spanning qualitative epistemological examples to probe probabilistic coherence, quantitative benchmarks of Bayesian inference capabilities, and analyses of the impact of non-Bayesian updates on downstream task performance. Across different scenarios, we find that LLMs generally violate Bayes' rule when updating their beliefs in context. However, when equipped with modern reasoning abilities and code execution capabilities, their performance drastically improves, enabling even the close approximation of analytically intractable posteriors. Our evaluation shows that current LLMs with access to appropriate tools could already be used to make complex belief updates in scientific discovery, enabling rapid acceleration of the efficiency and scale of AI-powered scientific discovery.
  </details>

- **[AgentMonopoly: Behavioral Patterns and Persona Control in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29330)**  `arXiv:2610.29330`  
  _Changshun Wu, Mengyu Li, Mathis Marsault, Xiaowei Huang, Chih-Hong Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How do LLM agents' strategies differ, and how much can instructions to adopt a persona change their behavior over a game? We study these questions with AgentMonopoly, an environment where agents communicate, buy and trade properties, and make decisions that affect later turns. We compare models from four families, each at two sizes, in games with identical models, different sizes of the same family, or different families. We also assign one agent a persona, a description of how it should behave, intended to change a tendency observed in games without that persona. The tested models differ in how often they trade, waive rent, and manage cash and properties. Larger models do not consistently win more games. Persona instructions change the words agents use and some of their actions, but the changes do not always match the role across all decisions. These results show why persona following should be assessed through actions and outcomes as well as language. As our findings are conditional on the tested checkpoints, prompts, and five seeds per configuration, the open-source nature of AgentMonopoly provides the experimental infrastructure for further extending these comparisons. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SpatialBrain: Spatial Intelligence Growing from Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29317)**  `arXiv:2610.29317`  
  _Yifan Zhou, Ziyang Gong, Xiaojun Ye, Yifei Liu, Donggang Li, Xiaolong Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning requires more than access to geometric tools: a reasoner must decide when direct observation is sufficient and when to reconstruct, measure, or change reference frames. We present SpatialBrain, a framework that retains experience about these decisions while keeping its vision-language backbone fixed. The key distinction is between evidence about a scene and experience about how to reason with that evidence. 3DFusionMemory fuses current-scene observations into queryable spatial relations. A Spatial Skill Library provides reusable tool procedures, and Spatial Cognition associates task conditions with experience about when and how to use memory and skills. Completed trajectories are abstracted into process records and consolidated between task blocks, allowing later decisions to draw on earlier execution without transferring scene coordinates or reference answers. Across five spatial-reasoning benchmarks, SpatialBrain improves on direct answers from the same backbone on four and ties on the fifth. Component comparisons, experience-coverage curves, and traced resource decisions characterize how the framework accumulates and uses these resources.
  </details>

- **[ARCANA: A Benchmark for Abstraction and Analogical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29271)**  `arXiv:2610.29271`  
  _Ruqi Chen, Zilei Wang, Wei Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analogical reasoning is widely considered a fundamental component of human intelligence. Few benchmarks are designed to explicitly test far analogical reasoning - reasoning between domains that are superficially dissimilar yet share deep relational or structural similarities. In this work, we introduce an analogical reasoning benchmark called Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus of ANAlogy (ARCANA). The tasks of ARCANA require performing far analogical mappings from real-world objects or physical phenomena to abstract grid-based transformations, different from the well-known ARC-AGI benchmark which primarily emphasized innate human priors. We validate ARCANA through a human study, demonstrating that humans solve these tasks reliably. In contrast, evaluations across a range of large language models show a distinct performance gap compared to humans, revealing systematic failure modes that remain challenging for current models. To address this gap, we propose ConceptSIR, a reasoning framework based on concept slippage, introspection, and reflection. Experiments demonstrate that ConceptSIR enables standard LLMs to solve several challenging ARCANA tasks that were unsolved by direct prompting in our evaluation. Overall, our results suggest that far analogical reasoning remains an open problem for contemporary LLMs. All the materials are available atthis http URL. The complete codebase, including benchmark, and ConceptSIR, is publicly available atthis https URL. All ARCANA data and task instances can be accessed and explored atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CrashGym: Measuring the Decision Value of Experimentation for LLM Agents in Counterfactual Market Crashes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29245)**  `arXiv:2610.29245`  
  _Haocheng Lu, Haoxuan Cheng, Mingyang Chen, Minjun Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model agents are increasingly asked to run experiments, yet benchmarks rarely measure whether those experiments improve the decisions they are meant to inform. We introduce CrashGym, a deterministic, replayable limit-order-book simulator in which an agent must choose between a deleveraging intervention and a liquidity backstop for a market about to suffer a shock-triggered crash. Before committing, the agent may query intervention experiments in a laboratory realization of the market. Its choice is then scored on an independently seeded target realization in which the simulator executes every feasible intervention, so regret relative to the best intervention is computed by directly simulating every alternative on the same target repeats, with no human or model-based judging. The simulator implements each amplification channel---persistent price impact, liquidity withdrawal, margin-driven liquidation, and an evolving strategy mix---as an explicit mechanism that can be switched on in stages. In a prospectively specified study on 24 newly sampled worlds, laboratory access reduces the mean regret of the one deployment with a complete record (Luna) from 8.878 to 3.785, a paired reduction of 5.092 utility units (nominal 95% world-bootstrap interval: 1.028--9.507), or 57.4% of the OBSERVE-ONLY mean. Prospective comparisons for two further deployments are incomplete; in a separate exploratory study, mean regret is lower with laboratory access for all four deployments evaluated. A laboratory whose strategy mix evolves endogenously yields no established advantage over one with a fixed mix. CrashGym thus measures the value of experimentation as avoided decision loss and lets simulator detail be judged by the decisions it supports.
  </details>

- **[From Scores to Safe Sets: Exact Safety--Constrained Decoding for Medication Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29228)**  `arXiv:2610.29228`  
  _Animesh Agarwal, Meysam Ghaffari, Nina Fatehi, Anushka Swarup, Carlos Morato_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent generative medication recommendation systems ultimately produce sets of drugs, but drug--drug interaction (DDI) constraints depend on the set as a whole. A natural post-hoc approach is to first predict medications and then locally repair interacting pairs by removing one drug at a time. We ask whether this local repair step itself introduces avoidable error. Using fixed medication scores from FLAME's frozen classifier on MIMIC-III, we compare increasingly strong local repair strategies with an exact global decoder while holding the scores, output size, and DDI constraint fixed. The global decoder jointly selects the highest-scoring feasible medication set using mixed-integer linear programming. Across validation and test splits, global decoding consistently achieves higher Jaccard similarity than greedy repair, degree-aware repair, and degree-aware repair strengthened with exhaustive 1-swap local search. Its advantage is small under loose DDI constraints and becomes larger as the constraint tightens; on the test split it reaches +0.0064 Jaccard over the strongest local baseline at the strict DDI operating point. The finding also holds across a fine accuracy--DDI sweep at matched realized DDI exposure and under three score representations. At the strict setting, global decoding retains more true-positive medications while removing more false positives. These results show that medication recommendation is not only a representation-learning problem: even with a frozen clinical model, how its scores are converted into a constrained medication set can materially affect the final prediction.
  </details>

- **[When Multiple Choices Are Correct: Asymmetric Probability Allocation in Jev and Other Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29217)**  `arXiv:2610.29217`  
  _Rushab Munot_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models return normalized probability distributions over candidate choices, which downstream applications can use to select among choices. Although their providers emphasize the reliability of these probabilities, calibration on tasks with a single correct choice does not establish their reliability when multiple choices are correct. We evaluate three decision models (Jev, Liquid d1, and GPT-6 Luna Decisions) on three diverse datasets (MetaTool, HotpotQA, and ECtHR) to study probability allocation and thresholding. Even when models rank every correct choice above every distractor, in 28% to 50% of those lists, at least one correct choice receives a probability below the uniform probability of 1/n, where n is the number of choices. We also present each model with five identical copies of a correct choice and observe probability disparities, with mean gaps ranging from 17 to 79 percentage points. We further show how these allocation disparities affect the recovery of multiple correct choices under probability thresholding. Decision-model probabilities can rank correct choices, but a probability threshold does not identify the set of correct choices.
  </details>

- **[Steering Black-Box Large Language Model Agents with Pitfall Anticipation World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29139)**  `arXiv:2610.29139`  
  _Jiongxiao Wang, Han Ding, Youzhi Luo, Yawei Wang, Yun Zhou, Linbo Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier black-box Large Language Model (LLM) agents are proficient at long-horizon reasoning and precise action generation. However, when it comes to a specific agentic task, they have no internalized knowledge of how that particular environment unfolds. A world model offers a natural remedy, providing the agent with environment specific knowledge by foreseeing how the environment will respond to the action it is about to take. Prior world models for LLM agents mainly predict next observations at each step, which is costly and often uninformative because individual observations provide limited task-relevant guidance and not every step requires call for foresight. This raises a key question: what a world model should predict and when should it be invoked for agentic tasks? Our pilot study suggests that world model guidance is most useful when it anticipates pitfalls within a bounded multi-step future. Motivated by this, we introduce Pitfall Anticipation World Model (PAWM), which selectively intervenes when a potential pitfall arises and predicts the impending failure to steer the agent away from it. PAWM is trained in two stages: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on synthetic data labeled with agent pitfalls, followed by GRPO co-training with a black-box LLM to further optimize it in the target environment. PAWM improves Claude Sonnet 4.5 performance by 24.3% on ScienceWorld and 7.7% on WebArena-Lite, outperforming a range of baselines. These results demonstrate that PAWM provides an effective form of world model guidance for black-box LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[LaVGuard: Selective Decoding for Contextual Value Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29135)**  `arXiv:2610.29135`  
  _Jiale Dai, Liuxian Ma, Hongcan Deng, Xiaoke Niu, Wenjing Zhang, Zhaoxiang Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A content-safe response can still promote priorities that conflict with its task: an assistant may demand compliance when asked to preserve autonomy. We study contextual value drift as a task-relative decoding problem with an explicit allowance for change. LaVGuard separates an authorized ten-dimensional profile from a frozen estimate of the values expressed in the visible prefix. A profile-relative density gate selects when to evaluate candidate continuations; their signed progress determines a logit tilt constrained by both changed-position and cumulative-KL budgets. We establish local surrogate improvement, pathwise budget feasibility, and a bound on response-distribution change. Profile conditioning raises boundary TPR at 5% FPR from .58 to .72. At the same .12 mean intervention ratio, the selection study reports risk-safety scores of .88 for LaVGuard and .72 for position-matched edits. The resulting interface makes task priorities actionable while keeping the extent of intervention explicit and bounded.
  </details>

- **[NormTune: Norm-Guided Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Reasoning-Intensive Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29134)**  `arXiv:2610.29134`  
  _Zhangming Li, Qinghao Hu, Weifan Guan, Yifan Zhang, Peisong Wang, Jian Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full fine-tuning has achieved remarkable success on reasoning-intensive tasks, but it often requires substantial GPU resource consumption. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) provides a promising alternative for reducing this cost, yet we observe a substantial performance gap on reasoning-intensive tasks. In this paper, we present three key observations showing that this gap is closely associated with norm-related under-adaptation, where PEFT produces markedly smaller weight-update norms than full fine-tuning. Based on these observations, we propose Norm-Guided Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (NormTune), a simple yet effective strategy that adaptively strengthens insufficiently updated layers according to their weight-update norms. Extensive experiments show that NormTune consistently enhances multiple PEFT methods across challenging reasoning benchmarks. Specifically, when trained on S1K-1.1, integrating NormTune into rank-8 LoRA, Bone, and MoRA yields substantial average accuracy improvements of 24.24\%, 11.33\%, and 16.21\% , respectively, across AIME 2024, MATH 500, GPQA, and AIME 2025. Moreover, NormTune-enhanced rank-256 LoRA, Bone, and MoRA achieve performance comparable to S1 full fine-tuning, with respective average gains of 2.17\%, 0.93\%, and 3.00\% over this baseline.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Self-Improvement on a Budget: Scoring Code Edits Before They Run](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29121)**  `arXiv:2610.29121`  
  _Erfan Darzi, Sree Bhargavi Balija, Sheng He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A coding agent that rewrites its own scaffold has to choose which proposed edit to expand before that edit has been evaluated, and the search then has to return one agent. Each run fits one linear readout on the parent's steering-split solve rate and eight coordinates of the proposed diff, from 96 scaffold edits labeled by four evaluations of a 16-instance fit split, and then freezes the coefficients. HumanEval+ reserves 48 instances as the steering split and Polyglot reserves 64. That split scores parents and keeps an archive of four nodes. Huxley-Gödel Machine selection draws a parent by Thompson sampling on the steering counts of nodes that have already been evaluated, and it expands the child that parent proposes. Every method then returns the earliest evaluated node whose steering-split solve count is maximal. The tables report the held-out score of that node on the other 100 HumanEval+ instances and the other 145 Polyglot instances, next to the root scaffold and the calibration edit with the highest fit-split label. Twelve runs of GPT-4o return a node at 87.0% on held-out HumanEval+, against 84.2% for the parent-rate ablation. On that split Huxley-Gödel Machine selection returns 85.1%, the root is at 81.2%, and the calibration-best edit is at 82.8%. On the same split Claude Sonnet 4.5 returns 86.6%, beside 86.5% for Huxley-Gödel Machine selection, 85.3% for the parent-rate ablation, and 85.1% for the calibration-best edit.
  </details>

- **[Context-Augmented, Session-Calibrated Blood Pressure Tracking from Photoplethysmography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29091)**  `arXiv:2610.29091`  
  _Farouk Ganiyu Adewumi, Timothy Oladunni_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  this http URLdistinguish the contributions of acquisition context and photoplethysmography (PPG) to session-calibrated blood pressure (BP)this http URL. The original comparison used three repeats of five-fold participant-level cross-validation in 653 AuroraBP participants, excluding calibration-labelled prediction targets. Matched analyses separated context and PPG and removed elapsed-time terms. Later exploratory follow-ups retained PPG, accelerometer signals and three initial cuff readings. Separate studies evaluated pulse-rate fusion and an intraoperative VitalDB model. Mainthis http URLoriginal wearable-state model achieved participant-level mean absolute errors (MAEs) of 7.697/6.139 mmHg for systolic/diastolic BP (SBP/DBP). Added context reduced MAE by 0.436/0.126 mmHg (97.5% intervals 0.305-0.573/0.053-0.200). In the matched factorial without the external PPG prior, PPG improved on calibration, time and protocol-provided posture/activity by 0.214/0.135 mmHg; removing time left the DBP interval including zero. Standard PPG descriptors contributed, but additional nonlinear/attractor benefit was not established. In a separate follow-up series, a sensor-history ensemble achieved 7.299/5.445 mmHg versus its static-rich reference's 7.363/5.470, small gains of 0.064/0.025 mmHg. Larger DBP errors did not improve uniformly. Quality-weighted fusion reduced pulse-rate MAE from 3.985 to 3.690 beats per minute. VitalDB MAEs remained 15.375/8.833 mmHg despite small PPG gains. Significance. The contribution is a calibrated evaluation framework with explicit controls, reproducible feature-cache-to-results analyses and retained negative findings. The sensor-history gain is exploratory after repeated dataset reuse. Independently estimated wearable states, complete raw-signal reproduction of the supplied richer features, external transfer and clinical adequacy remain unestablished.
  </details>

- **[Does Every Instance Count Equally? Order Effects in LLM Multi-Instance Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29057)**  `arXiv:2610.29057`  
  _Jingxuan Chen, Mohammad Taher Pilehvar, Jose Camacho-Collados_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Users increasingly rely on large language models (LLMs) to analyse collections of independent inputs, such as product reviews or social media posts, and return a single aggregated answer. This setting requires multi-instance processing (MIP), where a model combines information from multiple instances into one prediction. We study a task in which the model must infer a binary label for each instance, and identify the most frequent label among the full instance set. The correct answer should be invariant to instance order. By evaluating fifteen LLMs across five datasets and seven instance order patterns, with each instance set held fixed across patterns, we find substantial order sensitivity. Accuracy can differ by about 90 percentage points between the best and worst order patterns, with models generally more accurate when majority-label instances appear at the start than when they appear in the middle or at the end. For subsets of models, available reasoning traces and gradient-based attribution reveal uneven emphasis across positions. Traces disproportionately mention early instances, while attribution scores are generally higher near the beginning and end than in the middle. These results show that instance order can systematically bias aggregated predictions, highlighting the need to evaluate MIP across different input arrangements.
  </details>

- **[AutoFraming: Synthesizing Dynamic Frames of C Code with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29046)**  `arXiv:2610.29046`  
  _Han Zhou, Yu Luo, Dianxiang Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown considerable promise in synthesizing formal specifications from source code. However, existing approaches primarily focus on functional contracts and loop invariants, while largely neglecting frame conditions, which specify the memory locations that may or may not be modified during execution and constitute an essential part of formal specifications. To address this limitation, we propose an LLM-based approach for synthesizing ACSL frame specifications alongside functional contracts from C code. Our approach analyzes code property graphs (CPGs) to extract frame-relevant information from the input code and leverages this information to guide the LLM in generating accurate frame conditions. The resulting specifications capture both region-separation constraints and data dependencies that govern variable modifications. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. On a benchmark of 84 ACSL-compliant functions from non-trivial real-world systems, our method successfully synthesizes error-free frame specifications for 92.9% of the functions, representing a 179% relative improvement over state-of-the-art techniques. On a second benchmark consisting of 51 functions, the success rate increases from 80.4% to 96.1%. Furthermore, when integrated as an auxiliary component into existing LLM-based ACSL-synthesis frameworks, our approach improves frame-specification generation performance by up to 61.9%.
  </details>

- **[GRAIL: From Saliency to Semantics in Time-Series Explanation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29036)**  `arXiv:2610.29036`  
  _Enver Menadjiev, Yiqiu Shen, Jaesik Choi, Julia Stoyanovich_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Explaining a time-series model requires both locating the evidence and extracting meaningful knowledge from it. Yet a single timestep is not a semantic unit: the meaningful patterns live across many samples, not in any one of them. Per-timestep saliency is therefore noisy, high-dimensional, and unstable. Existing methods address this by introducing fixed-size windows and treating them as independent players in a cooperative game. Evaluating a coalition of such players requires defining a ``baseline'' region, which prior work tackles with fixed counterfactuals (e.g., a flat line, noise, or a spectral surrogate). This choice frequently dominates the resulting explanation, obscuring the model's true decisions.To address these issues, we propose GRAIL, a region-based time-series explanation framework that uses domain-specific intervals, such as the P-wave and the QRS complex of an electrocardiogram (ECG) beat, as players. We describe each region with human-readable features and generate counterfactual baselines with an on-manifold conditional generator. Such a choice of players also admits non-temporal features: static covariates enter as singleton players attributed with the temporal regions on one scale, so a patient's tabular evidence (e.g., age, sex) is weighed directly against the signal. We attribute the predictions to the features via Owen values with common-random-number variance reduction. Experimental results on both synthetic and real data show that the proposed framework produces faithful and robust explanations, evaluated under matched on- and off-manifold operators, offering meaningful knowledge to domain experts.
  </details>

- **[Inheritance as an Admission Test for LLM Prompt Merge Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29023)**  `arXiv:2610.29023`  
  _Zan-Kai Chong, Hiroyuki Ohsaki, Bryan Ng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are often improved by iterating until a fixed verifier is done. Once that verifier saturates, the loop has nothing left to climb. We instead score agents against the current population, and a frozen panel records whether anyone got better. The panel mean can rise inside the seeds by elimination alone. We therefore count a run as training only when its final mean beats the best generation-zero member. A merge is admitted only when the child moves toward the donor, past the child the same operator produces at zero dose, and the lower confidence bound on that movement reaches half the requested dose. That is the admission test. A detectable shortfall rejects the operator, and the test is otherwise undetermined. We keep realised fitness \phi as the selector and reference performance \theta as the unused ruler. Every measurement uses gemini-3.1-flash-lite on one negotiation task, and no fixed-oracle arm was run. In the open loop, tactic append grafts short donor rules and is admitted. Free rewrite moves only a third of the requested dose and is rejected. Template append pastes raw donor replies into the child. Its movement clears the bound on the first probe but not on the re-probe, and it is left undetermined. A held-out re-probe on the five situations the merge never saw confirms that both appends move, and it does not rescue free rewrite. Neither append rises with the dose. Constrained rewrite is admitted in the open loop and was not run in the closed loop, which changes only the operator. The population, the scores and the task stay fixed. No free-rewrite run beats the best generation-zero member. Every tactic-append run and every template-append run does. Sampled loop children keep that split. Free rewrite moves no further toward the donor, and both appends still clear the bound, by less than in the open loop.
  </details>

- **[An Evidence-Driven Multi-Agent Compound AI System for Head and Neck Proton Radiotherapy Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29010)**  `arXiv:2610.29010`  
  _Qingqing Wang, Liqiang Xiao, Chang Chang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radiation treatment planning requires balancing competing objectives to satisfy clinical criteria while accommodating physicians' preferences. This study aims to develop an evidence-driven, multi-agent compound AI system that integrates DRL-based automated treatment planning with LLM-based conversational plan refinement for proton PBS therapy. A policy network trained using PPO first generates multiple plans without human intervention, from which one is selected as the target plan and the others serve as references for subsequent refinements. For challenging cases requiring further adjustments, an LLM-based multi-agent system enables plan refinement through natural language interactions. The system comprises a planning agent that recommends evidence-supported adjustments, a chatbot providing a unified conversational interface, and an evaluation agent that retrieves clinical metrics and identifies constraint violations. To enhance domain-specific reasoning, the planning agent based on Qwen3-14B is designed to justify its recommendations using evidence from previously generated plans and post-trained on task-specific data using SFT and GRPO. The framework was trained and evaluated using 21 patients with H&N cancers, with 5 PPO-generated plans per patient. Of these, 50 plans were selected for instruction-guided refinement. Compared with the base LLM, the post-trained Qwen3-14B demonstrated improved instruction-following, information retrieval and domain-specific reasoning capabilities. The resulting adjustments more effectively improved user-prioritized objectives while limiting deterioration in competing objectives, yielding more clinically appropriate trade-offs in patient-specific plan refinement. The proposed compound AI system provides a user-friendly planning platform, offering a promising approach to personalized proton PBS treatment planning.
  </details>

- **[Self-Improving Agents with World Model Predictive Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28945)**  `arXiv:2610.28945`  
  _Haori Lu, Tianyu Gao, Yaoyao Liu, Yingying Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly expected to interact with environments and improve their decisions during deployment. However, how anticipating future outcomes and drawing on accumulated interaction experience can jointly support decision-making remains underexplored. We propose a test-time framework, inspired by Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control, that combines multi-branch predictive analysis with experience-based adaptation. At each step, the agent proposes multiple candidate actions and imagines their outcomes with a world model, so that alternatives can be compared without being executed in the environment. Since language environments provide neither explicit dynamics nor costs, the predicted outcomes are evaluated for task progress and risk, and MPPI-style weights over the resulting costs guide the final decision. A dual-branch experience memory enables self-improvement: the planner branch refines candidate generation and action selection, while the world model branch improves next-observation prediction. Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld across three backbones demonstrate the strong overall performance of our method and the effectiveness of experience-guided predictive control. Our code will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[D-Skill: Distilling Teacher Knowledge through Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28914)**  `arXiv:2610.28914`  
  _Shuo Yuan, Dacheng Li, Joseph E. Gonzalez_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier language-model agents excel at complex tasks, but high inference costs make transferring their capabilities to smaller models essential. Traditional knowledge distillation achieves this through parameter updates, requiring access to the student's weights and additional training compute while encoding the transferred knowledge implicitly in the parameters. We introduce D-Skill, a framework that treats the teacher as a black box and distills its behavioral advantage into an interpretable natural-language skill by analyzing paired rollouts and identifying behavioral divergences and agreements. The resulting lessons are consolidated offline, with any compatible skill-update backend, into a skill document that is provided to the student at inference time. D-Skill constructs the skill entirely offline from fixed trajectories, without labels, further environment interaction, or weight updates after rollout collection. Across SpreadsheetBench-Verified, ALFWorld, and chess puzzles spanning multiple teacher-student pairs, D-Skill closes 75%-95% of the teacher-student gap without touching model weights. After a single offline pass over the paired traces, D-Skill outperforms one-epoch SFT baselines and remains competitive with multi-epoch SFT. Under the same model family, the resulting student also incurs 5.7x-8.3x lower inference cost per task than the teacher. Our results demonstrate that natural-language skill distillation offers a performant, interpretable, and efficient alternative to weight-based capability transfer.
  </details>

- **[DiffResearch: Accelerating Deep Research with Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28909)**  `arXiv:2610.28909`  
  _Alan-Barsag Gazzaev, Alexey Gavrilov, Mikhail Mozikov, Sergey Muravyov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research systems iteratively plan, gather evidence, synthesize, and revise, making end-to-end latency a key bottleneck for interactive use. Almost all production systems are built on autoregressive (AR) language models and inherit left-to-right decoding in planning and long-form writing. We present DiffResearch, an open multi-agent deep research framework that places a diffusion language model (dLLM) at the core of every stage, and measure how a diffusion backbone changes latency and report quality. Holding the agentic scaffold, retrieval code, prompts, and evaluation fixed, we swap the backbone between an open diffusion model and a comparable AR model. Across 100 Deep Research Bench tasks, the diffusion backbone is roughly 6 times faster end-to-end, driven by order-of-magnitude reductions in planning and writing. We further introduce dFast, a decomposition mode that reads intermediate denoising states and extracts subqueries before generation finishes, providing a 1.99 times median speedup for decomposition in isolation with no loss of report quality. On the benchmark's RACE rubric, dFast obtains the best overall score among the three configurations. Finally, in a public system-level leaderboard snapshot, an instance built on a stronger diffusion model ranks above several commercial systems. We release DiffResearch under an Apache-2.0 license.
  </details>

- **[Hidden Messages Between Diffusion Language Model Agents: Causal Evidence for Latent Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28905)**  `arXiv:2610.28905`  
  _Alan-Barsag Gazzaev, Alexey Gavrilov, Sergey Muravyov, Ilya Makarov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models form answers through a sequence of revisable continuous states, but multi-agent systems usually discard those states and make agents communicate only after decoding text. We ask whether one independent diffusion trajectory can pass a compact hidden message into another and, crucially, whether the receiver uses what that message means. In Hidden Messages, a sender compresses its denoising state into four signed dense vectors; the receiver consumes them while retaining its own context, canvas, and trajectory. Removing a message tests whether the channel matters; replacing it with a message from another example tests whether its content matters. Across controlled tasks, derangement removes a +63.6-point advantage on Tiny-A2D and a +100.0-point advantage on Dream-7B, while targeted substitutions steer the receiver toward the substituted fact. On QASC with the two supporting facts split across agents, matched communication reaches 80.8%, but a wrong-example message lowers accuracy to 46.8%, a content effect of +34.0 points. The surprising result is temporal: one late transfer reaches 87.8%, exceeding four recurrent exchanges by +7.0 points. These experiments provide causal evidence for continuous hidden communication between diffusion-LM agents and show that when information is transferred can matter more than how many times agents exchange it.
  </details>

- **[Speedrunning Relational Foundation Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28855)**  `arXiv:2610.28855`  
  _Gabriel Wedson Mendonça de Souza Veloso, Luan Borges Teodoro Reis Sena, Denis Oliveira Correa, Francisco Galuppo Azevedo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Progress on foundation model pretraining is gated by who can afford a trial, and relational pretraining is no exception. Recipes are developed inside individual labs, reported under private protocols, and do not accumulate. Speedrun leaderboards remove that barrier by decentralizing the search rather than the training run. The problem is fixed, the stack is open, entries are ranked by wall-clock time to a quality target, and one GPU is enough to hold the record. We introduce modded-nanoRT, the first such leaderboard for Relational Transformers. Which database to hold out decides whether the benchmark can resolve improvements at all, so we fix it by calibration, ranking six candidates by how sharply a 45-minute learning curve separates improvement from seed noise. We open the leaderboard with typed trilinear attention, a relational operator that conditions attention scores and values on the foreign key traversed, together with Muon and SIGReg. The recipe reaches the target in 305.63 seconds on one NVIDIA H200, 8.11x faster than the reference implementation, and wins 13 of 15 pairs outside the development database. We will release the benchmark, its frozen artifacts, and the record runs. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sketch2Sim: an end-to-end modular framework for hand-drawn circuit analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28836)**  `arXiv:2610.28836`  
  _Mohammad Kawsar, Al Amin Islam Ridoy, Md Rakib Hossain, Farzana Akter, AKM Bahalul Haque, Rejwan Bin Sulaiman, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study proposes a sketch-to-simulation system to solve handwritten circuit diagrams. The system converts raw images into a Simulation Program with Integrated Circuit Emphasis (SPICE)-compatible netlist and automatically calculates the circuit's nodal voltages and currents. Another objective is to propose a holistic framework that benefits students and educators by enhancing the teaching and learning experience of electric circuits and expediting engineering design reviews. This article presents a multistage pipeline that converts raw photographs of handwritten electric circuits into executable SPICE-like netlists, providing a solution. A dataset of 1,000 hand-drawn Resistor-Inductor-Capacitor (RLC) circuits was curated and annotated for components, text regions, and character recognition. The proposed system decomposes a circuit diagram into three stages: (i) dual You Only Look Once (YOLO)-based detectors for components and text regions, (ii) a Convolutional Recurrent Neural Network (CRNN) with Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) and Optical Character Recognition (OCR)-based domain-aware symbol value extraction, and (iii) image processing with thresholding, skeletonization, and Density-Based Spatial Clustering of Applications with Noise (DBSCAN) to automatically identify nodes and map them with corresponding components. The proposed system produces the netlist and solves the circuit. The qualitative and quantitative ablation studies on modular stages, as well as end-to-end tests, demonstrate robust performance across various handwriting styles, lighting conditions, andthis http URLstudy serves as an interactive learning aid and practical tool for professionals, providing a comprehensive user interface (UI), enabling rapid digitization of circuit sketches for study, design, prototyping, or troubleshooting.
  </details>

- **[Decision language models as affordable and rapid alternatives to LLM judges of clinical generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28826)**  `arXiv:2610.28826`  
  _Rotem Gershon, Seffi Cohen, Dean Ariel, Havatzelet Bilavsky-Yarden, Amiel Sberro, Shai Yitzhaki, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated judges of clinical artificial intelligence (AI) outputs are necessary, but large language model (LLM) judges are costly and slow at scale. We compared Jev, a decision language model that returns structured judgments, with frontier and open-weight LLM judges in four use cases. On 118 pediatric answers, Jev's agreement with physicians' ordinal quality ratings (Gwet's AC2 0.50; expected score 0.53) matched or exceeded that of seven of 12 LLM judges, including frontier models, Claude Opus 5.5 (0.49) and GPT-6 (0.38). On physician-graded binary HealthBench criteria, Jev matched or exceeded four of six LLM judges. On MedHELM's open-ended tasks, Jev's agreement with a three-LLM jury approached or exceeded the jurors' internal agreement. On HealthBench Professional, Jev matched the benchmark's reference judge, GPT-5.4, on 91% of criteria (other judges, 91-93%). Frontier judges cost 73-294 times more than Jev and took 7-57 times longer. Jev may enable efficient clinical AI evaluation at scale.
  </details>

- **[Forging More Tasks into Better Harnesses: Agentic Harness Evolution via Successive Scale Racing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28815)**  `arXiv:2610.28815`  
  _Jingzhe Liu, Jiliang Tang, Tong Zhao, Neil Shah, Clark Mingxuan Ju_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic harness evolution optimizes an agent's instructions, tools, and control flow by using its task execution experiences as evolving evidence. While more evolution tasks should intuitively improve performance, our preliminary study reveals a paradoxical failure mode: the performance of existing methods degrades as the task pool expands. Our further analysis demonstrates that this degradation stems from suboptimal evidence selection during optimization, as different evidence combinations lead to substantially different evolution outcomes. To address this issue, we propose Successive Scale Racing (SSR), a framework that adaptively searches for optimal evidence combinations in each evolution round. SSR constructs diverse evidence combinations based on the corresponding task quality and coverage, and efficiently races the resulting candidate harnesses under a small compute budget. Empirically, across three distinct benchmarks, SSR achieves state-of-the-art results in all six configurations. Notably, as the task pool grows from 40 to 200, SSR steadily improves the pass rate from 64.3% to 68.3%, while the baselines' performances all drop. Furthermore, its racing mechanism identifies near-optimal candidates while reducing validation executions by 60%.
  </details>

- **[SpecEvo: Speculative Evolution with Large Language Models for Cost-Efficient Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28706)**  `arXiv:2610.28706`  
  _Ha Minh Hieu, Tongyao Zhu, Do Xuan Long, Min-Yen Kan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-driven evolutionary search is a promising paradigm for scientific discovery, but existing methods remain costly and slow, often applying expensive reasoning uniformly while underusing evidence from failed, redundant, and saturated trials. We introduce Speculative Evolution (SpecEvo), a multi-LLM framework that coordinates lightweight and frontier models through continual consolidation of search evidence. Lightweight models power both parallel Speculators, which generate and refine candidate programs, and an Advisor, which distills accumulated successes, failures, and saturated directions into persistent guidance for subsequent exploration. At sparse checkpoints, a frontier-model Navigator inspects the evolving population and intervenes through synthesis, surgical refinement, or reframing according to search stagnation. This speculate-then-consolidate workflow delegates routine exploration and evidence consolidation to inexpensive models while reserving frontier reasoning for strategic interventions whose benefits extend across many subsequent low-cost evaluations. Across 176 tasks spanning four scientific and algorithmic discovery domains, SpecEvo matches or outperforms frontier-model baselines while reducing API cost by up to 80%, achieves up to 1.5x greater evaluation efficiency than cost-efficient baselines on equation discovery, and improves over the strongest CO-Bench baseline by 3.8%. These results support trajectory-conditioned model coordination as an effective approach to cost-efficient scientific discovery.
  </details>

- **[Scaling in Games: Continued Pre-Training For Embodied Agents In Diverse Virtual Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28698)**  `arXiv:2610.28698`  
  _Kuan Zhang, Yukun Chen, Zhihao Yang, Yue Su, Xiangnan Wu, Zirong Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling data, diversity and model capacity has improved embodied learning in physical environments, where diverse experiences remain grounded in shared physical laws. Whether these gains extend to virtual worlds with different visual styles, dynamics, controls, and objectives remains unclear. We investigate this question through a controlled study of continued pre-training (CPT) across three axes: data volume, diversity, and model size. Our 120 training configurations span four model sizes from 0.8B to 27B, six data budgets from 50 to 1,600 hours, and five mixtures ranging from Minecraft-only data to diverse multi-world corpora. We assess performance and generalization using three-level validation losses and gameplay benchmarks from in-corpus worlds to unseen worlds. Our results show: (1) more data improves in-corpus performance, diversity improves transfer to related worlds with limited gains in distant worlds, and model size improves data efficiency but generalizes worse to distant worlds under single-world training; (2) we observe an early interface phase with broad cross-world benefits and a later world phase with gains narrowed to in-corpus and related worlds, and fit an empirical scaling law relating validation loss to data volume, diversity and model size; and (3) our models are competitive with Minecraft specialists and frontier models in 3D worlds, and perform best in real-time settings. However, the limits of imitation and persistent gaps in distant worlds suggest that building generalist embodied agents requires richer training stages beyond continued pre-training.
  </details>

- **[A Small Model Asks Better: Fine-Tuning Query Clarification for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28686)**  `arXiv:2610.28686`  
  _Lihong He, Lucian Popa, Marina Danilevsky_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query clarification is essential for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems that face ambiguous user queries. We consider multi-turn grounded conversational scenarios where a user query is either ill-formed or ambiguous in the context of the prior dialog or, more interestingly, it may be well-formed but when posed against retrieved documents there are multiple relevant interpretations. The task is to recognize both such cases and then generate an appropriate clarification request to the user. We present ClariEdge, a new dataset of approximately 3,900 examples that include such ambiguous queries along with negative examples that do not require clarification. We also show that a lightweight fine-tuned LoRA achieves 96.6% classification accuracy and outperforms prompted baselines of many larger models on both classification and quality of clarification generation. Our study shows that hard negatives are an essential ingredient to separate out queries that are truly ambiguous from queries that may look ambiguous but are not (i.e., can be resolved from the context). The same LoRA achieves high performance in both pre-retrieval and post-retrieval scenarios; thus, it can be used in two places in a RAG pipeline as a check before answer generation. The ClariEdge dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Evolving Self-tuning Benchmarks for Long-Horizon Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27307)**  `arXiv:2610.27307`  
  _Hyunji Alex Nam, Michael Dennis, Natasha Jaques, Minsuk Chang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For AI assistants to move beyond simple chat interfaces and reliably assist users with applications like Gmail, Venmo, and Slack, we need to improve their tool-use capabilities. However, existing tool use benchmarks are still limited and difficult to scale because they rely on human supervision or are too domain-specific. To make data generation scalable, we propose, TURTLE (Tool Use Related Tasks with Long-Horizon Evolution), an Unsupervised Environment Design (UED) approach to evolve existing tasks using a proposer, a solver, and a feedback generator large language model agents. The proposer is rewarded for difficult yet solvable tasks based on the solver's empirical performance, and the feedback generator summarizes the solver's execution traces for the proposer to exploit. TURTLE produces evolving, self-tuning test suites adaptable to different environment requirements, from supporting user simulators and dense document retrieval to interacting across multiple apps. Empirical results on AppWorld, $\tau$-Bench, and AutomationBench using Gemma 4 31B-it show that the evolved tasks target the desired difficulty range (e.g., a 165% increase in the percentage of hard tasks on AppWorld), increase task diversity (in terms of reasoning and simultaneous tool interactions), and elicit a broader range of failure modes than the original tasks.
  </details>

- **[Learning What to Remember: Resource-Efficient Self-Improvement for Lifelong Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26453)**  `arXiv:2610.26453`  
  _Guannan Lai, Zi-Yang Bai, Jun-Peng Jiang, Han-Jia Ye_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-improving agents aim to continuously improve their behavior by learning from experience after deployment. However, accumulating and reusing more experience does not necessarily lead to better decisions, particularly when generation, interaction, and memory resources are jointly constrained. Under such stringent constraints, we find that several existing lifelong-memory methods provide limited benefits over simple retrieval or even no-memory agents. This reveals a fundamental challenge for resource-efficient self-improvement: agents must learn not only which past experiences are relevant, but which are actually worth reusing to improve future decisions. When memory capacity and opportunities for reasoning and recovery are limited, retrieving an ineffective experience can consume scarce resources and hinder downstream execution. To address this challenge, we propose TokenWorth, a lightweight feedback-driven memory adaptation framework that learns query-dependent memory worth from sparse execution feedback. Without updating the backbone model, TokenWorth uses the learned worth to guide both experience retrieval and bounded memory management, enabling the agent to continually adapt how it reuses accumulated experience. Across three LifelongAgentBench environments and both local and API-based deployments, TokenWorth achieves the highest task success rate while using the fewest output tokens among the evaluated methods under the specified resource budgets. These results suggest that effective self-improvement under resource constraints depends not on accumulating more experience, but on learning what is worth remembering. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[REFLEX: RNA Ensemble Generation via Flexibility-Calibrated Stochastic Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26261)**  `arXiv:2610.26261`  
  _Wanli Ma, Tianmeng Hu, Ke Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RNA molecules often function through multiple experimentally observed conformational states. A central challenge is to capture ensemble diversity without sacrificing structural fidelity: samples may collapse toward averaged representative structures or drift into unsupported conformational regions. We introduce REFLEX (RNA Ensemble generation calibrated by FLEXibility), a sequence-conditioned generator for RNA backbone-frame ensembles based on a heteroscedastic stochastic bridge on SE(3). During training, experimental B-factors calibrate residue-wise bridge widths, exposing each observed conformer through local perturbation neighborhoods with residue-specific scales while keeping constrained regions sharper. A learned flexibility condition guides the bridge-field network to recover target conformer-specific frames from these calibrated noisy states. At inference, REFLEX uses only the input sequence, requiring no MSA-subsampling heuristics. On a multi-conformer RNA benchmark, REFLEX achieves the best precision-recall trade-off for ensemble coverage across evaluation thresholds, while recovery-stress ablations indicate gains in target-frame recovery under stronger perturbations.
  </details>

- **[When Form Changes but Logic Doesn't: Building Logic-invariant LLMs through Logical Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25402)**  `arXiv:2610.25402`  
  _Xuyuan Liu, Xinshuai Dong, Elynn Chen, Yujun Yan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance across diverse reasoning tasks, yet it remains unclear whether this performance reflects an understanding of underlying logical structure or a reliance on shallow semantic cues. A key indicator of such understanding is whether models treat logically equivalent inputs consistently, producing the same predictions despite differences in expression. Through systematic evaluation on curated data, we show that even strong LLMs often fail this test: they do not consistently preserve predictions across logically equivalent inputs and generalize poorly to unseen logical forms. To address this limitation, we propose LoGIcal STructure-guided reasoning (LoGIST), a framework that guides LLM reasoning with explicit logical structure. LoGIST maps each input instance to a decision diagram, a directed acyclic graph that captures the underlying logical structure, and encodes this graph into a logical embedding that conditions the LLM's reasoning process. Across multiple training settings, LoGIST reduces inconsistency under logical equivalence and improves generalization to unseen logical forms, suggesting that logical invariance is a useful target for building more robust and generalizable logical reasoning in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[OwlSight: A Panoramic Monitor for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23670)**  `arXiv:2610.23670`  
  _Lewen Yan, Yanxu Zhu, Jie Zhang, Jing Shao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring offers actionable evidence for oversight AI systems, with the potential to enable early detection and intervention. However, existing approaches are focused on specific tasks, whereas the regulation of AI, particularly reasoning models, requires monitoring a broader range of tasks. We introduce Factor for CoT monitoring, a strategy that supports customizing monitoring requirements in the prompt, which enables a single monitor to cover different tasks by relying on different factors. Furthermore, we introduce two types of factors, fine-grained and coarse-grained (e.g., monitoring whether a specific email address has been leaked versus monitoring privacy leakage), to accommodate diverse user monitoring needs. Based on this, we develop a CoT monitor named OwlSight that relies solely on factor to monitor behaviors related to multiple dimensions, including capability, safety, and trustworthiness. Experiments across 15,971 reasoning trajectories show that OwlSight surpasses general-purpose LLMs by 1.32-30.57 and 11.71-27.81 points at fine and coarse granularity, respectively, and achieves state-of-the-art results on OpenAI's CoT monitorability benchmark.
