The network is thereby capable of learning semantic features robust against variations in anatomical structure, imaging contrast, resolution and spatial context, which provide universal representation capabilities that serve as a foundation for segmentation learning. Subsequently, in the second stage, we continue to train an Organ-specific Encoder-decoder Segmentation Network (OESNet), whose encoder is initialized with the pretrained weights. This training process is facilitated by another dedicated data engine, which generates diversified visual images while strictly retaining the inherent spatial layout of abdominal organs. Comprehensive evaluation on seven abdominal organ segmentation datasets across three different modalities including CT, MRI, and PET demonstrates that ReSeg consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods while eliminating the need for real training images.
  </details>

- **[OccJEPA: Occlusion as Masking for Amodal Video Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29061)**  `arXiv:2610.29061`  
  _Genpei Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Amodal video segmentation asks for the complete mask of an object in every frame, including the parts hidden behind other objects. The best current methods generate these masks with fine-tuned video diffusion models, which need many denoising steps and an extra depth network, and give a different answer for every sample. Self-supervised video world models of the JEPA family are trained on a closely related task: predicting the representation of masked space-time regions from the visible ones. We therefore treat occlusion as masking. The tokens that the object's visible mask does not cover are dropped, the pre-trained V-JEPA 2.1 predictor fills them in, and a light head decodes the observed and predicted features into amodal masks. The resulting model, OccJEPA (Occlusion-as-masking JEPA), is deterministic and 33.5x faster than the diffusion-based state of the art, and more accurate on SAIL-VOS, on TAO-Amodal, including a held-out test split (+4.00 AP50) and visible masks tracked by SAM2, and on Kubric. Controlled experiments trace the gain to the predictor's pre-trained weights rather than to its capacity, for world models from 87M to 1.85B parameters. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Weakly Supervised Fish Segmentation through Point-Supervised Tracking and Motion-Informed Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29047)**  `arXiv:2610.29047`  
  _Vedanth Kallakuri, Katherine A. Skinner_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding the properties of fish populations is increasingly important. Analyzing fish length, width, and mass is integral to maintaining healthy stock in the aquaculture industry, while counting wild fish populations supports marine conservation and coastal reef restoration efforts. Deep learning-based segmentation models offer a promising means of obtaining such information at scale. Most of these models, however, require costly and time-consuming per-pixel segmentation labels for training, which can take up to two minutes per fish to annotate manually. To address this inefficiency, we propose a weakly supervised segmentation pipeline that requires only point-labeled data (one click per fish) to produce accurate segmentation masks. Our pipeline consists of four stages. First, a point-supervised tracker predicts point annotations for each frame. These points are then converted into motion-informed language expressions, which guide a language-conditioned segmentation model (LMPM++) to capture temporal consistency and produce candidate segmentation masks. Finally, optical flow and background subtraction are applied as post-processing filters to suppress noise from incorrect segmentations. Experiments on the DeepFish dataset show improvement in foreground mean intersection over union (mIoU) as compared to baseline models.
  </details>

- **[Single-Image Food Mass Estimators Do Not Transfer Their Gram Scale Across Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29025)**  `arXiv:2610.29025`  
  _Yongsheng Lian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating food mass from a photograph is central to image-based dietary assessment, but whether trained models transfer to new cameras and foods is unclear. We fine-tuned DINOv2-base on 2,253 weighed Nutrition5k dishes to predict mass from one RGB photograph and compared it with a zero-shot vision-language model, Qwen3-VL-32B, on Nutrition5k and three external datasets: ASA24 studio portions, ECUSTFD whole foods and Food Portion Benchmark meal trays. In domain, the fine-tuned model's mean absolute error was 24.8 g, less than half of Qwen's. On external datasets it did not beat Qwen. For a food it had never seen, it ranked portions by size, but no better than a count of food pixels. Its gram scale was wrong in two ways: it changed with how much of the scene the photograph showed, and it was off by a different factor for each food. Training on other foods or trays from the tested dataset cut the external error by 38 to 65%. DINOv2-large, a larger backbone trained on the same dishes, did not reduce the external error. One labeled photograph of the same food cut the model's ASA24 error to 28.3 g, better than that photograph achieved alone. Prediction intervals that held the true mass for 90% of Nutrition5k dishes held it for at most half of external photographs. Nutrition5k often photographed the same plate several times, so a random test split shared near-identical photographs with training and overstated accuracy. Single-image mass estimators therefore need labeled photographs from the setting where they are used.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Cross-Grade Generalization in Banana Variety Classification via Hybrid CNN-Transformers and Knowledge Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29008)**  `arXiv:2610.29008`  
  _Khush Kataruka, Mohammad Muzammila, Zahid Akhtar, Raghavendra Ramachandra, Praveen Kumar Chandaliya_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, automated fruit classification systems have become a more critical component in precision agriculture for efficient yield estimation, quality control, and supply chain optimization. However, they still face significant challenges under varying quality, fruit maturity, illumination, texture degradation, morphological attributes, domain shift, and post-harvest handling conditions. In this study, we address the challenging issue of cross-grade banana variety classification. This is a real-world setting where models that have been trained on specific quality grades must generalize to unseen grades to handle significant domain shift. Under a rigorous Leave-One-Grade-Out evaluation protocol, we present a thorough comparative analysis of CNNs, ViTs, and dual-stream hybrid architectures using a large-scale dataset of 31,678 banana images with three varieties and three quality grades. Specifically, to address the drawbacks of single-paradigm frameworks, we present a dual-stream Swin ResNet hybrid framework enhanced with KL-divergence-based self-distillation to align probabilistic feature representations across the CNN and Transformer streams. Comprehensive experimental analyzes show that the presented hybrid framework with KL-divergence based self-distillation achieves better overall performance, i.e., it attains a mean accuracy of 81.25% and significantly surpasses CNN and Transformer baseline models. Qualitative analysis further confirms that the hybrid framework in this study improves discriminative feature transferability, improves attention localization, and stabilizes optimization.
  </details>

- **[T-DIG: Text-Conditioned Diversity for Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29003)**  `arXiv:2610.29003`  
  _Simran Khanuja, Rohit Gandikota, Gaurav Parmar, Graham Neubig_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) platforms typically return several images per prompt so users can explore different visual interpretations and choose one that matches their intent. However, T2I models are usually optimized for single-image quality which comes at a cost of multi-image diversity, limiting this exploration. Diversity is also contextual: one user wants variations in background, while another wants them in artistic style, and the same user may later want variations in clothing. The generated images should differ along whatever axis the user cares about. In this paper, we introduce T-DIG, a text-conditioned diversity framework for image generation and editing. T-DIG is an iterative framework comprising multiple modules that lets users type prompts, inspect default generations, request variations using natural language, and continue refining outputs until they're satisfied. At its core, T-DIG uses an LLM policy that produces K axis-conditioned prompts or edit instructions for a frozen generator or editor. We train this policy with GRPO using a text-conditioned diversity reward and alignment penalties. Because the image generator remains frozen, T-DIG applies to open-weight, distilled, and proprietary models. We also release the first dataset for text-conditioned prompt diversity in image generation, with the training and validation splits built from human-written axes and a test set drawn from real user interactions with T-DIG. T-DIG far exceeds baselines in improving text-conditioned diversity, while preserving base-prompt alignment across a variety of metrics and human judgements. We show that user-directed diversity exploration can be added to a wide range of image generation models, open and closed, without the need to access their weights. Website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[MedBOE: Optimization-Based Estimation of Complete Patient Body Models from Whole-Body Medical Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28985)**  `arXiv:2610.28985`  
  _Hoijoon Jung, Yuhao Wei, Mingxiao Tu, Younhyun Jung, Michael Fulham, Jinman Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  True whole-body medical imaging captures a patient from head to toe and provides comprehensive anatomical and/or functional information. This coverage enables reconstruction of the external body surface, yielding patient body models. However, scanner geometry (e.g., bore diameter), patient characteristics, and positioning can leave body regions missing (e.g., lateral arms/torso, hands, forearms, elbows), producing incomplete patient body models. Such incompleteness limits downstream medical analyses, including derivation of body-habitus metrics (BHMs), such as body volume and surface area, for injected radiotracer activity regimens, cardiometabolic risk assessment, and computational anatomical modeling for medical imaging simulation and radiation dosimetry. We present the Medical Body model Optimization-based Estimator (MedBOE), an automated, training-free framework for estimating complete patient body models from whole-body images with missing body regions. Our innovation is to leverage the Skinned Multi-Person Linear (SMPL) template, a parametric body mesh representing a broad range of body shapes and poses, in medical imaging. MedBOE aligns the SMPL template to the incomplete patient body models via attribute-guided optimization and estimates missing body regions through geometrically and volumetrically constrained deformation. We evaluated MedBOE using two public datasets: AutoPET (91 whole-body PET-CT images) and Human Implicit Tissues (HIT; 40 whole-body segmentations from MRI images). MedBOE achieved the best overall volumetric and geometric performance among evaluated methods. BHMs derived from MedBOE were within 2% error, suggesting low percentage errors for downstream analyses. MedBOE offers an automated, training-free method for estimating complete patient body models from whole-body images with missing body regions.
  </details>

- **[TEMPL: A Resource-Efficient and Transparent Model-Free Framework for Pseudo-Label Generation in Material CT Scans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28974)**  `arXiv:2610.28974`  
  _Ashiqur Rahman, Venkata Devesh Reddy Seethi, Austin Yunker, Rajkumar Kettimuthu, Hamed Alhoori_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The scarcity of large annotated datasets limits deep learning (DL) segmentation in material CT analysis. DL-based pseudo-labeling can help, but it is often computationally expensive and hard to interpret. We introduce a multi-stage, model-free framework that generates pseudo-labels directly from grayscale CT scans. It combines thresholding, morphological dilation, and a custom breadth-first search region-growing algorithm to produce polygonal annotations without pre-trained weights, labeled data, or a GPU. We compare it against the zero-shot Segment Anything Model (SAM), a fully supervised 3D U-Net, and classical baselines. On a dendrite dataset with ground truth, our method reaches 63.19% IoU versus 10.74% for zero-shot SAM, with four times the instance recall at 0.5 IoU (33.3% versus 8.3%). The supervised 3D U-Net serves as a reference ceiling that requires expert labels. On a silica sand specimen, YOLO models trained on our labels performed comparably to models trained on SAM labels in a qualitative comparison. Across 1,053 extracted grains, the median equivalent diameter was 163.8 micrometers, near the specimen's known 106 to 150 micrometers sieve range, compared with 219.5 micrometers for SAM, whose labels merge adjacent particles and add spurious detections. A sensitivity analysis shows a stable operating range for the key threshold and a sharp, predictable failure point linked to phase contrast. Our method needs 15s per image on a standard CPU versus 28s for SAM on a GPU. These results support our transparent approach as a tool for annotation bootstrapping and microstructural quantification in resource-limited settings.
  </details>

- **[Pair-Free Coarse-to-Real Video Generation via Real-to-Coarse Image Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28954)**  `arXiv:2610.28954`  
  _Gonzalo Gómez-Nogales, Daohan Lu, Peiye Zhuang, Tinghui Zhou, Yi Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coarse-to-real (C2R) video generation converts coarse animation, simulation, or game-rendered videos into photorealistic videos while preserving scene layout, camera motion, and dynamics. Training such models requires aligned (coarse, realistic) video pairs, which are expensive to create and confine prior systems to narrow synthetic domains. We exploit a simple asymmetry: paired videos are hard to obtain, but paired real-to-coarse images are easy to construct with existing editing and 3D reconstruction tools. Using these image pairs, we train the DINO-R2C model to transfer realistic images to coarse images in DINO feature space, and apply it frame-wise to ordinary real videos to synthesize pseudo-coarse videos. The C2R# model then learns to generate each real video from its pseudo-coarse counterpart, free from needing (coarse, realistic) video pairs. At inference, the DINO-R2C model is discarded and actual coarse videos drive the C2R# model directly. Thus, our pair-free training scales with any real-video corpora rather than with paired synthetic videos. We will release the model and project webpage atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MINER: Modeling Inter- and Intra-Person Variance for Reidentification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28941)**  `arXiv:2610.28941`  
  _Oron Nir, Eliyahu Strugo, Ariel Shamir_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clothes-Changing Person Re-Identification (CC-ReID) remains challenging due to drastic appearance shifts across outfits. Most prior works devise biometric signal-extraction heuristics to enhance inter-person variance, e.g., using segmentation masks, while leaving intra-person variance uncontrolled. We present MINER, a simple model-agnostic framework that suppresses garment bias by contrasting hard examples and adaptively balancing dataset-dependent intra- and inter-person variance. Extensive experiments show that MINER improves retrieval across CC-ReID benchmarks, achieving state-of-the-art performance on four datasets and ranking among the leaders in all three tracks of the open TVRID RGB-Depth ReID challenge.
  </details>

- **[Data-Driven Glyph Positioning for Handwritten Music Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28899)**  `arXiv:2610.28899`  
  _Elona Shatri, Jiří Mayer, Jan Hajič jr_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthesised data is a common strategy to address the lack of annotated handwritten music scores in Optical Music Recognition (OMR). Smashcima is a recent synthesis framework that assembles pages of handwritten scores by sampling glyph images from MUSCIMA++ and positioning them with hard-coded rules. In this work we ask whether these rules are grounded empirically and whether a learned model for context-dependent positioning can further improve synthesis quality. We extract inter-glyph distributions from around 285 thousand annotated relationships across MUSCIMA++ and OmniOMR, finding that several key parameters deviate systematically from real handwriting. Replacing these constants with empirical means reduces positioning RMSE by up to 60%. We then train a Graph Neural Network (GNN) on the Music Notation Graph structure to predict context-dependent offsets, achieving an additional 50 to 85% RMSE reduction on stems and beams by modelling how neighbouring glyphs constrain placement. Cross-dataset analysis shows that spacing differences between the two corpora are explained by note density (3.3), not style, and that duration-proportional spacing does not hold in handwriting (R2 = 0.001). In downstream evaluation, a YOLOv8 detector pretrained on GNN-positioned synthetic data achieves +30% mAP50 over the baseline and +11% over empirical means when fine-tuned on 200 real handwritten staves, showing that positioning accuracy matters for downstream OMR.
  </details>

- **[Policy-Mask: Budget-Aware Adaptation of Diffusion Features to Discriminative Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28889)**  `arXiv:2610.28889`  
  _Ivan Listopadov, Maksim Nakhodnov, Aibek Alanov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting intermediate features from a frozen diffusion model for discriminative tasks is non-trivial because the useful signal is distributed across the denoising trajectory, network depth, and channels. In practice, this requires choosing a timestep, a block, and a channel subset. Existing methods either fix these choices by hand or optimize only a few extraction hyperparameters, most often the timestep, then reuse the same rule across inputs and budgets. We introduce Policy-Mask, a budget-conditioned policy trained on the downstream objective that jointly selects all three under a prescribed channel budget. Without normalization, the channel-score contribution grows with the number of selected channels and dominates the timestep and block updates. We normalize it by subset cardinality, keeping the three policy heads on comparable scales and stabilizing joint training. At matched budgets, Policy-Mask improves over fixed extraction and feature-filter baselines across segmentation, classification, and semantic correspondence, reaching 38.9 mIoU on ADE20K, 75.4% top-1 on CIFAR-100 at 128 dimensions, and 76.3 PCK@0.1 on SPair-71k. Component-wise ablations show that block selection materially affects downstream accuracy, and the highest recorded channel-budget score occurs at 256 retained channels, identifying a practical operating point for compact diffusion representations. We further adapt Policy-Mask to diffusion transformers, including PixArt-$\Sigma$ and SD3.5 MMDiT, by replacing U-Net channels with image-token hidden dimensions.
  </details>

- **[DualHeads: Reward-tilted few-step diffusion made simple](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28858)**  `arXiv:2610.28858`  
  _Yuanzhi Zhu, Eleftherios Tsonis, Simo Alami Chehboune, Xi Wang, Stéphane Lathuilière, Vicky Kalogeiton_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distilled diffusion models generate high-quality images in a few sampling steps, but aligning them with task requirements and human preferences is difficult. Methods developed for multi-step diffusion models rely on differentiable rewards, or on score fields and stochastic trajectories that distilled generators may lack. We ask whether a few-step generator can be adapted using only external reward evaluations and reference samples, and show that two binary classifiers suffice. Our method, DualHeads, implements them as lightweight heads on a frozen diffusion backbone. The reward head learns which samples of the same prompt score higher, the reference head separates reference images from generated ones, and the generator maximizes a weighted sum of their logits. When the heads are optimal, this objective is KL-regularized maximization of the learned reward, and the ratio of head weights sets the regularization strength. Empirically, with both rule-based and model-based rewards, a few-step DualHeads generator reaches task rewards close to those of 40-step reinforcement learning methods while preserving image quality, at no extra inference cost. We hope that this simple and elegant dual-head formulation will help advance few-step reward-tilted generation.
  </details>

- **[Depth-guided Geometric Adaptation for Few-shot Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28856)**  `arXiv:2610.28856`  
  _Zhiqi Miao, Estefanía Talavera, Weichuan Zhang, Jiapan Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot image classification (FSIC) frequently suffers from visual ambiguity, where background clutter and RGB appearance similarities hinder discriminative feature matching in data-scarce settings. To mitigate this, we propose DAFIC, a geometry-guided framework that incorporates monocular depth into representation learning, episodic adaptation, and dense support-query matching. DAFIC first learns geometry-aware token representations through RGB-D self-supervised pre-training. Subsequently, the Depth-guided Geometric Adaptation (DGA) module uses depth-derived geometry to refine patch representations with geometry-enriched class context while suppressing background interference. Spatial Bidirectional Patch Matching (SBiM) then performs bidirectional dense matching on the refined features, reducing sensitivity to spurious correspondences and background clutter. Extensive experiments on four benchmarks show that DAFIC achieves strong performance against existing methods, with ablation and robustness analyses supporting the effectiveness of coupling geometry-conditioned episodic adaptation with bidirectional matching for few-shot visual recognition.
  </details>

- **[HorizonComposer: Spatiotemporally Consistent Driving Video Editing with Enriched Traffic Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28829)**  `arXiv:2610.28829`  
  _Mauricio Soroco, Yuqiu Liu, Zaid Tasneem, Francesco Pittaluga, Abhishek Aich, Wuyang Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-guided video generative models offer a scalable solution for stress-testing autonomous driving systems by simulating diverse environmental conditions. However, driving scenes operate within a dense Operational Design Domain (ODD) governed by strict traffic semantics. When applied to global driving-condition edits, general-purpose instruction-guided video editors frequently corrupt these semantics, leading to structural hallucinations and ``temporal shock.'' This work distills and protects traffic semantics during video editing at three levels. First, we construct a hybrid dataset by combining temporally aligned real-world videos with synthetic edits, balancing foundational scene structure with unambiguous appearance signals. Second, during training, we adapt advantage conditioning from offline reinforcement learning to align the model using dense rewards. We extract dense, pixel-level rewards from the hybrid data to serve as a localized signal and actively steer generation toward structurally reliable pixels. Finally, our model mitigates temporal shock by introducing thinking frames, a transition mechanism that seamlessly reconciles static image conditioning with dynamic video context. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method prevents the corruption of multi-actor dynamics, establishing state-of-the-art spatiotemporal consistency, yielding consistent user preference (39% relative improvement in content preservation and 12% in temporal consistency), and achieving a significant 5% gain in downstream road line segmentation and 7% gain in downstream multi-object detection.
  </details>

- **[Register Anything Model for Generalizable and Robust Point Cloud Registration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28821)**  `arXiv:2610.28821`  
  _Zheng Qin, Junhua Xi, Yan Huang, Jiahong Lai, Anwen Huang, Qiong Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Register Anything Model (RAM), a unified model for generalizable 3D point cloud registration that robustly estimates 6-DoF transformations across diverse domains spanning sensors and environments. Existing registration methods are typically tailored to a single domain, with carefully tuned architectures and hyperparameters, and thus degrade markedly when applied to novel domains. We bridge this gap by training a single model on diverse data from indoor, outdoor, and object domains. A central challenge is the severe geometric discrepancy across domains, i.e., differences in scene scale, sensor noise, point density, and structural patterns, which hinders processing with a unified point-based model. To address this, we introduce a geometry-aware normalization strategy that jointly voxelizes and normalizes point clouds into a standardized geometric space, adaptively based on their geometric properties to preserve salient local structures while standardizing resolution. Built on this normalization, RAM follows a hierarchical coarse-to-fine paradigm, and employs an efficient geometric transformer that models local geometric consistency instead of globally over all points. This design mitigates discretization noise introduced by voxelization, and improves computational efficiency. Extensive experiments on 12 benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in terms of inlier ratio and registration recall. Notably, our method outperforms the counterparts trained on each separate domain, showing superior generalization capability.
  </details>

- **[SAIL: A Learnable Generalisation of Spatial Autocorrelation over Graph-Structured Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28806)**  `arXiv:2610.28806`  
  _Piotr Keller, Fayyaz Minhas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In vision and scientific domains involving spatial data, a key question is whether node feature values vary independently across space or form coherent spatial structures. Classical spatial autocorrelation statistics such as Moran's $I$ and Geary's $C$ quantify this phenomenon but operate on a single scalar variable with fixed neighbourhood weights, limiting their use for high-dimensional graph-like data. We introduce SAIL (Spatial Autocorrelation Index Learner), a differentiable framework that generalises spatial autocorrelation into a learnable operator by jointly learning feature projections defining candidate scalar fields and inter-node neighbourhood affinities. SAIL produces interpretable local and global spatial coherence descriptors, enabling supervised prediction and unsupervised discovery of spatial autocorrelation modes and hotspots. We show that SAIL exactly recovers classical spatial statistics under appropriate parametrisations and, for tasks driven by latent spatial autocorrelation, can approximate the Bayes-optimal predictor with an inductive bias matched to such tasks. Experiments on synthetic data and large-scale biomedical graphs demonstrate statistical correctness and discovery of spatial coherence with applications across vision, geospatial analysis, and biomedicine. The code repository is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[JUICE: Unified Multimodal Modeling in Pretrained Representation Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28801)**  `arXiv:2610.28801`  
  _Jiahan Li, Huangyuan Su, Shengbang Tong, Shusheng Yang, Ruihan Guo, Suozhi Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most unified multimodal models treat images and text asymmetrically: a pretrained language model generates text, an attached diffusion head generates images, and outputs that contain both are produced in a fixed order, so the first output cannot adapt to the second. We ask whether both modalities can instead be generated by one process that is trained on them jointly from scratch. Pretrained encoder spaces hold both modalities in one continuous form and carry knowledge learned at scale, and recent work finds them effective as generative targets for vision and language. We therefore model the joint distribution directly in these spaces. We introduce JUICE (Joint Unified multimodal modeling In Continuous Encoder spaces), a single flow matching Transformer over frozen image and text representations, in which each modality has its own noise level. Which modalities are clean and which are generated then decides the task: image generation, captioning, visual question answering, or joint generation. Controlled pretraining runs test each design choice. At equal guidance, generating text as continuous states leaves image generation intact (48.41 GenEval, against 47.86 for a model trained only for text-to-image generation), while captions lose some exact wording. Training on joint and conditional denoising together beats either alone, and semantic encoder spaces beat VAE latents and raw pixels as targets. At 2.1B parameters, with no language model initialization and at 224x224, JUICE reaches up to 0.88 on GenEval and 73.1 on VQAv2. By sampling both modalities from the joint distribution (coupled sampling), it can also draw a route on a map while writing out the matching moves. On mazes with several valid solutions, joint success rises from 54.6% with independent sampling to 93.7%, and no modality has to go first. On visual spatial planning, JUICE solves 99.8% of maps at its training sizes.
  </details>

- **[Complexity-Aware Surgical Video Reasoning Segmentation with Digital Twin and Context Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28776)**  `arXiv:2610.28776`  
  _Yiqing Shen, Mathias Unberath_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional surgical segmentation relies on closed-set categories or explicit text queries, which cannot resolve implicit queries that require reasoning about workflows, instrument functions, and temporal relationships. To address these limitations, we propose SurgReasonSeg, a complexity-aware method that brings reasoning segmentation over digital twin (DT) representations to surgical video. Specifically, SurgReasonSeg constructs DT representations from surgical foundation models and trains two low-rank adapters (LoRA) of one large language model with reinforcement learning (RL), each specialized for a different query complexity. A lightweight router, also trained with RL, selects between the two adapters to balance latency against accuracy. SurgReasonSeg also sets the temporal context with an adaptive sliding window conditioned on query complexity and workflow phase, and retrieves relevant historical cases from a surgical context memory by workflow similarity and procedural relevance. We further introduce EndoVis-ReasonSeg, a surgical reasoning segmentation benchmark with 1,460 training and 961 test samples across semantic, spatial, and temporal categories. On EndoVis-ReasonSeg, SurgReasonSeg improves J&F over the strongest baseline, ReSurgSAM2, by 0.178, 0.237, and 0.297 on semantic, spatial, and temporal queries, and it also achieves the best results on the Ref-EndoVis17 and Ref-EndoVis18 referring segmentation benchmarks. A backbone-matched attribution protocol separates the contribution of the upstream segmenter from that of the reasoning modules. Our method runs at 18.8 FPS averaged over the query distribution, enabling real-time reasoning segmentation in surgical videos.
  </details>

- **[Different Defects, Different Effects: A Controlled Audit of Face Recognition Training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28766)**  `arXiv:2610.28766`  
  _Ana Dias, João Ribeiro Pinto, Hugo Proença, João C. Neves_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The development of face recognition has been strongly supported by the availability of large-scale training datasets. Among them, MS-Celeb-1M has played an important role by providing millions of facial images from nearly 100K identities. However, the size and heterogeneity of such datasets increase the likelihood of annotation errors, redundant samples, and other forms of data inconsistency. This motivated the introduction of refined versions, such as MS1MV2 and MS1MV3, aimed at improving the quality of the training data. Despite these cleaning efforts, we show that several types of training-data defects remain in MS1MV3. By proposing complementary data-auditing strategies, we identify five defect categories: exact and near-duplicate images, split identities, crop errors, multiple-face ambiguity, and invalid content. We then assess the effect of correcting these defects under a controlled face-recognition training protocol based on IResNet-50 and ArcFace. The results show that targeted interventions can preserve, and in some cases improve, recognition performance while requiring fewer optimization steps to reach the performance of the original training set. Duplicate removal provides the clearest overall benefit, while split-identity merging and invalid-content removal also improve training efficiency. Overall, the results indicate that the effect of dataset cleaning depends strongly on the type of defect being addressed and provide further evidence that training-data quality remains an important and comparatively underexplored factor in large-scale face recognition. The training manifests and code used in this work will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[EvaOmni: An Evidence-Grounded Benchmark for Audio-Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28444)**  `arXiv:2610.28444`  
  _Xiaoxing Hu, Wenxi Yao, Chaoyuan Shi, Ziqi Ye, Xiaorong Guo, Peixuan Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni LLMs can naturally locate key audio and visual evidence and integrate information across modalities for audiovisual reasoning. Existing audio-visual reasoning benchmarks that mainly rely on multiple-choice questions usually score models solely based on answer correctness, making it difficult to assess whether a model truly forms a reasoning chain from evidence to the correct answer. In addition, textual or unimodal shortcuts may allow models to answer correctly without analyzing the necessary audio-visual evidence, further weakening the reliability of evaluation benchmarks. To address these issues, we introduce EvaOmni, an evidence-grounded benchmark containing 392 videos and 1,027 purely human-annotated question-answer pairs, covering 15 capabilities and three levels: retrieval and localization, basic understanding, and complex reasoning. We design a series of rigorous annotation procedures in which experts annotate audio and visual evidence and construct video-based distractors; humans review whether questions meet strict inspection criteria, and finally models filter textual and unimodal shortcuts. Beyond high-quality question-answer pairs, we require models to simultaneously return the answer and supporting evidence intervals in order to evaluate the complete reasoning chain. We propose the Coverage Grounded Score (C.G.) to measure how much annotated evidence a model identifies, and the Bottleneck Grounded Score (B.G.) to penalize poor temporal localization in either modality; both are incorporated into answer correctness. Across 7 open-weight and 17 proprietary Omni LLMs, Gemini3.7-Flash achieved the highest overall accuracy (79.4%), C.G. (70.8%), and B.G. (35.1%). At Level 3, it reached 79.1% accuracy, but its B.G. was only 12.3%. These results show that even the most advanced models still have room for development in grounded audio-visual reasoning.
  </details>

- **[CountVideoBench: A Benchmark to Evaluate Prediction Biases on the Object Counting task by Video-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28356)**  `arXiv:2610.28356`  
  _Marc Chapus, Carlos Crispim-Junior, Véronique Eglin, Atilla Baskurt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object counting in videos remains a poorly characterized capability of multimodal large language models. Counting questions are included in many existing VideoQA benchmarks, but the ground-truth count distributions in these benchmarks are heavily skewed toward small values. Across the nine benchmarks we survey, the median ground-truth count is at most five, and the three most frequent values together account for between 45% and 93% of the videos. The resulting mean accuracy is then dominated by the most frequent counts; a value-dependent bias at a rare count contributes negligibly to the average and remains invisible. We introduce CountVideoBench, a benchmark of 2,600 synthetic videos covering 26 cardinalities in the interval [5, 50] (100 videos each), grouped into six clusters of consecutive counts that span the range while enabling near-neighbor comparisons. Each video contains a single class of static objects under a controlled camera motion, removing object motion and reducing recognition as confounds. We evaluate sixteen video-language models from nine architecture families in a zero-shot setting. No model exceeds 27.6% Exact Match. The dependence of accuracy on cardinality varies across models, ranging from monotonic decline to a non-monotonic peak-and-collapse pattern, which rules out increasing difficulty with cardinality as the dominant explanation of counting failure. Every model further concentrates its predictions on its own set of preferred integers, with accuracy peaks coinciding with these values and collapsing at adjacent counts. These findings highlight the need for balanced, diagnostic counting benchmarks to reliably assess this fundamental visual capability.
  </details>

- **[RayCo4D: Ray-Conditioned Continuous Motion Model for Monocular 4D Dynamic Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28351)**  `arXiv:2610.28351`  
  _Yuanxiang Ni, Xianliang Huang, Ruihan Hou, Feng Xie, Hao Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing dynamic 3D scenes from monocular video requires inferring motion from noisy and intermittently visible observations. Long-range tracks provide valuable geometric constraints, but integrating their fragmented temporal coverage into shared, continuous motion remains challenging. We introduce RayCo4D, a ray-conditioned continuous motion model for monocular 4D reconstruction that explicitly separates visual measurements from latent 3D motion states. Each track is associated with a reference-time 3D query constrained to its camera ray, and its image and depth observations constrain shared latent motion nodes through local many-to-many associations. Each node maintains an $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ pose and body-frame velocity at sparse support times. A white-noise-on-acceleration process prior couples neighboring states and defines continuous-time interpolation, allowing observations across visibility intervals to jointly constrain the same latent state sequence. The inferred shared motion is transferred to Gaussian primitives through dual-quaternion blending, while reference-anchored local $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ residuals capture fine-scale non-rigid deformation beyond the shared scaffold. Experiments on diverse monocular dynamic scenes show that RayCo4D achieves competitive novel-view synthesis quality, improves structural and temporal consistency under rapid motion and incomplete tracks, and supports continuous-time rendering.
  </details>

- **[Reliability-Guided Vision-Language Decoding with Text-Aware Skip Refinement for Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28118)**  `arXiv:2610.28118`  
  _Sivan Talaei, Fatemeh Daneshfar, Abdulhady Abas Abdullah, Mourad Oussalah_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical image segmentation remains challenging when targets exhibit limited visual distinction, irregular structure, or substantial variation across imaging modalities. Medical text can reduce this ambiguity, but indiscriminate fusion may introduce unreliable guidance. We present RELiF, a vision--language framework that integrates text through reliability-guided decoding. Its Reliability-guided Fusion Gate (RFG) adaptively controls cross-modal updates, while the Text-guided Skip Refinement Adapter (TSRA) refines high-resolution features using text guidance. RELiF is evaluated on radiographic, CT, and endoscopic segmentation tasks, where it shows competitive performance across diverse imaging characteristics and target appearances. Additional analyses examine efficiency and stability. The complementary uncertainty representation derived from the reliability response also shows an association with prediction difficulty, with higher uncertainty values occurring more frequently in pixels associated with segmentation errors. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[LaLa-World: Scaling Latent World Models with Natural Language Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27851)**  `arXiv:2610.27851`  
  _Junha Song, Geonmo Gu, Jeongeun Park, Heejeong Nam, Lucas Maes, Byeongho Heo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper shows that JEPA-style latent world models (LWMs) can learn across diverse domains and data sources by adopting the language-conditioned interface of video generation models. We establish this approach as an effective and efficient basis for planning with imagination and downstream policy fine-tuning. To this end, we introduce LaLa-World, a Language-conditioned Latent World model that learns from simulation and robot trajectories together with broader image and video data. Two designs make this work. First, we describe both numerical actions and visual changes in language, enabling joint training of a single future predictor across heterogeneous datasets. Second, we build the predictor on a language model, allowing it to interpret language conditions while predicting future latent states. The resulting predictor achieves higher average MPC planning performance than existing LWMs trained separately for each domain or task. Adding image and video data further improves average planning performance and robustness to action phrasing. In a matched comparison with an RAEv2-based video generation model baseline, LaLa-World reaches comparable planning performance with $6\times$ less training cost and $13.9\times$ faster planning. Beyond planning, the pretrained LaLa-World serves as an initialization for LaLa-Policy, which jointly predicts actions and future visual representations. LaLa-Policy performs competitively with Cosmos Policy on LIBERO and RoboCasa, with up to $5.5\times$ faster inference.
  </details>

- **[Text-Anchored Modality Experts with Uncertainty-Guided Routing for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27571)**  `arXiv:2610.27571`  
  _Xiaohua Huang, Bingrui Lei, Xiuyan Shao, Ling Zhou, Yuan Zong, Wenming Zheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal sentiment analysis (MSA) aims to understand user-generated videos by integrating multiple modalities. Existing methods often treat modalities as symmetric, overlooking the fact that language provides a dominant semantic prior while nonverbal signals are complementary and noise-prone. To address this, we propose a Text-Anchored Modality Experts with Uncertainty-Guided Routing, termed TAME, that reformulates MSA as an uncertainty-guided refinement paradigm, where the language prior is calibrated and enhanced by selectively trusted nonverbal evidence. Specifically, TAME adopts an asymmetric Mixture-of-Modality-Experts framework, in which textual features act as a fixed and always-activated shared expert, while audio-textual and video-textual cross-modal branches serve as dynamically routed auxiliary experts. Furthermore, we introduce lightweight Prophet Heads to quantify the sample-level predictive uncertainty of each individual modality, and this uncertainty metric is further incorporated into the modality router to realize reliability-aware expert aggregation and fusion. Experiments on CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, and CH-SIMS datasets demonstrate that TAME achieves state-of-the-art performance on the majority of metrics while reducing parameter count by up to 78.7% and FLOPs by up to 41.2% over the mixture-of-expert baseline.
  </details>

- **[XGLASS: X-Ray, Gaze and Language Assisted Slice Sequencing for CT Volumes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26255)**  `arXiv:2610.26255`  
  _Michael Glöckler, Timo Ropinski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) achieve remarkable performance on 2D medical images, such as X-rays or pathology images, while 3D VLMs that directly process CT/MRI volumes lag behind due to limited 3D training data and the predominance of 2D foundation architectures. To leverage powerful 2D VLMs for analyzing medical volumes, which contain hundreds of slices, it is therefore necessary to focus only on those few slices, which are diagnostically relevant. To enable such a prioritization, we propose XGLASS, a method that transfers medical expertise from 2D X-rays to 3D CT for task-aware slice prioritization. XGLASS uses disease-conditioned importance maps derived from language-aligned radiologist gaze on X-rays and spatially aligns them with coronal CT projections, bringing gaze priors and CT content into a shared 2D representation. The fused gaze-prior/CT projection is then used to predict a disease-specific ordering of axial slices, prioritizing diagnostically relevant slices. XGLASS significantly improves budgeted retrieval over 2D and 3D-native baselines, achieving a top-10 hit rate up to 75.9% for the best-performing disease. In practice, this means that after inspecting only the top 10 slices (4.17% of a 240-slice scan), XGLASS identifies a diagnostically relevant slice in more than 75% of cases. For downstream slice-wise finding classification, an XGLASS-selected 30-slice candidate set reduces model calls by 87.5% while improving F1 by 11.3 percentage points over all-slice inference, 4.2 points over the strongest 2D selector, and remains competitive with 3D-native selectors while requiring up to 130.8 times less time and 10.9 times less GPU memory per finding, supporting its use as a lightweight preselector for volumetric CT analysis. The code and pretrained checkpoint are available onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HORIA: 4D Human and Object Reconstruction from a Monocular Video with Interaction-Aware Re-Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25960)**  `arXiv:2610.25960`  
  _Hyeongjin Nam, Taeryung Lee, Daniel Sungho Jung, Kyoung Mu Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Joint 4D reconstruction of a human and object from a monocular video is essential for understanding how people interact with their environment. While recent methods have made noticeable progress, capturing such human-object interactions remains difficult due to two main challenges. First, a video typically contains composite semantic context spanning multiple interaction phases, making it difficult to track the temporal changes of the interaction in the reconstruction. Second, under human-object occlusion, a single-view observation contains incomplete visual information about the interaction, resulting in geometrically inconsistent reconstructions. To address these challenges, we introduce HORIA, built upon two core designs. First, we decompose the entire input video into temporally grounded and semantically distinct interaction phases with motion-aware captioning, obtaining a specific text description for each phase. Second, conditioned on these text descriptions of the interaction, we propose an interaction-aware re-rendering that compensates for the limited single-view observation. Specifically, we render the initially reconstructed 4D human and object from novel viewpoints and refine the renderings with a text-to-video diffusion prior, which provides auxiliary novel-view supervision beyond single-view cues. As a result, our framework produces temporally coherent and accurate 4D reconstructions under diverse interaction scenarios, achieving state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[MT Forcing: Towards Stable and Consistent Multi-Turn Image Editing with Autoregressive Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25404)**  `arXiv:2610.25404`  
  _Houyuan Chen, Fanzhou Wang, Xiangyu Fan, Zesong Qiu, Yukun Wei, Songlin Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn image editing aims to modify an image according to a sequence of instructions, enabling more effective interactive visual content creation for users. However, existing methods often exhibit noticeable drift after only a few editing turns, manifesting in two aspects: 1) unstable visual quality, with accumulating artifacts and degraded details; and 2) inconsistent image content, with unintended changes to preserved regions and prior edits. To address these challenges, we propose MT Forcing, a novel training paradigm for multi-turn image editing with unified multimodal models (UMMs) based on autoregressive diffusion. Specifically, we propose Single-Turn Distribution Matching Distillation (Single-Turn DMD), which supervises student self-rollouts with a teacher that applies cumulative edits to the source image in a single turn, providing visual-quality guidance without recursive error accumulation. Additionally, we introduce Teacher-Forcing Guidance, which supervises history-dependent transitions on reference trajectories during warmup and as a regularizer during distillation, encouraging cross-turn content consistency beyond what per-turn distribution matching alone can ensure. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly improves the multi-turn image editing capabilities of UMMs, yielding more stable visual quality and stronger cross-turn consistency.
  </details>

- **[RAHF-Net: Range-Aware Heterogeneous Fusion for LiDAR-Assisted RGB Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25400)**  `arXiv:2610.25400`  
  _R.D. Lin, Pengcheng Weng, Ge Wang, Han Ding, Fei Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper studies LiDAR-assisted RGB semantic segmentation, where LiDAR point-cloud features improve dense pixel-wise predictions in the RGB image plane rather than 3D point labels or detections. Existing 2D fusion methods often project point clouds into shallow depth/range maps and apply spatially uniform fusion, discarding rich 3D structure and ignoring the range-dependent reliability of outdoor LiDAR. We propose RAHF-Net, a Range-Aware Heterogeneous Fusion Network. Its Cylindrical-to-Perspective (C2P) module projects pretrained cylindrical voxel features into the image plane, preserving structured 3D semantic priors. A Range-Aware Heterogeneous Fusion (RAHF) module then uses a soft depth-derived range mask to perform LiDAR-guided geometric enhancement in reliable near-field regions and visual-context enhancement in sparse far-field regions. On KITTI-360, RAHF-Net achieves 65.76 mIoU, outperforming representative RGB-only and RGB-LiDAR baselines, including CMX by 1.45 points. Experiments on DELIVER further demonstrate improved robustness under adverse weather, illumination degradation, and sensor corruption.
  </details>

- **[Painting Change Map as guidance for generating painting processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25369)**  `arXiv:2610.25369`  
  _Markus Pobitzer, Chang Liu, Niccolò Biondi, Nicu Sebe_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Digital art generation has undergone a massive paradigm shift, moving from static image synthesis to dynamic video generation that can mimic the human painting process. While existing models can synthesize visually stunning time-lapses of paintings being created or "unpainted," they largely treat the creative process as a black box. Current conditioning mechanisms are either too rigid, relying on static geometric cues like Canny edges or depth maps, or too holistic, utilizing simple scalar progress variables that offer no localized control. Consequently, users lack the agency to steer how a painting evolves over time. In this work, we argue that true control over the video generation of artwork requires a joint understanding of two fundamental dimensions: where an artist acts, and when those actions take place. To achieve this, we introduce the Painting Change Map, a novel spatio-temporal control mechanism that explicitly tracks normalized structural changes across a 1,000-timestep continuum, a resolution sufficient to capture fine-grained stroke-level changes while remaining computationally tractable. By mapping localized visual differences into an encoded RGB space, our representation enables fine-grained guidance for both the forward painting and reverse unpainting processes. Extensive benchmarking demonstrates the efficacy of our approach. We outperform existing control baselines and achieve competitive or superior results compared to state-of-the-art painting methods. Furthermore, under oracle conditions, our method surpasses all baselines by a significant margin. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Goal Images Are Control Targets: Spatially-Grounded Goal-Image Synthesis for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24885)**  `arXiv:2610.24885`  
  _Riddhi Chatterjee, Ritish Shrirao, Kushagra Mehrotra, Viswanath Gopalakrishnan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Goal-conditioned manipulation policies are steered by a goal image, a picture of the desired post-manipulation scene synthesized from the current observation and a language instruction. The prevailing assumption is that better image-editing quality yields better goals, so goal generators are built and compared using general image-editing metrics. We show this assumption is false. Across two independent goal-conditioned policies on CALVIN, the image editors that top aggregate editing-quality leaderboards (Gemini 3 Pro Image and GPT Image 1.5) produce worse control targets than our proposed far smaller finetuned open models, and the gap widens sharply for the multi-step (long-horizon) tasks robots actually face. A per-dimension analysis explains why. Downstream policy success is governed almost entirely by manipulation adherence (selecting the right object and the right final state; Spearman $\rho \approx 0.9$ with success), while photorealism and aesthetic fidelity, precisely where large proprietary editors excel, are uncorrelated with success ($\rho \approx 0$). This dissociation holds on both CALVIN and the real-world-aligned SIMPLER-Bridge benchmark. We then present SGE-Goal, a spatially-grounded decomposed editing framework that predicts initial and final 2D edit-region masks before goal-image synthesis. SGE-Goal turns open base models (FLUX.1 Kontext, FLUX.2 klein) into the strongest goal generators we evaluate on CALVIN, and second only to Gemini 3 Pro Image on SIMPLER-Bridge. Base models fine-tuned with SGE-Goal also hold up under chaining far better than the best proprietary editor, sustaining roughly seven times the success rate at the longest (five-step) chains. We release code, datasets, checkpoints, and a human-aligned diagnostic evaluator. Our results argue that goal generators must be selected by downstream utility, not by editing-quality leaderboards.
  </details>

- **[Arbitrary-Scale Anisotropic 3D MRI Super-Resolution with Gaussian Prototypes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24858)**  `arXiv:2610.24858`  
  _Aditya Rastogi, Ramon Leiser, Prateek Singh, Martha Foltyn-Dumitru, Martin Bendszus, Fabian Bamberg, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reducing MRI acquisition time improves patient comfort, reduces motion artefacts, and increases scanner throughput. Clinical protocols often shorten acquisitions by sacrificing spatial resolution, particularly along the slice axis. Super-resolution can recover higher-resolution images, but existing methods are typically specialised to one scale or acquisition geometry, limiting their use across heterogeneous clinical protocols. We present GSMR3D, a continuous volumetric model that reconstructs arbitrary isotropic and anisotropic resolutions with a single checkpoint per MRI contrast. It combines a learned dictionary of anisotropic 3D Gaussian prototypes with scale-conditioned frequency encoding and an exact measurement-consistency projection, keeping reconstructions consistent with the acquired low-resolution data. We train separate models on 3,731 contrast-enhanced T1-weighted and 30,254 T1-weighted brain MRI volumes from clinical and population-based cohorts. We evaluate GSMR3D against geometry-specific specialists for isotropic, in-plane, and through-plane super-resolution using reconstruction quality and downstream tasks: brain tumour segmentation, brain-metastasis detection, and brain parcellation. GSMR3D outperforms specialists at isotropic $2\times$ and achieves better LPIPS, DISTS, and FID across all geometries, although specialists retain higher PSNR in anisotropic settings. It more faithfully preserves glioma segmentations across all geometries, achieves the highest lesion sensitivity on an independent brain-metastasis cohort, and the highest brain-parcellation agreement for isotropic and in-plane reconstruction. Reconstruction takes about 6s per volume, adding little overhead relative to acquisition-time savings. GSMR3D offers a flexible approach to MRI scan-time reduction with one model per contrast across geometries while preserving information relevant to downstream tasks.
  </details>

- **[NV-Generate-Body: 3D Latent Diffusion Model forControllable Anatomy, Tumor, and Demographic Body Mask Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24153)**  `arXiv:2610.24153`  
  _Can Zhao, Pengfei Guo, Dong Yang, Yufan He, Yucheng Tang, Ahmed Harouni, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Curating large labeled CT datasets is expensive and constrained by patient privacy. We present NV-Generate-BodyMask, a 3D latent diffusion model that synthesizes whole-body CT segmentation masks with precise, joint control over organ anatomy, tumor burden, and patient demographics. The model operates in the compressed latent space of a frozen mask autoencoder, generating $256^3$ volumetric label maps conditioned on a 19-dimensional vector via Adaptive Group Normalization and cross-attention. A size-adaptive ROI loss with spatial halo weighting and a paired tumor injection pipeline enable accurate tumor size conditioning despite the scarcity of annotated tumor cases. Trained on 8,571 masks across 26 cohorts spanning 132 labels, the model achieves Spearman $\rho = 0.70$--$0.92$ for organ size, 85--100\% tumor recall for lung, pancreatic, colon, and bone lesions, and strong demographic alignment (BMI $\rho = 0.78$, sex-conditioned perfect prostate control).
  </details>

- **[MTEdit: Packed Trajectories Beat Rollouts for Multi-Turn Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23751)**  `arXiv:2610.23751`  
  _Houyuan Chen, Fanzhou Wang, Xiangyu Fan, Songlin Yang, Zhitao Yang, Lvmin Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn image editing aims to modify an image according to a sequence of instructions, making it better aligned with human-AI collaborative creation than single-turn editing. However, constructing high-quality multi-turn training data remains challenging, as conventional sequential rollout accumulates errors and introduces visual-quality drift across editing turns. To address this challenge, we introduce MTEdit, a multi-turn image editing dataset built through a pipeline centered on packed trajectory canvas (PTC) generation. By jointly generating multiple editing states within a unified visual context, this pipeline reduces visual-quality drift across turns. Covering both step-wise and context-aware editing scenarios, MTEdit comprises 500K multi-turn image editing trajectories, with up to 8 turns and an average of 5.38 turns per trajectory, making it one of the most extensive datasets for multi-turn image editing. We further introduce MTEdit-Bench, a benchmark designed to evaluate both scenarios by assessing models' ability to follow editing instructions, maintain content consistency, resist visual-quality drift, and effectively use historical context across turns. Using MTEdit, we fine-tune Qwen-Image-Edit-Causal to obtain Qwen-Image-MTEdit. On MTEdit-Bench, we evaluate a broad range of closed-source and open-source image editing models, including Qwen-Image-MTEdit. Experimental results show that Qwen-Image-MTEdit achieves the best performance among the evaluated open-source models, demonstrating the effectiveness of MTEdit in improving models' multi-turn image editing capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Just Respect the Semantics: Image-Token Compression via Pre-LLM Semantic Variable-Resolution Tokenization and In-LLM Relevance Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18690)**  `arXiv:2610.18690`  
  _Tianhao Fu, Austin Wang, Charles Chen, Roby Aldave-Garza, Victor Xie, Xiyue Jiao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image-token compression must reduce computation without hurting the semantics. We introduce Varest, guided by semantic fungibility: tokens within a coherent semantic region can be summarized coarsely, while object boundaries and literal text require finer distinctions. Object and text localizers guide variable-resolution merging, preserving spatial coverage and finer detail at boundaries and text. Learned [REL] registers then prune visual tokens after early decoder processing. Training across budgets enables one checkpoint per backbone. Across three backbones and 13 benchmarks, Varest outperforms 15 baselines in mean performance retention at every evaluated budget, retaining 86.10-92.33% of full-token performance with only 5.56% of the original layer-averaged image-token count.
  </details>

- **[Traditional and Recent Approaches for Infrared Small Target Detection : Overview, Challenges and Perspectives (Part 2)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18291)**  `arXiv:2610.18291`  
  _Thierry Bouwmans, Anastasia Zakharova, Belmar Garcia, Marwa Chendeb El Rai, Meghna Kapoor, Badri Narayan Subudhi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Since the rise of supervised deep learning models, researchers have attempted to solve infrared small target detection using various models like convolutional neural networks, GANs, Transformers, Mixture of Experts and recent graph/hypergraph neural networks. Moreover, these deep neural network methods have surpassed the traditional unsupervised models and the low-rank and sparse decomposition unsupervised models reviewed in "Traditional and Recent Approaches for Infrared Small Target Detection : Overview, Challenges and Perspectives (Part 1)". In this context, the aim of this paper is to provide a complete survey of the deep learning models in infrared small target detection. First, we categorize the different approaches found in the literature in terms of their modeling strategies. Then, we discuss them in terms of the critical situations that they claim to handle. Furthermore, we present the specific performance evaluation protocols. Then, we conclude with several promising directions for future research.
  </details>

- **[Traditional and Recent Approaches for Infrared Small Target Detection : Overview, Challenges and Perspectives (Part 1)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18276)**  `arXiv:2610.18276`  
  _Thierry Bouwmans, Anastasia Zakharova, Belmar Garcia, Marwa Chendeb El Rai, Meghna Kapoor, Badri Narayan Subudhi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Infrared small target detection is often used in different civilian and military applications to detect static or moving targets in scenes like in ground, air and sea surveillance. Research in this field started more than three decades ago, and the last two decades have witnessed very significant publications in this field following the rise of low-rank and sparse decomposition (model-based methods) and deep learning models (data-based methods). Furthermore, several surveys can be found in the literature but none of them addresses an overall review in this field. In this context, the aim of this paper is to provide a complete survey of the traditional and recent approaches in infrared small target detection. First, we categorize the different approaches found in the literature in terms of the modeling models, i.e mathematical models, machine learning models and signal processing models. Then, we have discussed them in terms of the critical situations that they claim to handle. Furthermore, we present the available resources, datasets and libraries. Then, we conclude with several promising directions for future research. For the sake of reading, this survey is divided into two parts: 1) "Traditional and Recent Approaches for Infrared Small Target Detection: Overview, Challenges and Perspectives (Part 1)" about conventional and low-rank and sparse decomposition (model-based methods), and 2) "Traditional and Recent Approaches for Infrared Small Target Detection: Overview, Challenges and Perspectives (Part 2)" about deep learning models (data-based methods).
  </details>

- **[Beyond Masks and Trajectories: Flow-Guided Latent Action Injection for Stable Surgical Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09800)**  `arXiv:2610.09800`  
  _Tsz-Yui Qin, Siyu Zhou, Chi-Keung Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical video generation holds substantial potential for surgical education, simulation, and data augmentation, yet generating surgical videos with realistic and clinically plausible motion remains challenging. Most existing methods rely on auxiliary conditions, such as masks, trajectories, depth, or reference videos, to achieve visually plausible synthesis. Yet, these auxiliary conditions typically require additional manual annotation or specialized acquisition, making it difficult to scale such methods beyond small, curated datasets. This motivates the need for a reference-free architecture capable of generating high-quality surgical video without requiring auxiliary visual conditions at inference time. We propose FLAIR, a Flow-guided LatentAction Injection framework for Reference-free surgical video generation. FLAIR learns action priors from optical flow of real surgical videos, dynamically predicts corresponding latent action representation from an input prompt, and injects it into a frozen base model to generate surgical videos with improved action consistency. We further construct SurgActionClip-30K, the first large-scale surgical vision dataset comprising action-centric segmented clips and structured caption labels, addressing the persistent lack of fine-grained, action-centric surgical datasets. Lastly, we introduce SurgMetrics, the first surgical domain-specific evaluation metrics for quantifying the quality of generated surgical videos, addressing the persistent absence of clinically grounded evaluation standards in this domain. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FLAIR enables generating high-quality surgical videos using text-only inference without auxiliary conditions, and validation in SurgMetrics demonstrates its strength in alignment with human perception compared to traditional metrics.
  </details>

- **[One-Slide Calibration of Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08944)**  `arXiv:2610.08944`  
  _Ming Ren Hou, Tianyi Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scanner variation changes how pathology foundation models represent the same tissue. We introduce SlideRuler, which uses other regions within a slide as internal controls to predict a query region's scanner-induced shift. A transfer map learned from paired rescans enables calibration from a single scan at inference while keeping the foundation model fixed. Across two encoders and five SCORPION scanners, learned transfer reduces mean target-to-source embedding distance by 16.3-38.5% relative to raw embeddings. Replacing controls with unrelated same-scanner regions reveals a positive same-slide contribution in all four evaluation settings, including scanner holdout. A source-anchored variant reduces source-feature displacement by 47.7-83.6% relative to learned transfer while retaining most of its alignment gain. By drawing calibration information from the slide itself, SlideRuler offers a path toward more consistent use of frozen pathology models across imaging systems.
  </details>

- **[Long-MDR: Long-Context Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Deep-Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05195)**  `arXiv:2610.05195`  
  _On Tai Tang, Huanyao Zhang, Jiepeng Zhou, Wentao Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The next generation of multimodal research agents must reason over long-lived research histories rather than short model completions. During a single task, an agent may repeatedly search the web, inspect visual evidence, revisit earlier hypotheses, and accumulate tens of thousands of tokens of multimodal context. Despite this trend, online RL for multimodal research agents remains largely confined to shorter contexts and interaction horizons. We push online RL training to 128k context and 75+ tool-interaction turns. To our knowledge, this is the first online multimodal deep-research RL study trained at 128k context, and the first trained with a 75 tool-turn horizon. Scaling to this regime exposes several practical limitations of conventional RL training. Early in training, weak policies make poor use of large interaction budgets, causing expensive rollouts with little reward improvement. Later, policy entropy can collapse before performance has saturated, prematurely ending useful learning. We introduce Long-MDR, a three-component training recipe designed specifically for this setting: On-Policy Distillation Warmup, Progressive Horizon Expansion, and Entropy-Triggered Rescue. Together, these techniques improve both the learning efficiency and stability of long-horizon RL, enabling continued gains in a regime where direct training is slow and costly. Code will be available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[DiDE:Direct Injection with Color-Texture DEcoupling for 3D Stylization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02044)**  `arXiv:2610.02044`  
  _Tao Wu, Alexandra Gomez-Villa, Senmao Li, Yaxing Wang, Joost van de Weijer, Kai Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in rectified flow-based image-to-3D generative models have enabled high-fidelity 3D asset generation. Building on this, a growing line of work has exploited these strong 3D priors for training-free stylization, transferring visual attributes from a reference image onto a generated 3D asset. However, existing methods enforce an all-or-nothing paradigm: color and texture are transferred jointly, with no mechanism to control them independently -- a limitation we formalize as Disentangled 3D Stylization(Disen3D). To address this, we propose DiDE, the first training-free framework for Disen3D. Key to our approach is the observation that the structured latent space of image-to-3D models is overcomplete with respect to texture: texture information occupies only a small subset of the style-significant channels, leaving a free subspace available for independent color encoding. DiDE exploits this via a channel partition mechanism that processes a content image, a texture reference, and a color reference through dedicated branches and composes both style signals interference-free at every self-attention layer, preserving content geometry throughout. Experiments on Disen3D-Bench, our newly collected multi-reference benchmark, show that DiDE consistently outperforms 2D and 3D stylization baselines in color fidelity, texture transfer, and content preservation.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Reason with Compressed Context: Ground-Truth-Free Adaptation of OmniLLMs via Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39953)**  `arXiv:2609.39953`  
  _Jianghao Wang, Ke Meng, Jian Li, Chi Cheng, Longyu Qi, Liyin Liang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal large language models (OmniLLMs) enable unified audio-video understanding, but their long multimodal token sequences make deployment computationally expensive. Token compression reduces this cost, yet aggressive compression often lowers accuracy. Existing works predominantly focus on designing better compression mechanisms; however, adapting the underlying language model to reason effectively over the remaining compressed context remains under-explored. To address this, we propose CAFD (Compressed-Context Adaptation via Full-Context Distillation), a ground-truth-free self-distillation framework that adapts OmniLLMs to fixed compression pipelines without requiring reference answers, rationales, or correctness rewards. CAFD leverages the full-token view of the same multimodal sample as a source of privileged information: a full-context self-teacher provides soft target supervision to a compressed-context student along the student's on-policy trajectory. Evaluated on Qwen2.5-Omni-7B across five audio-video benchmarks, five compression pipelines, and five deployment budgets, CAFD demonstrates consistent gains, improving 120 out of 125 conditions with an average accuracy boost of 1.44 points and recovering 26.9% of the accuracy gap on average. These results demonstrate that the proposed ground-truth-free adaptation offers an effective and practical route to improving the accuracy-efficiency trade-off in deployed OmniLLMs.
  </details>

- **[BiMoGen: Bidirectional Motion-Text Generation via Unified Masked Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35407)**  `arXiv:2609.35407`  
  _Wanjiang Weng, Yongliang Wu, Xiaofeng Tan, Xingyu Zhu, Wenbo Zhu, Hongsong Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-motion generation and motion-to-text captioning are two fundamental tasks in human motion modeling, both grounded in the same underlying motion-text correspondence. Existing unified approaches mostly rely on autoregressive modeling, which imposes a fixed generation order and is therefore poorly suited to the bidirectional dependencies between language and motion, allowing early prediction errors to persist as fixed context and degrade both temporal coherence and cross-modal consistency. Masked discrete diffusion, which models sequences through iterative bidirectional prediction, allows prediction from bidirectional context without a fixed generation order. We therefore propose BiMoGen (Bidirectional Motion-text Generation), a unified masked discrete diffusion framework for bidirectional motion-text modeling. To stabilize training, we design Decoupled Uni- and Cross-Modal Training, in which masked pretraining first establishes cross-modal correspondence on paired motion-text sequences, after which supervised fine-tuning specializes the model for bidirectional generation. Masked diffusion nonetheless faces a train-test mismatch, as the model is trained on clean ground-truth context yet encounters self-generated and potentially erroneous context at inference, with errors committed under heavily masked states propagating through subsequent steps. We further introduce Generation-Aware Self-Correction that exposes the model to its own predictions during training and uses correction passes at early sampling steps for further refinement of previously unmasked tokens. Extensive experiments on HumanML3D and KIT-ML demonstrate competitive performance on both tasks, validating the effectiveness of the proposed two-stage training and self-correction designs. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EyeVQA: Benchmarking Ophthalmic Vision-Language Models from Recognition to Spatial Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32352)**  `arXiv:2609.32352`  
  _Gujie Shao, Zixun Xie, Xuechun Xing, Ruixiang Wang, Ziyun Lan, Yanlin Qi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown increasing potential for medical image understanding, yet their capabilities in ophthalmic imaging remain insufficiently characterized. Existing ophthalmic datasets are typically designed for individual diseases or specialized tasks, making it difficult to systematically evaluate whether VLMs can move beyond disease recognition toward comparative reasoning and fine-grained spatial grounding. We introduce EyeVQA, a unified visual question answering benchmark for comprehensive evaluation of ophthalmic VLMs. EyeVQA is constructed from 21 available ophthalmic datasets and contains 20,000 question-answer pairs spanning six disease groups and seven question types: Single-Choice, Multi-Select, Variable-Select, True-False, Ranking, Point Location, and Bounding Box. Gold answers are deterministically derived from source-provided diagnoses, severity grades, clinical findings, segmentation masks, bounding boxes, and anatomical landmarks, enabling reproducible evaluation without relying on model-generated annotations. Notably, 44.5% of the questions require reasoning across multiple images, extending evaluation beyond conventional single-image medical VQA. We benchmark fourteen representative general-purpose, scientific, and medically specialized VLMs under a unified zero-shot protocol. The best-performing model only achieves an overall score of 62.8, while substantial gaps remain in spatial grounding and cross-task generalization. These results highlight the limitations of current VLMs in comprehensive ophthalmic visual understanding and establish EyeVQA as a diagnostic benchmark for developing more reliable and spatially grounded ophthalmic multimodal models. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Video Understanding: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31713)**  `arXiv:2609.31713`  
  _Xinyu Deng, Siwen Luo, Daochang Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) become capable of processing increasingly diverse modalities and longer temporal contexts, an emerging line of work is moving beyond fixed video-language inference toward agentic systems that actively decide what information to inspect, retain, verify, and act upon. This survey reviews video understanding agents: systems that use video as the primary information source and solve understanding tasks through adaptive state construction and action selection. We first formalize an agent loop for video understanding, then address a central question: why do agents matter for video understanding? To answer this, we organize the literature through a challenge-to-design taxonomy, linking context bottlenecks to hierarchical evidence memory, evidence sparsity to active evidence acquisition, temporal causality to state and process tracking, and multimodal ambiguity to role-specialized coordination. We further review state space paradigms, learning paradigms, supervision signals, benchmarks, and evaluation protocols. Finally, we identify open directions toward agentic-native temporal modeling and video-native agents. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AxonSynth: Domain-Randomized Synthetic Data for Zero-Shot 3D Axon Segmentation in Light-Sheet Microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31431)**  `arXiv:2609.31431`  
  _Edward Gaibor, Kyriaki-Margarita Bintsi, Chiara Mauri, Emine Özen, Carmen Luz Leiva Ureta, Zayneb Bellatif, et al._
