Consequently, the standard $O(1/t)$ product-policy gap and $O(\log T)$ pseudo-regret rates hold when the certificates are uniform.
  </details>

- **[Leveraging Time-Series Foundation Models for Financial Crime Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29667)**  `arXiv:2610.29667`  
  _Danae Natalie Savvidi, H. Çağrı Bilgi, Kubilay Atasu, Arie van Deursen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial crime detection requires identifying illicit activity within large transaction networks, where suspicious behaviour is rare and unfolds over time. Existing graph-based methods capture relationships among accounts but typically underuse the temporal behaviour encoded in account transaction histories. We introduce Temporal-Context Edge Enrichment (TCEE), a framework that addresses this gap by summarising each account's transaction history with a frozen time-series foundation model (TSFM), and enriching each transaction's representation with the relevant temporal context from these summaries. These enriched representations can be scored directly for edge classification, or fed into a graph neural network (GNN) as edge features to capture broader structural information. Across six AMLWorld datasets, TCEE improves PR-AUC over the strongest evaluated baseline on five by a relative 10.8% on average, while training 6.0x faster end-to-end. Feeding TCEE's representations into a GNN raises PR-AUC a further 4.8% on the four smaller datasets. On an Ethereum phishing dataset, we show that TSFM-generated account representations fed into a GNN are also effective for node classification, improving PR-AUC by 14.5% over the strongest baseline. As TSFMs continue to advance, our results suggest they can strengthen financial crime detection by capturing temporal signals that complement graph-based approaches.
  </details>

- **[Reliability-Aware Probabilistic Fusion for Multimodal Learning with Incomplete Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29621)**  `arXiv:2610.29621`  
  _Patitapaban Palo, Abhisek Tiwari, Mattia Carletti, Anshul Thakur, Xiao Gu, David A. Clifton_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal inputs may be missing or degraded, raising distinct questions of evidence availability and trust. We introduce a reliability-aware Gaussian Product-of-Experts (PoE) in which an input-dependent reliability weight tempers each modality's latent precision, while missing experts are omitted. We characterize how reliability and availability affect posterior information and expert influence. Across two clinical multimodal tasks, the full framework remains competitive with complete inputs and achieves its clearest mean-AUROC advantage under modality absence, particularly on HAIM-MIMIC-MM. Ablations show a substantial gain from missing-aware training and an additional HAIM improvement from reliability weighting under matched PoE, missing-aware, and consistency training. Unseen test-time corruptions are used diagnostically and reveal modality-dependent reliability-weight and latent-uncertainty responses, including failure under severe degradation. Thus, reliability weighting provides an explicit evidence-control mechanism, while learning dependable quality signals remains an open challenge.
  </details>

- **[Local and Global Recursive Ridge Regression for Online Residual Correction of Frozen Multivariate Forecasters under Label Delay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29604)**  `arXiv:2610.29604`  
  _Takumi Fujimoto, Hiroaki Nishi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A frozen multi-horizon forecaster can be corrected online from its own residuals. However, under label delay the target of a block arrives only after the whole horizon has passed, and the corrector's retained state grows with the number of channels. Aiming to combine a small retained state with a large error reduction, we propose DRiC (DCT Ridge Corrector), which fits a ridge regression of the leading discrete cosine transform (DCT) coefficients of the next residual block on those of the frozen forecast and of the latest input block, with sufficient statistics updated recursively. Its local variant, DRiC-L, keeps one such regression per channel; its global variant, DRiC-G, pools all channels into one regression, whose statistics do not depend on the channel count. A guard tracks a running scale of the frozen forecast's residual for each channel; it skips updates on blocks far outside that scale and clips the issued correction. We evaluate on seven datasets (four ETT series, Weather with its missing-value codes filled, Electricity, and Traffic) with DLinear, PatchTST, and iTransformer backbones, against 10 naive, gradient-trained, and closed-form methods. DRiC-L with eight DCT coefficients (DRiC-L8) reduces the mean squared error (MSE) by 14.55% on average over 126 conditions, against 12.98% for ELF, the most accurate comparator, while retaining 15-17 times less state, although ELF is more accurate on Traffic. DRiC-G retains 14.6 kB on the 321-channel Electricity data and 18.9 kB on the 862-channel Traffic data and is the most accurate method at or below its state on both. However, 5 to 7 of the 10 comparators, all with at least 10 times its state, are more accurate there. On the unfilled Weather file, DRiC-L8 without the guard has on average 8.0 times the frozen forecast's MSE, whereas with the guard it reduces the MSE by 7.94%. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fast Bayesian Optimization Through Linear Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29580)**  `arXiv:2610.29580`  
  _Colin Doumont, Henry Moss, Philipp Hennig_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bayesian optimization (BO) is typically used only for problems where the underlying function is deemed "expensive" (e.g. in time or money), with the speed of the BO algorithm being of little importance. In the real world, however, problems span a continuous range of values, going from cheap to expensive. In this paper, we present BO algorithms through the lens of a speed-performance Pareto frontier: we compare BO against faster algorithms like CMA-ES, and highlight how certain regions of the Pareto frontier are currently empty. As a solution, we propose a new set of fast BO methods that exploit the linear structure in existing BO algorithms, for both continuous and combinatorial inputs (from ~10 to 6000+ dimensions). Our methods achieve performance similar to state-of-the-art BO, while being more than $10^2\times$ to $10^3\times$ faster, opening the field of BO to an entirely new class of problems.
  </details>

- **[The Reflexivity Threshold: A Phase Transition for Multi-Agent Performative Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29577)**  `arXiv:2610.29577`  
  _Ahmed Mahrous, Roberto Di Pietro_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many learning systems are performative: once deployed, their predictions change the data distribution on which future models are trained. In multi-agent settings, this feedback is routed through a network of interacting learners, so stability depends not only on the strength of performativity but also on who affects whom. We study repeated retraining in multi-agent performative prediction and identify a spectral threshold that separates stable learning from worst-case learning obstruction. Prior work often collapses cross-agent feedback into a scalar contraction constant, missing this network structure. We instead introduce the reflexivity matrix $\Gamma$, which records how strongly one agent's deployment can move another agent's best response, defined from per-agent gradient sensitivities and curvature parameters. The threshold occurs when the spectral radius of this matrix crosses one. Below the threshold, repeated retraining converges geometrically to a unique performatively stable equilibrium, with cumulative excess loss bounded uniformly in the time horizon. Above the threshold, we construct noisy supercritical instances in which every local-information learner incurs expected cumulative excess loss growing linearly for horizons up to order $1/(\rho(\Gamma)-1)$. Together, these results characterize the threshold behavior through upper and lower bounds and recover the single-agent scalar threshold from prior work as a special case.
  </details>

- **[Implicit Feature Reweighting for Label-Free Graph Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29571)**  `arXiv:2610.29571`  
  _Tianjun Yao, Chen Xi, Xinyang Li, Lixian Zhong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph-level out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization is critical for deploying graph neural networks in real-world applications where distribution shifts are inevitable. Existing approaches typically require class labels during representation learning to decide which substructures are predictive, limiting their applicability. We propose SCARCE, a label-free representation-learning framework that yields distribution-shift-robust graph representations from an environment partition of the training graphs alone, whether that partition comes from dataset metadata or, when none is available, from clustering a self-supervised embedding. Our method operates in two stages: (1) a variational graph autoencoder is warm-started for graph reconstruction; and (2) an adversarial confusion mechanism forces the encoder to produce representations from which a discriminator cannot distinguish environments, while preserving graph-reconstruction fidelity. We provide theoretical analysis showing that environment confusion contracts environment-discriminative directions in the representation space, which, under regularized downstream classifiers, is equivalent to imposing a stronger directional penalty on environment-associated features. This feature-reweighting mechanism reduces reliance on spurious correlations without erasing information, offering a label-free, continuous relaxation of last-layer reweighting methods. Experiments on a controlled synthetic datasets and the DrugOOD datasets show that SCARCE achieves state-of-the-art OOD generalization among label-free methods, using no class labels during representation learning.
  </details>

- **[Stochastic Pruning of the DPO Computation Graph: 1.4x Faster Training and Lower Peak Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29538)**  `arXiv:2610.29538`  
  _Yubing Wu, Wei Chen, Junmei Yang, Delu Zeng, Tiewen Pan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct preference optimization (DPO) incurs training costs in output scoring, sequence execution, and backpropagation, so reducing supervision alone does not necessarily save computation. We introduce a unified framework for stochastic pruning of the DPO computation graph that independently samples response positions for scoring, response prefixes for execution, and preference branches for backpropagation. By making these selections before the corresponding operations, the framework omits computation at each stage of a training step. Our analysis characterizes the objective changes induced by position and sequence pruning and uses inverse-probability weighting to obtain a conditionally unbiased single-branch gradient estimator for the sampled objective. Controlled measurements show that position and sequence pruning primarily reduce peak memory, with most output-memory savings attributable to an implementation that retains full response supervision. Branch pruning yields a $1.426\times$ step-time speedup over a concatenated DPO baseline and reduces training time by about 30\% relative to stock DPO across two model families, with slightly lower mean accuracy and benchmark-dependent changes in open-ended scores. These findings relate the resource benefits of stochastic graph pruning to the computation omitted and the resulting changes in the training objective.
  </details>

- **[Block Diffusion for Longitudinal EHR: Accuracy, Efficiency, and Representation Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29533)**  `arXiv:2610.29533`  
  _Ashvin Gupta, Joshua Placidi, Brendan Delaney, Alessandra Russo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive (AR) models have proved effective for zero-shot prediction from longitudinal electronic health records, but impose strictly sequential generation on trajectories containing temporally clustered and interdependent clinical events. We investigate block-based masked diffusion (BD) as an alternative on MIMIC-IV under matched tokenisation, context length, model capacity, and evaluation conditions. Across block sizes, BD exhibits a clear quality--efficiency trade-off, with small-block configurations retaining much of the predictive performance of AR while enabling substantially faster inference. A representative $B{=}8$ configuration achieves approximately $1.43\times$ faster evaluation. Although generative rollout remains below AR in most settings, frozen linear probes on BD and AR representations achieve near-identical performance across clinical prediction tasks, indicating that BD learns comparably informative clinical representations. Increasing the denoising budget yields diminishing returns, while alternative decoding strategies provide limited gains. Overall, our results show that block diffusion is a viable and computationally attractive approach to longitudinal EHR modelling, with further improvements in iterative decoding offering a promising route toward closing the remaining generative performance gap.
  </details>

- **[Are Joint-Embedding Predictive Objectives Effective for Longitudinal EHRs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29528)**  `arXiv:2610.29528`  
  _Ashvin Gupta, Brendan Delaney, Alessandra Russo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Masked language modelling (MLM) is the dominant pretraining objective for transformer encoders applied to longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs). However, reconstructing individual events may overemphasise noisy or weakly informative observations and does not explicitly encourage learning higher-level patient states. Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) instead learn by predicting representations in latent space and have shown strong performance in a variety of domains. In this paper, we investigate to what extent JEPA can be adapted to EHR sequence representation. Our empirical investigation using MIMIC-IV shows that although all three of our JEPA formulations train stably without representational collapse, they consistently underperform standard MLM. We find that target size, not target heterogeneity, increases prediction error, yet reducing the target size does not recover downstream performance. This suggests that stable latent prediction alone is insufficient for competitive EHR representation learning and highlights the challenge of defining clinically meaningful latent targets for EHR.
  </details>

- **[Shortcut-Resistant CAM Distillation for Long-Tailed Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29508)**  `arXiv:2610.29508`  
  _Wenhai Wan, Teng Zhang, Shao-Yuan Li, Xinrui Wang, Qiang-Sheng Hua, Songcan Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world datasets often follow a long-tailed distribution, making generalization to tail classes difficult. We revisit this problem through the lens of shortcut learning, where models prefer the easiest predictive cues (e.g., background or textures) over object-centric semantics, especially under scarce and biased supervision. We find that this tendency is amplified for tail classes: limited examples often share similar contexts, making non-semantic signals highly correlated and thus tempting shortcuts, whereas head classes with diverse appearances and environments encourage more stable object-focused representations. Motivated by this observation, we propose Shortcut-Resistant CAM Distillation (SRCD), a plug-and-play framework that transfers object-focused explanations from head to tail classes. SRCD operates in the Class Activation Map (CAM) space, where a CAM provides a class-specific spatial evidence map for a prediction. SRCD aggregates CAMs from a small set of head-class candidates into a shortcut-resistant teacher using an energy-model weighting based on coherence and concentration, and distills it to the tail-class CAM. We provide a theoretical analysis that quantifies shortcut reliance as shortcut-region evidence mass in CAM space and shows that SRCD suppresses tail shortcuts. Extensive experiments on long-tailed benchmarks consistently improve strong baselines. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Agentic Model Orchestration for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29504)**  `arXiv:2610.29504`  
  _Tong Guan, Tianxiang Zhan, Shirui Pan, Ming Jin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models (TSFMs) perform unevenly across forecasting tasks. Recent work uses agentic model orchestration (AMO) to select or combine TSFMs, often outperforming individual forecasters. Yet these gains may reflect stronger or more complementary candidates rather than better orchestration. To disentangle these effects, we introduce FairAMO, a controlled evaluation protocol with frozen candidate forecasts. Experiments on GIFT-Eval show that AMO improves accuracy, but its gains are bounded by the candidate TSFMs. The best orchestrators reduce MASE by less than 6% relative to the best single TSFM across all tasks. These gains depend on complementarity (different candidates excel on different tasks) rather than the number of candidates. Adding members yields diminishing returns, and a redundant member can erase these gains. The value of historical evidence also saturates quickly. A few recent windows capture all gains, and further history adds none. These findings separate system-level forecasting gains from the contribution of orchestration itself.
  </details>

- **[Asymmetry Tunneling Beyond Adaptive Equivariance Relaxation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29473)**  `arXiv:2610.29473`  
  _Eunho Jeong, Joon Jang, Hyeonjin Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Equivariant neural networks encode prescribed symmetries as powerful inductive biases, yet real-world data may exhibit symmetry-breaking structure that these priors cannot fully capture. Prior work has largely addressed this challenge through model-side equivariance relaxation, seeking an appropriate level of equivariance for the data. This raises a complementary question: must equivariance relaxation be adaptively controlled? To investigate this question, we introduce a residual path alongside a strictly equivariant model and deliberately restrict their interface to examine the transmission of task-relevant structure. Across five benchmarks, we observe both residual collapse and survival despite these restrictions; we call the latter phenomenon asymmetry tunneling. We further characterize this phenomenon through four complementary diagnostics. Our results suggest that adaptive control of equivariance relaxation is not a prerequisite for capturing potential symmetry-breaking structure.
  </details>

- **[Progressive Permutation Alignment with Temperature-Controlled Spectral Sharpening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29396)**  `arXiv:2610.29396`  
  _Yuta Horioka, Naoya Chiba, Yuki Uranishi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In implicit neural representations (INRs), 3D signals are encoded directly in network weights, making stable weight-space operations essential. However, permutation symmetry causes independently trained yet functionally equivalent models to appear distant in weight space, often inducing large loss barriers under linear interpolation. Existing neuron alignment methods either commit early to discrete matches and become trapped in inconsistent local optima or rely on computationally expensive gradient optimization over continuous relaxations, both of which are unreliable in challenging cases such as NeRF. We adopt progressive alignment, a layer-wise strategy that follows inter-layer dependencies from input to output. At each layer, a correlation matrix is estimated and aligned with a discrete anchor obtained by the Hungarian algorithm, after which temperature-controlled spectral annealing gradually sharpens the correlation structure toward a permutation matrix. This update is deterministic and lightweight, avoiding the inner-loop gradient optimization required by continuous relaxation methods. A final refinement stage further optimizes the network layer-by-layer via weight matching, starting from the aligned solution. Experiments on MLPs, CNNs, and Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) show that the proposed method reliably recovers neuron correspondences and achieves stable alignment across architectures. In practical experiments aligning independently trained NeRF models, our method reduces the loss barriers under weight interpolation compared with existing approaches.
  </details>

- **[Nominate-then-Adjudicate: LLM-Assisted One-Pass and Per-Instance MIP Solver Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29391)**  `arXiv:2610.29391`  
  _Yifan Zhao, Qianyang Zhao, Xinyu Guan, Kai Wei, Yuming Deng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixed-integer programming solvers expose hundreds or even thousands of parameters whose best setting varies per instance, yet per-instance search is prohibitively expensive. We frame this as configuration for heterogeneous Big Data optimization workloads and propose a nominate-then-adjudicate pipeline built around two design choices. First, a fixed portfolio is sampled once and reused across instances, removing per-instance configuration search at inference; on the evaluated families, the resulting shortlist retains substantial oracle headroom, making selection within it the empirical focus. Second, the fixed 20-feature representation can map structurally different instances to the same vector, preventing a feature-only selector from distinguishing them even when they prefer different configurations. The foundation model receives no task-specific weight updates, and selection is one-pass: one model call and no solver calls at inference. The study represents structural Variety through distinct formulation families and decision Value through solver-performance outcomes; the one-call, zero-solve protocol specifies the decision interface and leaves end-to-end latency unmeasured. Among comparisons exceeding a 20% practical-difference threshold, the complete adjudicator records 538 wins and 185 losses against the top nominee, while its win rate against the default rises from 84.9% to 90.2%. Against per-instance configuration baselines under a matched one-pass protocol it leads the cluster-level methods and the best-performing evaluated per-instance baseline, ISAC+.
  </details>

- **[Parallelizable Probabilistic Latent Reasoning for Diverse Reasoning Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29385)**  `arXiv:2610.29385`  
  _Seokhyun Lee, Seongjun Lee, Changhee Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models relying on verbalized Chain-of-Thought (CoT) are constrained to token-by-token generation in the language space, where many tokens serve textual coherence rather than core reasoning. This entangles genuine differences in logical trajectories with superficial variations in verbalization. Moving reasoning into a compact, continuous latent space offers a promising alternative for efficiently representing and exploring diverse reasoning trajectories. However, current latent models remain largely deterministic, capturing only a single reasoning trajectory rather than modeling a distribution over multiple valid alternatives. While stochastic approaches parameterize distributions over latent reasoning steps, they frequently suffer from unstable optimization and limited empirical performance. Furthermore, their reliance on autoregressive latent prediction during training precludes effective parallelization, leading to prohibitively long training times. To overcome these bottlenecks, we propose Probabilistic Latent Reasoning Models (PLRM), a parallelizable variational framework for probabilistic latent reasoning. PLRM adopts a JEPA-style objective in which the posterior infers latent distributions in parallel from observed reasoning trajectories, while the prior learns to predict the subsequent latent distribution via teacher forcing. To model heterogeneous reasoning processes, we introduce a discrete strategy variable together with a mixture-based prior, allowing the model to preserve the Gaussian-mixture structure induced by multiple distinct reasoning trajectories rather than collapsing them into a single Gaussian. Across diverse benchmarks, PLRM achieves performance competitive with deterministic latent reasoning models on single-trajectory datasets while enabling efficient training and consistently outperforming competing stochastic baselines on multi-trajectory datasets.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking and Error Analysis of Deep Search and Deep Research LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29346)**  `arXiv:2610.29346`  
  _Zixuan Yu, Zhenheng Tang, Xiaowen Chu, Bo Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve into autonomous Deep Search and Deep Research Agents capable of executing long-horizon, multi-step tasks, evaluating their reliability becomes increasingly critical. However, existing evaluation paradigms predominantly focus on final task outcomes, leaving a significant gap in understanding the intermediate execution traces, where agents are highly susceptible to various errors. To address this critical gap, we introduce a comprehensive methodology and analytical framework designed specifically to diagnose errors in intermediate workflows. Building upon this framework, we establish a structured taxonomy that categorizes these vulnerabilities into two primary dimensions: System-Level Errors and Task-Level Errors. Through a systematic examination of step-by-step execution traces and agent-environment interactions, we reveal how these underlying errors affect overall performance. Finally, through empirical experiments and systematic analysis of the identified error types, we provide actionable design guidelines, aiming to enhance the robustness, reasoning capabilities, and reliability of Deep Search and Deep Research agents.
  </details>

- **[DISRL: Dual-Scale Incremental Semi-Supervised Reinforcement Learning for Dynamic Community Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29315)**  `arXiv:2610.29315`  
  _Tianming Zhang, Jinxu Wang, Yixuan Zhang, Zhengyi Yang, Chenyu Hou, Bin Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic local community detection tracks the evolving community of designated seed nodes across graph snapshots. However, it faces two critical challenges: temporal supervision uncertainty, where sparse and outdated labels fail to provide seed-specific guidance under topology changes, and unbounded error cascades, where sequential policy transfer accumulates exploration errors over long horizons. To address these challenges, we propose DISRL, a Dual-scale Incremental Semi-supervised Reinforcement Learning framework. DISRL introduces a supervision construction module that dynamically generates seed-specific guidance from sparse known communities, and a dual-scale community exploration module that stabilizes policy evolution by decoupling short-term adaptation within temporal windows from long-term parameter consolidation across windows. Furthermore, we construct a dynamic graph benchmark with diverse community evolution scenarios. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DISRL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines under various dynamic settings.
  </details>

- **[YingLan: A Multimodal Ocean Foundation Model for Unified Representation Learning from Heterogeneous Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29313)**  `arXiv:2610.29313`  
  _Sen Jia, Siqi Wei, Junyu Wang, Zijun Lv, Pan Liu, Meng Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal ocean modeling remains challenging due to substantial heterogeneity in data structures, physical semantics, and spatiotemporal characteristics across satellite imagery, gridded oceanographic fields, and buoy observations. Existing approaches largely focus on task-specific ocean modeling, while learning transferable representations from heterogeneous observations remains an open problem. To address this challenge, we propose YingLan, a multimodal ocean foundation model that integrates physics-attribute-conditioned dynamic encoding, Spatiotemporal Increment Coupling (STIC), and ocean-state evolution-aware pretraining. Specifically, the dynamic encoding mechanism generates adaptive input projections conditioned on physical semantics and observation characteristics, enabling flexible representation of heterogeneous ocean variables. STIC models multiscale spatiotemporal dependencies through cross-scale interactions of latent temporal increments. The pretraining strategy further combines masked observation reconstruction with temporal evolution objectives to facilitate the learning of coherent ocean-state representations across different prediction horizons. YingLan is implemented using MindSpore and trained on Huawei Ascend 910B NPUs, demonstrating its compatibility with domestic AI computing infrastructure. In a seven-day sea surface salinity (SSS) forecasting task conducted in the Shenzhen coastal waters, YingLan achieves an average RMSE of 0.687508 PSU, corresponding to relative error reductions of 0.58%, 5.31%, and 12.42% compared with LangYa, XiHe, and GloNet2, respectively. These results provide initial evidence of the model's effectiveness in regional ocean forecasting, while broader cross-task and cross-regional evaluations remain necessary to assess its generalization capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Flat-RLVR: Sharpness-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards for Reasoning Capacity in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29281)**  `arXiv:2610.29281`  
  _Yeonbum Kim, Hyun Kyu Lee, Sung Whan Yoon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), particularly through group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), has emerged as a central paradigm for the post-training of large language models (LLMs). While RLVR is highly effective at enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs, it commonly induces \textit{distribution sharpening}, whereby the policy distribution becomes excessively peaked and rare-yet-correct trajectories are progressively suppressed, thus diminishing sample diversity. To this end, we promote flat-reward optima in RLVR to smooth the policy distribution. We theoretically link flat reward RLVR to reduced distribution sharpening, and empirically show improved sample diversity while maintaining single-attempt accuracy. Our method, called \texttt{Flat}-RLVR, searches for flat reward regions via sharpness-aware minimization during LLM post-training, combining this with GRPO variants to achieve better trade-offs between sampling efficiency and reasoning capacity.
  </details>

- **[Estimating Performance for Safe Deployment of Machine Learning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29262)**  `arXiv:2610.29262`  
  _Zeju Li, Konstantinos Kamnitsas, Mobarakol Islam, Melanie Roschewitz, Cheng Ouyang, Chen Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The main challenge in applying deep neural networks to real-world scenarios lies in distributional discrepancies between training and test datasets, often caused by variations in data acquisition conditions. Assessing the performance of pre-trained models without labels is crucial for safety, but this is complicated by deep models' overly optimistic probabilistic outputs, often exacerbated by class imbalances, leading to unreliable predictions. To address these issues, we introduce MOVAL, a novel MOdel eVALuation tool that estimates performance using unlabeled test data. MOVAL incorporates class-wise confidence calibration to adjust model outputs, allowing for the estimation of metrics beyond accuracy and mitigating the detrimental effects of class imbalance on confidence calibration. Additionally, we propose an ensemble approach that combines multiple calibrated confidence scores for a more robust solution. We validate MOVAL on natural and medical image datasets with various acquisition shifts, covering tasks from classification to segmentation. It achieves state-of-the-art results across evaluation metrics such as accuracy, F1-Score, AUC, and more. Furthermore, we demonstrate that MOVAL provides accurate performance estimations at the class, instance, and pixel levels, ensuring the transparent and safe deployment of machine learning models. We provide MOVAL as a deployment tool for monitoring and estimating performance under a range of domain shifts.
  </details>

- **[Delay-Guided Attention for Quality Prediction in Multistage Manufacturing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29220)**  `arXiv:2610.29220`  
  _Jiarui Zhang, Zhaoyang Guan, Zhixuan Xia, Naichen Shi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quality prediction in multistage manufacturing is challenging when inter-stage lags vary over time, as measurements taken at the same timestamp may reflect different process states. Existing approaches often preserve production order but fuse timestamp-aligned features or model delayed dependencies implicitly, without identifying the upstream history relevant to each downstream observation. We propose Delay-Guided Attention for Multi-stage Forecasting (DAMF), which explicitly estimates inter-stage lag distributions and uses them to guide cross-stage information transfer for final quality prediction. For each adjacent stage pair, a lag predictor compares the current downstream representation with candidate lagged upstream representations. An occurrence-magnitude factorization separately estimates the probability of a positive lag and its conditional magnitude, yielding a sample- and stage-pair-specific distribution over discrete lag states without requiring plant-specified delays at inference. DelayAlignment incorporates this soft distribution as a temporal prior into differentiable cross-stage attention, while gated residual fusion regulates information propagation across production stages. For interpretation, segment-wise Viterbi decoding converts pointwise lag distributions into temporally coherent lag paths, whereas forecasting retains the soft distributions for alignment. On the real-world LiquidSugar dataset, DAMF improves final quality prediction over the compared baselines. Evaluations on two controlled-delay benchmarks show more accurate and stable lag recovery, and ablation studies support the contributions of lag prediction, delay-guided attention, and structured temporal decoding.
  </details>

- **[PerSPL: Personalized Semi-supervised Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29219)**  `arXiv:2610.29219`  
  _Xiaoan Xu, Tingzhou Wei, Juncheng Dong, Korel Gundem, Ethan X. Fang, Zhengling Qi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized reward models require user-specific preference comparisons, but collecting such feedback is costly and provides only limited supervision for each user. At the same time, large pools of responses can be generated without user identities or preference labels, yet existing personalized reward models leave this data unused. Directly learning from these responses is difficult because pairwise objectives constrain relative scores within a prompt, whereas pseudo-labeling individual responses requires scores that are comparable across prompts for the same user. We propose \textbf{PerSPL} (Personalized Semi-supervised Preference Learning), a backbone-agnostic framework that uses pair-mean score centering to stabilize this cross-prompt score coordinate and iteratively turns unlabeled responses into user-specific training signals. Against seven representative baselines across four benchmarks---PersonalLLM, HelpSteer2, Reddit TLDR, and Chatbot Arena---PerSPL achieves the highest reported mean accuracy among the evaluated methods for both seen and unseen users. The two latter benchmarks provide real-user preference comparisons in summarization and open-domain chatbot interaction, respectively. These results indicate that unlabeled responses can provide additional supervision for personalized reward modeling when per-user preference labels are scarce.
  </details>

- **[Truncated Out-of-Bag Probability Gain: A Noise-Robust Criterion for Random-Forest Wrapper Feature Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29195)**  `arXiv:2610.29195`  
  _Sejong Oh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Random-Forest wrapper feature selection can use out-of-bag (OOB) predictions as a built-in validation signal, but the criteria that decide each forward step accumulate OOB estimation noise. We propose TOP-Gain (Truncated Out-of-bag Probability Gain), which scores a candidate feature by the mean sample-level increase in the OOB probability of the true class, with decreases truncated at zero. Under a simple noise model, truncation removes about two-thirds of the noise variance contributed by unaffected samples while preserving the signal, and it makes the ranking of candidate features markedly more reproducible. In a pre-registered benchmark on 26 OpenML-CC18 datasets, TOP-Gain achieved the best mean rank in area under the ROC curve (ROC-AUC), accuracy and Macro-F1 among 15 methods; it was significantly better than 13 of 14 baselines and matched the accuracy of VSURF with 21 times less single-core computation. Within an identical wrapper, it selected significantly more stable feature sets than net-gain, Brier, log-loss and accuracy criteria without loss of accuracy. A confirmatory study on 24 further datasets replicated the advantage over all baselines. The accuracy gains were broad rather than tied to specific dataset characteristics, and, as pre-registered, the stability gain grew as relevant features became sparser
  </details>

- **[Conditional Flow Models Convert Epistemic Uncertainty into Apparent Aleatoric Variance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29172)**  `arXiv:2610.29172`  
  _Navodita Sharma, Abhishek Shetty, Max Simchowitz, Pulkit Agrawal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conditional Flow Models (CFMs) are increasingly being used in place of deterministic predictors across machine learning, including for tasks that were classically formulated as regression. Given their success in such tasks, we ask the question: what useful information do the conditional distributions produced by CFMs carry beyond what a point predictor would provide?We identify a systematic phenomenon: in many natural regimes, CFM samples routinely have larger variance than the true conditional distribution, even in nearly deterministic regression problems. This effect is most pronounced where the model has not resolved the dependence on the conditioning variable, and thus the additional variance can be viewed as converting epistemic uncertainty in fitting the ground truth conditional distribution into apparent aleatoric variance in the generated samples. Through controlled synthetic and real-data experiments, and comparisons with explicit smoothing estimators, we show that the induced variance tracks model underfitting of complex or hard-to-fit targets, and decreases with additional training or data. We provide a theoretical account of the phenomenon in a simplified setting and demonstrate a consequent practical use-case of conditional flow-matching: designing high-coverage predictive sets that cover the ground-truth regression target.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Reward Hacking in Realistic Scenarios Through Representation Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29129)**  `arXiv:2610.29129`  
  _Austin Meek, Kyle Cox, Matthew Nguyen, Austin J. Brockmeier, Steven Basart_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward hacking is a major problem in current production machine learning systems, resulting in costly and dangerous failures. Here we study how to monitor for and mitigate reward hacking in realistic settings, including tool calls and long contexts. We apply a dynamic intervention to steering directions obtained by three different methods: difference-in-means vectors from labeled rollouts, gradient optimization-based vectors, and vectors extracted when models reference an anti-reward-hacking model spec. This dynamic intervention perturbs model representations less than uniform steering vector addition to the residual stream, allowing for more precise edits. We find that we are able to cheaply and significantly reduce reward hacking without correspondingly significant costs to model coherence or capability, and that steering vectors originally extracted from coding environments also generalize to reducing reward hacking in other domains. Our results pave the way towards latent space monitoring and control over realistic production systems.
  </details>

- **[Value-Rectified Distillation for Flow-based Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29075)**  `arXiv:2610.29075`  
  _Ke Jiang, Wen Jiang, Yoshinobu Kawahara, Xiaoyang Tan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We aim to address the problem of Out-of-Distribution (OOD) Mode Averaging, a critical problem that emerges when distilling Flow Matching (FM) based behavior policies in offline Reinforcement Learning (RL): When learning over multi-modal datasets, standard distillation inadvertently forces the policy to interpolate across different behavioral modes, causing the generated actions to collapse into invalid, OOD regions. To resolve this, we propose Value-Rectified Distillation (VRD), a novel framework that reformulates offline policy distillation as a value-guided generative trajectory alignment problem. By employing a dynamic coupling mechanism, VRD actively rectifies flow dynamics to explicitly decouple distinct behavioral modes, cleanly isolating high-value actions. Specifically, we instantiate VRD through two complementary algorithms: VRD-Q, which utilizes value-quantile behavior distillation to statistically filter out low-value behaviors, and VRD-R, which reorganizes the latent space to geometrically isolate high-value generative trajectories from the FM-based behavior policy. Extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate that VRD improves existing generative baselines across diverse continuous control and planning tasks on the D4RL, OGBench, and Minari benchmarks, especially over those multi-modal, non-expert datasets.
  </details>

- **[The Price of Choice in Selective Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29067)**  `arXiv:2610.29067`  
  _Daniel Cieślak, Andrzej Czyżewski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A selective classifier may be claimed to satisfy a risk-coverage contract if some rule from a predeclared menu (a threshold or a union of $K$ score intervals) attains coverage at least $\kappa$ and error at most $\alpha$. Refuting this existential claim requires ruling out every permitted rule. The intersection-union test (IUT) achieves validity without a multiplicity correction, but its sample cost can increase with menu richness. We characterize this cost and compare it with the optimal audit. For a fixed interior risk target $\alpha$ and gap $\gamma$, the minimax sample size for K-interval menus at coverage floor $\kappa$ is $K(1-\kappa)/(\kappa\gamma^2)$, up to logarithmic factors, in the stated small-gap, large-menu regime; exact IUT attains this rate. The lower bound holds even with a known score distribution, while the upper bound extends to menus of VC dimension $d$. Procedures that refuse to reject whenever any rule appears feasible on the audit sample can pay a linear $K$ cost even when a square-root rate is possible. Reference-free testing reaches a smaller scale under constant-risk alternatives, whereas unknown heterogeneous risks can make the linear cost unavoidable. In simulations with $K=4$, the raw menu cost varies 7.7-fold between $\kappa=.5$ and $\kappa=.95$; after theorem-motivated normalization the endpoint values differ by approximately 5%. Held-out experiments with three VLM judges show positive exact-hiding windows in all 60 split-specific confidence-gate families, with 28/30 contracts chosen on audit-A remaining hidden on untouched audit-B. On a cleaner multi-annotator target, hiding is weaker and transfers in 3/10 resplits. Frozen image and multimodal classifiers show boundary behavior consistent with the IUT guarantee and strong power relative to practical certificates.
  </details>

- **[Distilling Multi-Teacher Scoring Principles for Unsupervised Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29063)**  `arXiv:2610.29063`  
  _Dongchan Cho, Jiho Han, Juwon Hwang, Changhee Min, Minsang Kim, Namsoon Jung_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series anomalies take diverse forms, and individual detectors encode different anomaly scoring principles that rarely cover all anomaly types. Recent unsupervised time-series anomaly detectors provide complementary scoring signals, yet deploying them as an ensemble preserves the inference and maintenance cost of every individual detector. We study whether heterogeneous teacher detectors can be distilled into a single lightweight detector in the unsupervised setting, where no anomaly labels are available to define a shared target. AnoMix addresses this by extracting weak pairwise supervision from the training-set anomaly scores of frozen teachers. It normalizes each teacher score sequence independently and trains the student with CoRank, a multi-teacher ranking-distillation objective that converts teacher score gaps into pairwise supervision through teacher consensus. Across seven benchmark datasets, AnoMix successfully integrates complementary teacher scoring principles into a single deployable model, improving over individual detectors and static score ensembles while using a compact student with under 80k parameters on the benchmark settings. A zero-shot variant further shows that the distilled ranking signal transfers to unseen datasets and can outperform large pretrained time-series foundation models for anomaly detection.
  </details>

- **[ART-MPG: Autoregressive R/T Manifold Path Guiding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29049)**  `arXiv:2610.29049`  
  _Zehao Zhao, Yanyang Yang, Xiaole Zhang, Xiyu Deng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Specular chains concentrate important transport in small regions of path space and the structure depends on chain topology. We introduce ART-MPG, a compact neural model that samples chain length, an autoregressive reflection/transmission (R/T) string, and a direction from a topology-conditioned four-component von Mises-Fisher (vMF) mixture. We extend the repeated trial weighting in Manifold Path Guiding (MPG) by conditioning retry sampling on both chain length and the sampled R/T string, accounting for its known mass outside the stochastic re-hit trials. For a fixed proposal and target chain, this preserves the ideal expectation while reducing the expected retry count and reciprocal-estimator variance compared with retries conditioned on chain length alone. We distinguish the formal unbiasedness result from the capped and clamped renderer used in experiments. We evaluate fourteen scenes across a range of samples per pixel (SPP) against existing online and offline rendering algorithms. Our method consistently produces lower mean squared error at equal SPP than the online and offline baselines, with lower standard deviation across runs.
  </details>

- **[Better Policies, Worse Verifier Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29029)**  `arXiv:2610.29029`  
  _Daniel Cieślak, Jacek Łubiński_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verifier stacks increasingly sit inside post-training and test-time compute loops. We study a specific control loop: a component verifier is evaluated on fresh policy outputs against the full success label and refreshed when that aggregate score falls. For a conjunctive target $y = F \wedge M$, its on-policy AUC is $(1-q)A_{\mathrm{own}} + qA_{\mathrm{cross}}$, where $q = \Pr[F=1 \mid y=0]$. As the policy masters $F$, $q$ can rise toward one, removing weight from $F$-failures and rewarding information about $M$. We call this on-policy audit collapse. We derive a sharp identified set, a complete-erasure threshold, and the exact point at which the audit can prefer a worse component verifier; its population optimum is the full-outcome predictor, not the component oracle. An exact oracle exhibits the collapse, a natural multi-label text control shows inversion can select a head with lower canonical $F$-AUC at the observed failure mixture, and a prediction-registered ECG study shows that acting on the audit can reduce off-policy $F$-AUC, transfer the damage to an unseen generator, and increase downstream constraint violations. Preserving own-failure support prevents the damage. Matching label-complexity bounds and a controlled-mixture audit give the corresponding repair: audit the component on its own failures, and keep those failures in the refresh distribution.
  </details>

- **[Dissimilarity-Based Markov Decision Processes for Stabilizing State Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29024)**  `arXiv:2610.29024`  
  _Xun Shen, Yuepeng Wang, Daniyal Sajedi-Hosseini, Akifumi Wachi, Ken Kawano, Yoshihiko Fujisawa, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for stochastic systems typically optimizes expected cumulative rewards, while reliable and repeatable behavior also depends on how the state distribution evolves over time. Optimizing expected return alone does not guarantee that this distribution converges to a desired target or remains stable. This paper develops a theoretical framework for learning policies that stabilize state distributions. We formulate the problem as a Dissimilarity-based Markov Decision Process (DMDP), in which the policy acts on the current state distribution and the objective measures its dissimilarity to a target distribution. We establish sufficient conditions under which the optimal policy makes the target distribution asymptotically stable. To support policy learning, we introduce a finite-dimensional representation of distributions and show how stability guarantees for its parameter dynamics extend to the underlying state distributions. We further develop certificates for the learned policies, including their deployment using estimated distribution parameters. These results quantify how learning and estimation errors affect the guarantees and identify conditions under which learned policies retain asymptotic stability. For practical implementation, we combine distribution estimates and target-dependent rewards with PPO and SAC. A computable example on a linear system illustrates the proposed theoretical certificates. Furthermore, experiments on a HalfCheetah task demonstrate the benefits of distribution feedback and explicit target matching for regulating the velocity distribution.
  </details>

- **[Local and Sparse Kernel Operator Learning for Incompressible Flows: An Operator Lagrange Formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29009)**  `arXiv:2610.29009`  
  _Ramansh Sharma, Matthew Lowery, Houman Owhadi, Varun Shankar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a local and sparse kernel method for learning the solution operators of incompressible flows. It builds on our earlier property-preserving kernel method, whose global output reconstruction and dense operator regression scale poorly. Our method replaces the global output reconstruction by local divergence-free interpolants with divergence-free polynomials of any degree, solved in parallel on the GPU. These interpolants are divergence free, periodic on periodic domains, and, with a multiscale kernel, approach Kolmogorov statistics under refinement. A compactly supported kernel, positive semidefinite in any input dimension, makes the operator regression sparse. Its support radius, and hence the sparsity, is fitted to data, and optional affine polynomials in the input capture global trends. Operator Lagrange weights join the two. They cost one sparse solve per new input and act on the training fields before the output reconstruction, so their local coefficients are never formed. We show that, unlike direct evaluation, Lagrange evaluation does not amplify solve errors by the cancellation among spatial coefficients. On ten fluid benchmarks, validation selects sparse matrices nearly everywhere, with densities that mostly decrease as the training set grows, and affine augmentation lowers the error by more than an order of magnitude for forced isotropic turbulence. These sparse models are at least as accurate as our earlier dense method and more accurate than neural operators. Lagrange evaluation is as accurate as direct evaluation with local output reconstructions, and up to nearly three orders of magnitude more accurate with global ones. We also present GPU timings and storage.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Time Series Tokenization from a Frequency Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28997)**  `arXiv:2610.28997`  
  _Zelong Tian, Ming Jin, Bo Du, Shirui Pan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tokenization that partitions time series into subsequences has become a foundational paradigm in modern time series modeling. However, we find that current tokenization strategies inherently introduce spectral distortions, forcing the learned representations to diverge from the true underlying patterns such as trend, periodicity, and seasonality, thereby impairing model performance. Through theoretical analysis from a frequency perspective, we derive the boundary conditions under which such distortions occur. To overcome this distortion, we propose TimeFT, a nearly distortion-free frequency-based tokenizer for time series data. TimeFT obtains tokens through frequency-domain partitioning, frequency shifting, and Nyquist sampling. The method is parameter-free, incurs negligible computational complexity, and can serve as a drop-in replacement for existing tokenizers. Our extensive experiments on diverse tasks such as forecasting, classification, and anomaly detection demonstrate that TimeFT consistently and significantly improves performance.
  </details>

- **[Doob-Based Generative Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28990)**  `arXiv:2610.28990`  
  _Maojiang Su, Qijie Zhu, Yu Xiao, Shuyang Yu, Minshuo Chen, Zhaoran Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion and flow models provide expressive policy classes for robotic and sequential decision-making systems. However, their action likelihoods are generally intractable, limiting the direct use of widely adopted policy optimization methods such as PPO and GRPO. We propose Doob Policy Optimization (DoobPO), a framework that leverages Doob's $h$-transform for likelihood-free reinforcement learning fine-tuning of generative policies. DoobPO separates policy improvement from generative policy learning. It first learns a lightweight ratio model that represents the desired policy update, and then distills the resulting ratio-tilted target policy into the generative policy via conditional drift matching. This formulation applies uniformly to both diffusion and flow policies and supports a broad family of policy improvement rules, including PPO and GRPO. We prove that the practical conditional drift matching objective is gradient-equivalent to the ideal Doob drift matching objective. We further show that the induced diffusion process solves an endpoint-constrained stochastic optimal control problem. This establishes that it achieves the minimum-energy modification required to realize the desired policy tilt. Across MuJoCo continuous-control benchmarks, DoobPO achieves strong performance against competitive diffusion- and flow-policy baselines, showing a principled and practical route to RL fine-tuning for generative policies.
  </details>

- **[Attention-based Classification for Indirect Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28988)**  `arXiv:2610.28988`  
  _Amelia Kawasaki, Stefan Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indirect prompt injection (IPI) attacks, in which adversarial instructions are embedded in external data processed by a language model, pose a significant threat to the integrity of deployed LLM agents. Attention Tracker (Hung et al., 2025) is a recently proposed white-box detection method that identifies prompt injection attacks by monitoring how a model's attention is distributed between user task tokens and data tokens, claiming dataset-agnostic detection via a scalar focus score threshold. In this paper, we provide the first evaluation of Attention Tracker in a realistic agentic IPI setting using the AgentDojo benchmark, finding that the dataset agnosticism claim does not hold. We find that in agentic environments, long structured tool outputs subvert the fundamental assumptions Attention Tracker makes about attention that the method depends on and produces near-chance detection on AgentDojo across most model families. We propose AttnDA, an extension replacing the scalar threshold with a Fisher Linear Discriminant Analysis classifier over the per-head focus score vector, and evaluate both methods in a cross-dataset setting spanning four benchmarks. AttnDA improves detection performance across agentic and tool-integrated benchmarks, though transfer to AgentDojo remains challenging regardless of classifier choice. A covariance ablation further shows that removing inter-head correlation structure substantially contributes to performance, with complex covariance assumptions causing significant degradation on cross-domain data. These results indicate that the attentional distraction signal underlying focus-score-based detection does not transfer reliably to agentic environments, and that learned linear classifiers over head-level attention features offer improved detection performance, provided training data from the target deployment domain is available.
  </details>

- **[SSMCGM-Stream: A Streaming State-Space Model for Personalized Diabetes Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28976)**  `arXiv:2610.28976`  
  _Shakson Isaac, Chirag Patel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous glucose monitoring supports diabetes management but requires forecasting models that are accurate, interpretable, and efficient enough for real-time deployment. We present SSMCGM-Stream, a streaming state-space model that maintains a personalized hidden state updated with each incoming glucose reading to provide constant-time forecasting, physiologically structured counterfactual reasoning, and interpretable forecasts through hidden-state attribution. Built on this foundation, SSMCGM-Agent delivers risk-aware, natural-language decision support by translating forecasts, explanations, and counterfactuals into grounded recommendations through a guarded language-model interface. Evaluated on the T1DEXI cohort, SSMCGM-Stream achieves forecasting performance comparable to strong neural baselines while substantially reducing computational cost, enabling continuous on-device diabetes decision support.
  </details>

- **[How Does Orthogonalization Adapt to the Neural Network Hessian Structure? A Gradient Self Outer-Product Analysis at Initialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28975)**  `arXiv:2610.28975`  
  _Shenyang Deng, Garvit Goyal, Tianyu Pang, Shuhua Yu, Yaoqing Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon orthogonalizes a weight matrix's momentum before each step, and on neural networks this simple preconditioner beats AdamW in a wide range of tasks. Existing analyses explain Muon within general optimization frameworks or on a few specific models, and many of them view orthogonalization as a preconditioner. Since the Hessian is the optimal preconditioner under a quadratic approximation of the loss, these works leave a basic question open: what makes orthogonalization suited to the Hessian of a neural network? Prior work shows that the layer-wise Hessian of a neural network is row-block diagonally dominant, and Muon's implicit preconditioner, the Kronecker product $(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^\top)^{1/2}\otimes\mathbf{I}$ with $\mathbf{V}$ the momentum, has the same row-block structure exactly when $\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^\top$ is diagonally dominant. Diagonal dominance of $\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^\top$ has been observed in language models, but why a neural network produces it has not been explained. This work provides a quantitative asymptotic theory for neural networks at initialization, and points out the intuition that the overparameterization of the weight matrices drives the diagonal dominance. We analyze $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^\top]$, which equals $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^\top]$ at initialization, in four canonical models: symmetric matrix factorization, deep linear networks, two-layer ReLU networks, and single-layer linear attention. In each case the diagonal entries outgrow the off-diagonal ones at explicit rates as the hidden dimensions grow; for the query, key and value weights of attention the off-diagonal vanishes altogether. Simulations of the four models and experiments on language models at initialization confirm the predicted scaling.
  </details>

- **[From Weather to Enhanced Weathering: Weather Foundation Models for Carbon Dioxide Removal at Operational Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28973)**  `arXiv:2610.28973`  
  _Sasankh Munukutla, Felipe Oviedo, Danrong Zhang, Kenji Takeda, Juan M Lavista Ferres_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enhanced Rock Weathering (ERW) is a carbon removal solution with the potential to remove gigatons of CO$_2$ per year, but quantifying that removal today requires dense soil sampling and laboratory analysis. We evaluate five weather Foundation Models (FMs) as frozen encoders feeding lightweight decoders, under a leave-one-field-out protocol on 5,192 chronosequences from 264 fields across Brazil and the US. To our knowledge, this is the first holistic evaluation of weather FMs for ERW and the largest evaluation on operational ERW data. We find that across weather FMs, representations transfer to ERW: our best model, TerraNova-ERW, built on Aurora 1.5, positively orders measurements within fields it has never seen. Zero-shot prediction of a new field's mean removal is not yet feasible, and we attribute this to data scale: the same architecture excels on $\sim$$10^5$ natural-weathering sequences, anchoring the scale we expect ERW data must reach. Few-shot prediction, blending the model with a few samples per field, is supported today: TerraNova-ERW recovers over 60% of the highest-removal fields with two samples per field, roughly halves the samples needed to estimate a field's mean removal, and can cut sampling on our densest fields by 28-98%.
  </details>

- **[Online Open-World Wafer Defect Discovery using Hyperdimensional Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28949)**  `arXiv:2610.28949`  
  _Quanling Zhao, Ava Emami, Tsuyoshi Idé, Rajiv Joshi, Steven Holmes, Michael Passow, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional wafer defect analysis relies on offline, closed-world classifiers that fail to detect novel, unseen defect signatures emerging during continuous semiconductor manufacturing. To address this, we propose WaferHDC, an online open-world wafer defect discovery framework based on hyperdimensional computing (HDC). The memory-centric architecture of WaferHDC enables rapid anomaly detection and grouping of recurring failures into compact hypervector representations. This allows the memory space to dynamically scale with new defect modes without retraining a static classifier or archiving entire raw wafer history. WaferHDC first models normal spatial distributions, then dynamically groups recurring failures into persistent defect memory without labels or predefined class counts. It outperforms the strongest online baseline by 13.3 p.p. in adjusted Rand index and 10.5 p.p. in normalized mutual information on the industry-standard WM-811K dataset. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of WaferHDC for continuous and open-world wafer defect analysis.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Objective Protein Optimization via Modular Diffusion Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28943)**  `arXiv:2610.28943`  
  _Meir Roketlishvili, Felix Huber, Wenda Chu, Yisong Yue, Raul Astudillo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein engineering often requires improving several properties simultaneously, motivating the search for high-performing sequences that represent useful fitness tradeoffs. Discovering these sequences is challenging because the design space is combinatorially large and experimental campaigns often permit only a few rounds of batched measurements. We introduce \textit{MODS}, a modular framework for multi-objective protein optimization via inference-time diffusion steering. For each proposal, MODS samples augmented Chebyshev scalarization weights and a fitness-surrogate ensemble member to define a scalar steering reward. Varying the weights directs generation toward different objective tradeoffs, while varying ensemble members diversifies the predictions used for guidance. Because MODS expresses multi-objective acquisition through scalar rewards, it can be paired with different diffusion models and compatible steering procedures. After each evaluation batch, MODS updates the surrogates without retraining the generative model. We evaluate MODS on six protein optimization tasks using predictive oracles trained on experimental assay data, with two diffusion models and two steering procedures. MODS achieves competitive final hypervolume across all six tasks with both diffusion models, and substantially outperforms the best evaluated baseline on several tasks. Our results demonstrate a flexible and effective approach to multi-objective protein optimization with limited feedback.
  </details>

- **[The Data Differentiator Framework (DDF): Quantifying Marginal Data Value with Information-Theoretic and Game-Theoretic Measures in an Agentic Evaluation Pipeline (SDSAE)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28933)**  `arXiv:2610.28933`  
  _Sukesh Kumar, Dilip Singh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In data-rich enterprises the hard question is not whether a candidate dataset is good in isolation, but whether it adds information beyond what the organization already holds, and whether that increment is worth its price. We present the Data Differentiator Framework (DDF), a composite scoring method for candidate data assets that combines (i) a mutual-information-based redundancy penalty against the existing portfolio, (ii) a cost-normalized, significance-gated marginal utility score derived from an ablation study, and (iii) explicit governance inputs (trust, identity resolution quality, strategic weights) set by the business. The framework is executed by a staged pipeline, the Strategic Data Sourcing Agent Ecosystem (SDSAE), that short-circuits expensive model-retraining steps for assets failing governance or redundancy gates. Conceptually, the DDF extends the relevance-minus-redundancy principle of mRMR feature selection from the feature level to the asset level, and adds cost normalization, a statistical significance gate, and governance terms that per-sample valuation methods such as Data Shapley do not model. We validate the framework on the public credit-card fraud benchmark (284,807 transactions) with five constructed candidate assets spanning the redundancy spectrum. Only the genuinely differentiated asset passes the significance gate ($\Delta$AUC = +0.0029, DeLong $p = 1.4\times 10^{-9}$, Holm-corrected $p = 7.2\times 10^{-9}$) and receives an acquire recommendation (DDS = +0.663); the fully redundant asset scores lowest (DDS = -0.135). The recommendation is stable across 19 of 22 admissible strategic-weight configurations and across all decision thresholds in [0.15, 0.66]. We state clearly which quantities in the case study are computed from data and which are simulated governance inputs, and we discuss the design limits of a single-dataset evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Query Feedback and Teaching for Stable and Certifiable Language Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28918)**  `arXiv:2610.28918`  
  _Farnam Mansouri, Alireza F. Pour, Grigoris Velegkas, Sandra Zilles_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language generation in the limit studies whether a learner observing an enumeration of an unknown target language can eventually generate only valid, previously unseen elements. We study stability, introduced by Kalavasis, Mehrotra, and Velegkas (2025), which requires the generator's infinite output set to eventually remain unchanged, and introduce the stronger notion of certifiability, which requires the generator to determine from its observations that its current output is valid. Drawing on teaching sets in learning theory, we introduce a hierarchy of teaching dimensions for generation under three increasingly strict success criteria: correctness, stability, and certifiability. These dimensions measure the minimum number of selected examples whose appearance guarantees the corresponding success condition. We also study stability and certifiability in the membership-query model of Charikar and Pabbaraju (2025). We characterize stable and certifiable generation, both with and without query feedback, and establish their relationships with uniform generation and identification in the limit. In particular, we give a finite-witness characterization of stable generation, analogous to Angluin's tell-tale characterization of identification, resolving a gap left open by Kalavasis, Mehrotra, and Velegkas (2025). We further compare these generation notions with identification in the limit and contrast their teaching requirements on well-studied language families, including regular and erasing pattern languages.
  </details>

- **[Minimax-Optimal Predictive Flow Matching under Distribution Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28898)**  `arXiv:2610.28898`  
  _Aadithya Srikanth, Vaneet Aggarwal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow matching learns a generative velocity field by regression, but under distribution drift a field must be deployed before the current data arrive. We study this predictive problem for a fixed, possibly nonconvex, neural class, with dynamic regret against the best represented field of each round. We propose DyProd-FM, which refits flow matching on dyadic historical prefixes and selects among the fits with a sleeping Prod master. Without knowing any drift budget, it attains regret $\widetilde{O}(\min\{N_\omega+1,1+\sqrt{T\Omega}\})$ in the relative velocity risk variation $\Omega$ and $\widetilde{O}(\min\{N_\mu+1,1+T^{1/3}V_\mu^{2/3}\})$ in the target-law variation $V_\mu=\sum_t W_2(\mu_{t+1},\mu_t)$, up to approximation and fitting costs. Matching lower bounds for fixed classes show that both rates are optimal in $T$ and in the budgets up to logarithmic factors. Prior optimal dynamic-regret guarantees rely on a convex decision set with strongly convex or exp-concave losses, whereas our class may be nonconvex and no curvature in its parameters is assumed. The target-law rate rests on a second-order link between the two variations, which the base noise of flow matching provides.
  </details>

- **[Combating Catastrophic Forgetting in Continual Domain Adaptation via Knowledge Recasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28892)**  `arXiv:2610.28892`  
  _Zijie Liu, Zhen Tan, Charles Fleming, Tianlong Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting language models to new domains during post-training risks degrading previously acquired capabilities, a phenomenon commonly known as catastrophic forgetting. Existing mitigation methods largely treat forgetting as a consequence of excessive model drift. They therefore constrain updates by preserving old outputs, replaying old data, or regularizing changes to parameters and representations so that the updated model remains close to its previous state. We take a different view: Rather than pulling the updated model back toward its old state, we translate the new domain into the model's pre-existing knowledge space, making unfamiliar knowledge learnable through familiar procedures. Therefore, we propose Skill Schema Transport (SST), a post-training framework that transports new knowledge into the model's existing skill space. SST abstracts each example type into a skill schema, preserving its essential operations, dependencies, and verification steps while removing domain-specific surface forms. By recasting new examples as instances of reusable procedures, SST turns knowledge update from trajectory memorization into procedural reuse. In continual domain adaptation settings, SST consistently reduces forgetting while improving generalization to related tasks. Across two 3B instruction-tuned backbones, SST improves average final performance on streamed domains by 2.53 to 5.75 points over sequential post-training. It also lowers max-drop forgetting in all evaluated settings, with the largest reduction from 42.41 to 7.10 points on the Qwen-3B MATH stream, while substantially recovering the OOD degradation induced by sequential updates.
  </details>

- **[RL-IF: Tracking Non-local Training Data Influence for Online Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28883)**  `arXiv:2610.28883`  
  _Shixuan Liu, Cheng Tang, Yuzheng Hu, Fan Wu, Han Zhao, Jiaqi W. Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Online reinforcement learning (RL) has achieved significant success in decision-making tasks, but modern online RL remains highly sensitive to the quality of training experience. Understanding the contribution of individual training instances is therefore central to data-centric RL, with applications ranging from experience selection to policy interpretation. Existing data attribution methods for online RL primarily focus on local influence within a single training round, overlooking the non-local data influence across multiple training rounds. In this work, we formalize data attribution in online RL through a trajectory-level leave-one-out quantity, RL-LOO, which measures how one training sample influences a downstream target after subsequent online updates have taken place. We then derive RL-IF, a first-order estimator that propagates data influence through both optimization effects and policy-induced sampling effects, and provide theoretical guarantees on its approximation accuracy under smoothness assumptions. Our analysis further disentangles these two effects and shows both theoretically and empirically that the full RL-IF estimator can often be well approximated using the optimization effect alone. Building on this observation, we develop RINSE, a practical data filtering method for online RL. Experiments on standard control benchmarks and an RLHF-style toxicity-mitigation setting show that RINSE improves training efficiency and final performance over standard PPO and local attribution baselines. These results suggest that RL-IF provides a practical and principled framework for understanding and improving online RL.
  </details>

- **[How Much Gradient Does Truncated Backprop Lose in Looped Transformers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28871)**  `arXiv:2610.28871`  
  _Aur Shalev Merin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped transformers reuse one block of layers many times and are often trained by backpropagating through only the last k loops. We measure how much of the weight gradient this throws away, exactly and for every k from one backward pass, on nine looped language models, and report three findings. First, on a trained model this lost gradient is set near the output: once the loop count is well above k, it barely changes with the loop count (within about 0.03 from 32 to 64 loops on the three models we tested, as a pre-registered test predicted for Parcae). Where the backward signal shrinks, $\rho^k$, with $\rho$ the signal's fitted shrink per loop, approximates it at moderate loop counts but overstates it at long ones, because the fitted $\rho$ drifts up as the fit takes in more loops. On Ouro the signal grows, and keeping two of four loops still loses 81% of the final output's gradient. Second, the lost gradient falls during fine-tuning, by 0.07-0.22 within 300 steps on three of four models, on held-out problems too. The fall is not just the read-out's share of the gradient; training only the layers outside the loop reproduced up to half of it, and it also falls under truncated training. So a trained model's value overstates what truncation removes later. Third, we could not show that it predicts the training cost of truncation better than rules that need no measurement: on two new models it tied Parcae's cost at the same k, in a test too small to separate them.
  </details>

- **[A General Topological Symmetry Index for Critical Orbits In Neural Network Loss Landscapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28864)**  `arXiv:2610.28864`  
  _Jingzhou Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A systematic understanding of the global structure of neural-network loss landscapes remains challenging due to their high dimensionality. Symmetries arising from network architectures and learning objectives provide a way to investigate aspects of this structure. Although a growing number of studies have drawn attention to these symmetries, a systematic characterization of them, as well as of the critical orbits they give rise to, remains incomplete. To address this gap, in this work we establish a topological symmetry index, based on the methodologies from the family of equivariant degree theories, to decode these symmetries. The frameworks apply to arbitrary network architectures and include methods applicable to degenerate critical orbits. The index detects the isotropy of the critical points and quantifies the corresponding net topological contributions. Several examples, including softplus MLPs, Transformers, and equivariant neural networks, are presented to illustrate the computations.
  </details>

- **[When Do Gaussian Priors Fail? A Latent Geometry Investigation of RL Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28849)**  `arXiv:2610.28849`  
  _Wang Ru_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent diffusion models almost universally place a Gaussian prior on the VAE latent space. For base generation this default is unproblematic; under reinforcement-learning fine-tuning, however, it carries a structural limitation. The standard VAE's KL term does not merely ignore mode structure - it actively taxes separation and tightness. When data are multimodal, the ELBO optimum compresses distinct modes into an overlapping latent. RL fine-tuning amplifies this overlap: the score network is under-resolved on the saddle region between modes, dynamic-to-variational amplification rises sharply, and the collapse boundary beta_c is pushed far above its variational scale. On the real alanine-dipeptide Boltzmann distribution, a Gaussian-prior VAE collapses at beta_c = 0.224 while a mode-tracked Gaussian-mixture prior collapses at 0.00316, a 71x gap from the same pipeline.We trace this gap to a single geometric mediator, d_min/(2 sigma_0), and verify the causal chain through controlled interventions: moving geometry at fixed prior, and switching prior at matched geometry (full mediation; prior-label coefficient 0.995x, 95% CI [0.79, 1.25]). A three-source decomposition localises the amplification to the learned policy (zero ODE-solver contribution, bounded anchor mismatch); the coverage plateau is an unconditional benefit of mode-separating priors that no amount of RL capacity recovers.
