The remedy is conditional: a non-Gaussian prior is necessary precisely when the latent geometry would fall into overlap and the task is coverage-sensitive. A machine-checked theory skeleton supports the KL pricing structure - a divergent compactness tax under a Gaussian prior versus a finite assignment fee under a mixture - and the position-versus-plateau distinction delimits what is finite-resource from what requires changing the prior.
  </details>

- **[A Mechanistic Study of Automated Failure Attribution in LLM-based Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28811)**  `arXiv:2610.28811`  
  _Wendy Zheng, Liang Wu, Yinhan He, Mayank Darbari, Jundong Li, Liangjie Hong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic systems have demonstrated strong capabilities on complex tasks by enabling multiple interacting large language model (LLM)-based agents to collaborate and execute subtasks in parallel. However, this collaborative structure introduces new challenges for reliably evaluating and diagnosing system failures, particularly failure attribution: tracing a system's execution to identify the agent step responsible for an incorrect final outcome. Because manually performing this task is time- and labor-intensive, recent work has turned to LLMs as judges to automate the process, referred to as automated failure attribution (AFA). These approaches assume that a sufficiently capable model can infer the failing step directly from an interaction transcript. In practice, however, direct prompting often performs poorly, which motivates follow-up work that explores various ways to improve AFA performance, including alternative prompting strategies and inference-time interventions. In this paper, we examine a more fundamental question: whether this weak behavioral performance reflects a genuine capability limitation. Using linear probes applied to internal activations, we uncover a significant \emph{knows-but-won't-say gap:} models encode sufficient information to attribute a failure correctly but fail to explicitly output the responsible step. Building on this finding, we propose \emph{grafting}, a simple manual intervention that exploits knowledge already encoded in the model to improve AFA performance without requiring ground-truth labels at inference time. Extensive evaluations across multiple LLMs and benchmark settings demonstrate that grafting consistently outperforms direct-prompting approaches, underscoring the potential of leveraging latent model knowledge for more reliable failure attribution. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Realistic Models of Change: A Causal Perspective on Decision-Making and Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28803)**  `arXiv:2610.28803`  
  _Isacco Beretta_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While statistical learning has made remarkable progress in recent decades, driven by large datasets and data-driven protocols that have transformed scientific practice, it still faces a key limitation: it struggles to generalize beyond the data it has seen, often failing under distribution shift. Causality offers a promising solution: causal models focus on the underlying mechanisms of change rather than surface-level associations, enabling robust reasoning under external interventions. This allows systems not only to learn from data, but also to reason about hypothetical scenarios, adapt to new environments, and inform better decisions. This thesis explores how these approaches can inform each other, in two parts.The first part examines algorithmic recourse, that is, identifying which changes an individual can make to overturn an unfavorable decision. We propose an alternative perspective to the established framework, arguing for a more realistic cost function, a reformulation of the problem in causal terms, and the inclusion of a temporal dimension in policy evaluation.The second part focuses on dynamical systems, extending the notions of causal interventions and effects to discrete and continuous stochastic processes. We show that the equilibria of these processes, and how they change following an intervention, can be linked to Structural Causal Models (SCMs), viewed as compressed representations of the underlying temporal dynamics. We argue for the relevance of such temporal causal models, since in real-world scenarios actions take time to produce their effects.The common goal of both parts is practical: the utility, robustness, and trustworthiness of these models depend critically on their ability to realistically represent the underlying phenomena.
  </details>

- **[An Explicit Feedback Contract for Iterative ML-Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28798)**  `arXiv:2610.28798`  
  _Kehui Yao, Chuanbo Hu, Jiao Chen, Ramin Giahi, Reza Yousefi Maragheh, Lalitesh Morishetti, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Iterative ML agents develop models by editing code, running experiments, reading a validation score, and choosing what to try next. In current harnesses this loop is served by an exact validation score returned after every attempt, which leaves the validation split open to adaptive selection. In this work, we introduce an explicit feedback contract for ML-agent harnesses, which specifies what an agent may observe, when iteration should stop, and when the test split may be touched. The contract releases a validation score only when a submission clears a noise threshold computed from the paired standard error, so no margin has to be tuned. We ran experiments on click-through-rate prediction, learning to rank, and a control task with no signal to find. The control is the sharpest case, because the true gain there is zero by construction: an agent reading exact scores ended by reporting a gain of 0.0157, while under the contract it reported 0.0033. On both real tasks the contract narrows the validation-test gap, and test performance is slightly higher rather than lower. The largest difference is in when the agents stop. Exact feedback used its entire budget of thirty submissions on two of the three tasks, including the one where nothing can be learned, while the contract halted after four to twelve. A run that exhausts its budget is usually read as diligence. It is better read as a symptom: when the feedback channel is unbounded, noise keeps supplying improvements to chase.
  </details>

- **[ArcFlow: Training Flow Policies for Multiple Solver Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28788)**  `arXiv:2610.28788`  
  _Hieu Tran, Hong Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-matching policies adjust inference cost through the number of solver steps, but reinforcement learning at a single fixed solver budget can hide degradation at cheaper deployment budgets and even erase useful one-step behavior present in a pretrained policy. We introduce Action-Ratio-Clip Flow (ArcFlow), a deployment-aware algorithm for training one flow policy across a specified range of solver budgets. ArcFlow combines action-aligned supervision, budget exposure, and worst-budget checkpoint selection. For reconstruction objectives, its bridge aggregates temporal probes into one surrogate ratio and one PPO-style clip per action, with a boundary residual exactly equal to deployed one-step reconstruction error. Cycling exposes the shared field to the deployment range, and selection optimizes its performance floor. For native velocity objectives, multi-budget endpoint supervision applies the same principle without replacing the native update. Both realizations preserve the architecture and deployment-time cost. Across DM Control, manipulation, and locomotion, ArcFlow attains the highest mean one-step return among five flow-policy baselines on 25 of 30 tasks under each method's reported training settings. Component studies identify complementary improvements from boundary supervision, the fixed-grid bridge, budget exposure, and deployment selection. These results establish deployment-budget-aware training as an effective route to strong low-step control with one policy serving multiple inference costs.
  </details>

- **[Extreme Low-State Quantized Transformer for Electricity-Load Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28784)**  `arXiv:2610.28784`  
  _Muhammad Sabbir Alam, Harindra Sandun Mavikumbure, Victor Cobilean, Milos Manic, Jayasimha Atulasimha_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aggressive low-state weight quantization can substantially reduce neural-network representational flexibility, making predictive performance difficult to preserve when only a few discrete weight states are available. We investigate this regime for Transformer-based electricity-load forecasting using 3-, 5-, 7-, and 16-state weight quantization across feed-forward-only and attention-plus-feed-forward quantization. Two tasks predict either the next hour or the next 24 hours from the preceding 48 hours across two co-primary electricity-consumption datasets. Direct post-training quantization (PTQ) increased mean normalized root-mean-square error (NRMSE) relative to full precision by 6.25 and 7.04 percentage points in Datasets A and B, whereas quantization-aware fine-tuning (QAT-FT) recovered approximately 90.2% and 94.5% of these penalties. Quantization-aware training from random initialization (Scratch-QAT) also produced competitive low-state solutions, with particularly favorable behavior at three states. We further introduce accumulated-update thresholding, which accumulates optimizer-proposed weight changes before commitment. Relative to paired Scratch-QAT controls, this reduced committed shadow-weight updates by approximately 99.7% and discrete state transitions by approximately 77--80%, while mean NRMSE changed by only +0.042 and +0.197 percentage points. These results show that useful Transformer forecasting can be retained under highly discrete weight representations while strongly suppressing target-state activity, supporting a prospective route toward compact, low-activity edge forecasting using multilevel synaptic devices.
  </details>

- **[Second Thoughts: Exploring and Planning through Comparative Imagination in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28779)**  `arXiv:2610.28779`  
  _Hieu Tran, Hong Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models let agents compare imagined outcomes before acting, but an agent must still decide which alternatives to explore and when a proposed plan should replace its learned policy. We introduce SDI (Sibling Disagreement in Imagination), which connects these decisions through a shared actor-based reference. SDI compares action sequences imagined from the same model state, using repeated rollouts of the actor's own sequence to measure variation under fixed behavior. This reference scales both the spread across candidate returns, which provides an exploration signal, and a proposed plan's predicted advantage, which governs action acceptance. The agent executes a planned action when its advantage exceeds the reference variability by a fixed factor; otherwise, it follows the actor. SDI reuses the base agent's world model, actor, and critic without requiring an additional disagreement ensemble. Exploration and planning guide training data collection, while evaluation uses the learned actor alone. Across Meta-World, Adroit, and DM-Control, SDI achieves a higher observed task-average score than every compared baseline in each suite and matches or exceeds the strongest baseline's performance on 15 of 18 tasks. Component ablations and robustness analyses further examine the roles of exploration, planning exposure, and action acceptance. Paired evaluations from identical simulator states show that the gate improves both recovery from actor failures and preservation of successful behavior relative to ungated planning, while substantially reducing return degradation. Together, these results support comparing imagined alternatives against a shared actor reference as a unified approach to exploring and planning with world models.
  </details>

- **[Building a Nested LADDER for LLM Unstructured Post-Training Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28773)**  `arXiv:2610.28773`  
  _Nicolás Mauricio Cuadrado Ávila, Ivo Gollini Navarrete, Martin Takáč, Samuel Horváth_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Non-uniform allocation can improve post-training pruning of large language models, yet quality can still degrade sharply at high sparsity. To control degradation as sparsity increases, we introduce LADDER (Layer-Wise Allocation via Damage-Driven Evaluation and Refinement), which allocates sparsity incrementally. At each step, it evaluates pruning an additional fraction of weights from each eligible layer and commits the layer update that causes the least end-to-end degradation per removed parameter. This process produces non-uniform sparsity profiles and exactly nested masks across multiple budgets. We evaluate LADDER on five OPT, Llama, and Qwen models across 10-90% sparsity, using SparseGPT as its candidate-generation backend and comparing against uniform and adaptive allocation baselines. Results reveal a clear operating-regime trade-off: LADDER is comparable or slightly weaker at benign sparsity but becomes substantially more robust from 70% onward. This high-sparsity advantage largely persists after posterior distillation across four of five models and translates into improved retention of aggregate zero-shot accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Motion Equivariant Convolutional LSTM: When Motion Becomes a Gauge Symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28771)**  `arXiv:2610.28771`  
  _Vahid Hosseinzadeh, T. Anderson Keller, Michael Puthawala_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objects move, cameras pan, and weather drifts with the wind: much of what changes in sequential data is motion. Motion changes where things are, not what they are, and is therefore a symmetry of the data. Yet standard recurrent networks are not equivariant to it: their memory stays put while the input moves on. Flow equivariant recurrent networks first brought this symmetry into sequence models, for motions of constant velocity, or flows, by enumerating a bank of velocities. We extend this idea in three directions: from simple recurrent networks to convolutional LSTMs, from flows to motions whose velocity changes in time, and from enumerating velocities to estimating them. Treating a sequence as a field over time, we show that motion becomes a gauge symmetry: a global symmetry made local in time. Built on this view, our motion equivariant convolutional LSTM (MELSTM) uses its velocity model as the gauge field and is provably equivariant to every motion. Across future-frame prediction, classification of digits visible only through their motion, and action recognition under camera shake, MELSTM outperforms a convolutional LSTM and its flow equivariant counterpart with the same number of parameters: it is more accurate, generalizes to longer horizons and to unseen motions, and carries a fraction of the flow equivariant model's state.
  </details>

- **[Offline Apprenticeship Learning for Human-Centric Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28716)**  `arXiv:2610.28716`  
  _Md Mirajul Islam_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective decision-making in human-centric environments such as Intelligent Tutoring Systems (ITSs) and clinical healthcare requires policies that are adaptive, data-efficient, and ethically deployable. Apprenticeship Learning (AL) infers policies directly from expert demonstrations by recovering the underlying reward structure, enabling strictly offline learning without environment interaction. This dissertation develops a progression of offline AL frameworks organized around two challenges: reward modeling and demonstration quality. For reward modeling, EM-EDM clusters demonstrations by latent reward functions and induces cluster-specific policies, and THEMES partitions trajectories into temporally coherent sub-trajectories guided by a hierarchically learned reward regulator; across one educational and three clinical datasets, THEMES outperforms flat and hierarchical AL baselines. For demonstration quality, HALIDE integrates continuous quality signals into hierarchical policy induction, enabling principled reuse of mixed-quality data under distribution shift. CCHAL further attributes quality to individual decisions via Bayesian credit assignment, dual-arm criticality scoring that contrasts expert and non-expert behavior, and a Bradley-Terry contrastive IRL objective over failure trajectories, outperforming baselines including HALIDE on three clinical EHR datasets and an ITS dataset. Finally, a real-world classroom evaluation of StuEDM, an EDM-induced policy that preserves student agency and intervenes only on disagreement, shows significant learning gains over an Expert baseline and a higher pretest-adjusted posttest score than a CQL policy trained on a far larger corpus, while overriding students less than half as often. Together, this work establishes offline AL as a practical, data-efficient, and ethically viable paradigm for human-centric environments.
  </details>

- **[Big Claims, Small Margins: Do Reported GraphLLM Gains Survive Simple Baselines?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28714)**  `arXiv:2610.28714`  
  _Michal Podstawski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based methods for node classification on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) routinely report state-of-the-art accuracy. We ask how these results compare with inexpensive baselines run on the same splits. On the splits of three recent studies, we tune four baseline representatives with validation-only selection and compare them with the highest mean that each paper reports for an LLM-based method in the matched setting. Correct & Smooth on TF-IDF features trails this reference by a median of 2.3 percentage points, and an MLP on frozen e5-large-v2 embeddings by 2.8 points. Correct & Smooth is within 3 points of the reference or better on 15 of 24 tasks, but the reference is significantly higher on 12 of 22 testable tasks, so several reported gains remain substantial. Reading released code next to the papers, we also document evaluation risks: selection of hyperparameters or checkpoints on the test set, labels of test-pool nodes used in training, baseline numbers reused without disclosure, and a text-label misalignment in a released Cora loader. Reports of the same method on the same split also differ by up to 12.5 points across papers whose configurations are not documented to match.
  </details>

- **[STAR-XPC: Set Transformer for Estimating X-ray Transmission in Polymer Composites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28709)**  `arXiv:2610.28709`  
  _Can Erer, H. Birkan Yilmaz, Nebahat Aral_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating the energy transmission ratio of an X-ray beam through a polymer composite is a challenging task in radiation shielding studies. The reference solution with Monte Carlo transport simulation is highly accurate but computationally expensive: each of our reference simulations is capped at $4\times10^9$ photon histories and a 20,000 s (over 5 hours) time limit, while closed-form analytical methods fail to represent such complex scenarios. We present an artificial neural network model that maps the unordered multi-set of molecular constituents, their mass fractions, and four bulk physical descriptors directly to the transmission ratio $I(t)/I_0$ in 1-3 milliseconds. The model is a Set Transformer, which makes it permutation invariant by construction and accepts mixtures of different sizes up to 11 constituents within a fixed-size padded set. Each constituent is embedded with a chemistry-pretrained transformer language model, ChemBERTa. In our proposed method, training data are produced by an automated PENELOPE/penEasy pipeline using the radiation source at 30, 80, 100, and 120 kV tube voltages over polyethylene-based shielding composite slabs of thicknesses spanning 0.15-2.5 mm. After preprocessing, the dataset contains $n = 4697$ simulated configurations on which a leakage-free, jointly stratified 10-fold cross-validation is performed, attaining mean squared error MSE = $(1.12 \pm 0.56)\times10^{-4}$, normalized-MSE percentage NMSE% = 0.0294% $\pm$ 0.0147%, and $R^2 = 0.9988$. The corresponding per-sample percentage errors give an alpha-trimmed ($\alpha = 0.10$) absolute percentage error (APE) of 1.66% and a median APE of 1.15%. Our model's inference is several orders of magnitude faster than the underlying Monte Carlo simulation (sub-millisecond per query vs. several hours for the reference run) with effective accuracy in predicting $I(t)/I_0$.
  </details>

- **[SimHack: Predicting the onset of detectable reward hacking before RL training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28707)**  `arXiv:2610.28707`  
  _Junwei Deng, Alex Bie, Natalia Ponomareva_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When training LLMs to code with reinforcement learning, reward hacking is often detectable but difficult to prevent, since (a) environment hardening is increasingly difficult as training environments grow more complex; and (b) training-time interventions incentivize models to obfuscate hacking behavior to bypass detection.We instead study whether we can predict when detectable reward hacking becomes dominant before executing an RL run, enabling us to select lower-risk recipes in advance. We find that the initial hacking rate alone does not determine onset, which varies substantially across training recipes and repeated runs. We thus formulate this problem as predicting the distribution of the reward hacking onset step under a target training recipe.We introduce SimHack, a simulator that learns reward hacking training dynamics from a handful of smaller-scale reference RL runs and generates counterfactual simulation runs of unseen target recipes without costly retraining. Across unseen target recipes, SimHack predicts the median onset to a relative error of 11.3% and achieves across-recipe rank correlation of 0.85, while generalizing to differences in model scale, curriculum, number of rollouts, and learning rate between reference and target recipes. As an application, we show that SimHack can guide training decisions by screening candidate RL curricula, discovering curricula over the same set of scenarios that train for 96.6% longer without hacking.
  </details>

- **[Scalable Stress-Constrained Topology Optimization: A Super-resolution and Simultaneous Approach Based on Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28703)**  `arXiv:2610.28703`  
  _Xiangyu Sun, Shirin Hosseinmardi, Ramin Bostanabad_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale topology optimization (TO) remains computationally demanding, especially for stress-constrained problems. To address this challenge, we propose the simultaneous neural topology optimization (SNTO) framework where we represent state, adjoint, and design variables with custom neural networks (NNs) whose parameters are jointly optimized within a single optimization loop that leverages continuous adjoint analysis and automatic differentiation. This optimization minimizes a multi-component loss function that combines the design objective, an aggregation of the local stress constraints, and the potential energy functionals. Our NNs have a unique architecture that accelerates convergence while enabling the networks to capture sharp gradients and boundaries which are critical to stress-constrained TO. To achieve scalability to millions of degrees of freedom (DoF), we develop a hardware-aware training scheme in which the design domain is partitioned into subdomains that are distributed across multiple GPUs. To control peak GPU memory usage, we further slice each subdomain into smaller regions whose loss gradients are evaluated sequentially and accumulated to update the NN parameters. Unlike domain decomposition approaches, our partitioning naturally ensures field continuity across the subdomains and does not require the introduction of additional terms into the loss function. For validation, we first evaluate the proposed framework on various 2D stress-constrained benchmarks and compare our results to a conventional finite element-based approach, showing that SNTO produces super-resolution designs with sharper boundaries, smaller gray areas, and lower volume fractions. We then consider large-scale 3D problems with up to eight million collocation points and distribute the training over up to eight GPUs.
  </details>

- **[Observation Assimilation Transformer: Probabilistic Weather Analysis from Sparse Station Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28327)**  `arXiv:2610.28327`  
  _Christian Donner, Michele Cattaneo, Maxim Samarin, Francesco Zanetta, Icíar Lloréns Jover, Gabriela Aznar Siguan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the Observation Assimilation Transformer (OAT), a geometry-aware neural process that corrects and refines a prior numerical weather prediction (NWP) field into a high-resolution posterior field by assimilating sparse observations. OAT is an encoder-decoder transformer that considers a set of observed locations and their NWP prior to condition predictions at a set of arbitrary unobserved query locations. Its sparse attention mechanism makes it scalable to thousands of station observations and depends on relative positions between locations, never on the absolute coordinates. The model allows for partially missing observations, predictions at arbitrary continuous locations without grid projection, and outputs are parametric distributions. A station-randomized training algorithm is developed with continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) loss. We test OAT on two datasets - one at regional scale with hundreds of stations, and one at continental scale with thousands of stations. On these datasets OAT outperforms several baselines and shows well calibrated predictions. In an illustrative case study, OAT's downscaling capabilities are demonstrated by downscaling ERA5 (~31km) to COSMO (1km), where OAT achieves lower mean absolute error (MAE) and CRPS for temperature and precipitation on test stations compared to COSMO.
  </details>

- **[Hybrid Momentum Methods for Optimizing Deep Learning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27674)**  `arXiv:2610.27674`  
  _Haifeng Xu, Yijin Ren, Chenyu Zhou, Qi Deng, Dongdong Ge, Yinyu Ye_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Momentum is central to stochastic optimization in deep learning, including the training of large language models. Classical momentum methods typically rely on a fast exponential moving average (EMA) scheme, which discounts past gradients on a fixed timescale and may underweight older gradients that remain informative. This creates a trade-off between retaining long-term history and adapting to recent changes. We introduce Hybrid Momentum (HyM), which combines a linearly weighted average of past gradients with a geometrically discounted fast momentum. The former assigns weights proportional to the gradients' iteration indices and retains a growing span of history, while the latter emphasizes recent gradients. This hybrid construction is modular and can be combined with adaptive preconditioning and other update transformations. In particular, we propose HyMUON, which applies Newton--Schulz orthogonalization to the hybrid direction. Illustrative examples and controlled experiments demonstrate the benefits of combining gradient information across these two timescales. Across image classification, machine translation, and language modeling, HyM and HyMUON match or improve upon strong baselines. HyMUON improves over Muon on IWSLT14, and in both the 100k- and 600k-step GPT-2 124M OpenWebText regimes. For LLaMA-1.3B pretraining on C4, HyMUON achieves a final validation loss of 2.777, compared with 2.903 for Muon.
  </details>

- **[Lower Bounds for Smooth Nonconvex Zeroth-Order Optimization: The Price of Determinism under Low Curvature Trace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27550)**  `arXiv:2610.27550`  
  _Yuxing Peng, Zhiqing Tang, Weijia Jia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low curvature trace, an effective rank condition, is a common explanation for why randomized zeroth-order methods scale to high-dimensional tasks such as language model fine-tuning. Can deterministic methods achieve the same savings? We show that they cannot. We study finding $\varepsilon$-stationary points of $L$-smooth nonconvex functions on $\mathbb{R}^d$ from exact function values, assuming that below the starting value the Hessian is dominated by a fixed matrix with trace at most $LE$; this is a fixed-matrix form of the effective rank condition used to explain zeroth-order fine-tuning. When $ET\le d$, the Gaussian two-point method that such fine-tuning uses needs only $O(ET)$ calls if it rejects steps that raise $f$, where $T=L\Delta/\varepsilon^2$ is the iteration count of gradient descent from initial gap $\Delta$, and we prove that every randomized method needs $\Omega(ET)$ calls, so this cost is optimal up to constants. In contrast, every deterministic method needs $\widetilde\Omega(d\min\{T,\sqrt{ET}\})$ calls, and adaptive subspace search attains $O(d\min\{T,\sqrt{ET}\})$: a deterministic method pays about $d$ calls per discovered direction, and the trace budget allows about $\sqrt{ET}$ directions. Determinism thus multiplies the cost by a factor of order $d/(E+\sqrt{ET})$ up to logarithmic factors, which grows linearly with $d$ for fixed $E$ and $T$; once $E$ exceeds $T$ by a logarithmic factor, deterministic methods need $\Omega(dT)$ calls, as without any trace budget. Our lower bounds hold for $C^\infty$ functions with the matrix bound on all of $\mathbb{R}^d$, even when each call also returns gradient projections priced by rank.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Policy Discrepancy: Capability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26950)**  `arXiv:2610.26950`  
  _Xuanfeng Yang, Le Gai, Jiaming Wang, Xuan Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) integrates domain specialists into a shared student, but determining how strongly each teacher should supervise remains challenging. Existing discrepancy-based allocation can assign strong supervision even when the student already matches or exceeds the teacher's task performance, since behavioral mismatch does not necessarily imply a remaining capability deficit. We propose Capability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (CA-MOPD), which combines specialist merging with capability-guided allocation to account for what the student has already learned. CA-MOPD first merges reinforcement-learning specialists into a strong student initialization, then uses verifier-estimated teacher--student capability gaps to adjust domain-level distillation strength. Supervision decreases as capability gaps close and increases when estimated student capability regresses, while dense token-level guidance remains provided by the teachers. Across six benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, and instruction following, CA-MOPD achieves an average score of 32.22, outperforming naive MOPD, Open-MOPD, and the merged initialization by 0.86, 0.83, and 0.74 points, respectively. Controlled comparisons further support the benefits of merged initialization and capability-gap guidance under matched training budgets.
  </details>

- **[TrustAgent-RFF: Agentic Diagnostic Planning for Reliable RF Fingerprinting under Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24930)**  `arXiv:2610.24930`  
  _Chuan Liu, Tianya Zhao, Yiting Wang, Junqing Zhang, Xuyu Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radio frequency fingerprinting (RFF) models can achieve accuracy under source domain conditions but become unreliable when the receiver, distance, collection day, or environment changes. Incorrect predictions in unseen domains may receive high softmax confidence, making confidence thresholding insufficient for trustworthy deployment. We propose TrustAgent-RFF, a prediction level reliability framework for RFF that operates with a frozen in-phase and quadrature (I/Q) classifier without target domain labels. For each device prediction, the framework acquires radio frequency evidence: counterfactual stability, prototype consistency, acquisition domain similarity, and signal quality. A source calibrated trust estimator combines these diagnostics with classifier confidence, prediction margin, and evidence availability indicators. Source derived lower tail penalties and cross module interactions reduce trust when diagnostic signals indicate weak support. A large language model (LLM) based planner adaptively selects the next diagnostic action based on evidence, trust state, action costs, and remaining budget. A deterministic source calibrated decision layer updates the prediction state as Accept, Reject, or Defer. Experiments across five RFF datasets and classifier backbones reveal an average source to target accuracy drop of 38.7% and show that many incorrect target domain predictions remain highly confident.
  </details>

- **[An important Bottleneck in LLM Optimizers: Principle-Level Local Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24291)**  `arXiv:2610.24291`  
  _Nian Ran, Tianhe Jia, Zhengyang Mu, Yue Wang, Sebastian Brulin, Markus Olhofer, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feedback-driven LLM optimizers are increasingly used for evolutionary search, Bayesian optimization, heuristic design, and scientific discovery. Yet rapid initial gains often give way to prolonged plateaus despite substantial remaining evaluation budgets. We investigate \emph{principle-level search collapse}, where an optimizer's search becomes confined to a narrow set of design principles, even though it may continue to generate distinct candidates and achieve small improvements. We assemble a diagnostic benchmark spanning molecular optimization, network sentinel-node selection, and GPU-kernel optimization, and introduce a decision-tree protocol combining principle narrowing, prolonged performance plateaus, and gaps to attainable reference solutions. Across 161 assessable runs on the three problem domains, 78.3\% receive support for collapse, including 55.3\% with strong support. Candidate-level variation can nevertheless conceal restricted search at the principle level. We then test \emph{diversity injection}, a common response to search stagnation, such as higher t, multiple LLMs, and web search, and show that it provides limited and inconsistent benefits: among 65 molecular runs, still 73.8\% and 53.8\% receive support and strong support respectively. We finally test reference-principle interventions and find that they improve some runs, but their benefits vary across models and tasks: access to a better principle does not guarantee its effective use. Our benchmark and diagnostic protocol provide a basis for evaluating future optimizers on principle-level collapse. Our findings highlight the need for online collapse detection and better mechanisms for generating and maintaining alternative principles during optimization.
  </details>

- **[Generalization Guarantees of Randomized Block Coordinate Descent for Nonconvex Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22213)**  `arXiv:2610.22213`  
  _Ruixi Hu, Liang Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Randomized block coordinate descent (RBCD) is a popular optimization algorithm for many large-scale machine learning problems. The existing theoretical analysis of RBCD mainly focuses on its convergence behavior from an optimization perspective, while its generalization behavior remains less understood for nonconvex problems. In this paper, we establish high-probability generalization guarantees for RBCD with nonconvex objectives. In the general nonconvex setting, we derive bounds on the gradients of both empirical and population risks, requiring only standard smoothness assumptions. With polynomially decaying step sizes, we tune the iteration number to balance optimization and generalization, thereby quantifying how early stopping leads to the optimal convergence rate for the population gradients. In the gradient-dominated setting, we provide bounds in terms of functional suboptimality, which nearly match the optimistic rates in convex and low-noise settings. Finally, we develop dimension-independent bounds for loss functions of a specific structure, making the generalization guarantees applicable to high-dimensional problems.
  </details>

- **[Compression Through Time: An Interplay of Separability, Stationarity and Capacity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16660)**  `arXiv:2610.16660`  
  _Nikolaus Kopp, Franz Pernkopf, Bernhard Geiger_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Information-theoretic and geometric compression have been widely studied as possible explanations for generalization. Yet their temporal dynamics in recurrent networks remain largely unexplored. Using discretized LSTMs, whose finite embedding space enables direct estimates of information quantities, we observe pronounced compression across the final timesteps of many datasets. We explain this effect through three competing mechanisms: (i) class separability permits stronger compression at the final timestep; (ii) shared recurrent dynamics propagate this pressure across timesteps with similar activation states, making its extent depend on temporal stationarity; and (iii) limited representational capacity opposes prolonged compression because it restricts information available for subsequent computation. We systematically test these mechanisms through controlled synthetic interventions and real-world sequence tasks. Our results identify class separability, temporal stationarity, and network capacity as key determinants of temporal compression, and suggest that compression acts as a regularizer-potentially beneficial when excess capacity is available, but harmful to generalization when it consumes memory needed for the task.
  </details>

- **[Pessimistic Latent Task-aware Optimization for Robust Offline Meta-Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15428)**  `arXiv:2610.15428`  
  _Chang-Hoon Jeong, Kisung Shin, Hanwool Sul, Byoung-Tak Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Context-based offline meta-reinforcement learning (COMRL) has advanced almost entirely through better task representations, which separate task identity from artifacts of the behavior policy so that the latent reflects the task alone. The implicit expectation has been that a clean representation will transfer to out-of-support (parameter-extrapolation) tasks. On such a task, however, the encoder produces a latent outside the training support, where the policy has never been trained. We address this gap with Pessimistic Latent Task-aware Optimization (PLATO), which exposes the policy to off-support latents during training. PLATO assumes a mild manifold hypothesis: task latents lie on a low-dimensional manifold, and out-of-support tasks lie farther from the training centroid. It exploits this geometry by perturbing inferred latents outward from the centroid to synthesize counterfactual tasks. A learned decoder ensemble then rolls out short trajectories at each perturbed latent, and the policy is updated on the imagined transitions under a pessimism weight that down-weights steps on which the ensemble disagrees. We prove that the perturbation leaves the training support by a controllable margin and that the out-of-support generalization gap is bounded by the same perturbation reach, and we give explicit conditions, verified empirically, under which the epistemic disagreement at the perturbed latent stays bounded away from zero. On eight MuJoCo continuous-control benchmarks, PLATO consistently improves out-of-support returns over strong COMRL baselines while remaining competitive on in-distribution tasks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Telling FIBS: Probes Effectively Detect Sabotage and Catch Unverbalized Deception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12445)**  `arXiv:2610.12445`  
  _Oskar J. Hollinsworth, Alex F. Spies, Tigist Diriba, Adam Gleave, Chris Cundy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent incidents have highlighted the challenge of monitoring LLM agents and the danger of models deceiving people. We show that white-box deception detection via probes can be scaled up to frontier monitoring settings by collecting the largest deception dataset to date for training probes and introducing a novel probe architecture which can aggregate information across many layers and tokens. Our probes surpass black-box monitors on agentic coding benchmarks, achieving 98.3% AUROC in SHADE-Arena and 98.0% in BashArena (vs. 97.7%, 97.6% resp. for action-only text monitors), and show improved efficacy as the underlying model is scaled up. We further test probes on several cases where deception cannot be determined from the context alone; in these cases of introspective deception, the ground truth can only be determined through careful elicitation or knowledge of a model's training data. In one such evaluation, we show that probes can distinguish transcripts containing a model's true hidden goal from other goals with an AUROC of up to 99.7%. Our probes also detect deception on prominent open-weight models which lie about politically sensitive topics when put under pressure. We release our training dataset, dubbed FIBS, to help drive frontier deployment of effective probes, and encourage the community to expand upon it with further examples of deception and sabotage.
  </details>

- **[A Generalisation Signal Need Not Be a Model-Selection Signal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39099)**  `arXiv:2609.39099`  
  _Aditya Nagarsekar, M P Ashish Bhat, Aadi Nesarkar, Vrishti Godhwani, Rahul Yedida, Aditya Challa, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model selection in computational biology often relies on validation data drawn from the training regime, even when deployment lies outside it. When validation no longer preserves which model is best, a natural alternative is to rank candidates using properties of the trained network itself. We test this idea using a novel, forward-only proxy motivated by the norm of the Hessian, alongside common Hessian measures, across molecular property, protein fitness, and drug-response tasks. Contrary to our hypothesis, geometry does not become more useful as validation Spearman correlation deteriorates: augmenting validation helps some shifts but significantly harms others. More surprisingly, the proxy still correlates with generalisation gap on most tasks even when Hessian trace and top-eigenvalue relationships are weak or reversed, yet this signal does not reliably identify the deployment-best model. A curvature bound need not preserve cross-model rankings, and low geometric scores can even favour collapsed predictors. Thus, a generalisation signal need not be a model-selection signal.
  </details>

- **[Interpretable but Fragile? Robustness of Concept Bottlenecks under Geometric-Semantic Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38625)**  `arXiv:2609.38625`  
  _Hanwei Zhang, Tianma Hu, Gaojie Jin, Xu Cheng, Ronghui Mu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) are designed to provide interpretable intermediate representations, yet how such bottlenecks affect robustness remains unclear, with existing studies reporting mixed and sometimes contradictory findings. We argue that these discrepancies arise from conflating different robustness notions and perturbation regimes, rather than from fundamental disagreements about CBMs themselves. To disentangle these factors, we introduce a generator-based evaluation framework that enables controlled comparisons between standard classifiers and CBMs under two distinct perturbation types: continuous geometric perturbations in latent space and discrete semantic interventions in concept space. Within this framework, we evaluate robustness both empirically, via prediction and concept-level sensitivity metrics, and certifiably, using randomized smoothing in latent and concept spaces. Across experiments, we reconcile previously conflicting findings by clarifying when, and in what sense, concept bottlenecks do or do not improve robustness. By further analyzing robustness under varying task conditions, including class semantic similarity and concept vocabulary size, we show that interpretability does not inherently confer robustness. Instead, concept bottlenecks shift where and how sensitivity manifests, revealing a nuanced interpretability robustness trade off that depends critically on the perturbation regime and task structure. Together, our results show that interpretability and robustness are distinct objectives: interpretable intermediate representations do not uniformly improve robustness, but instead redistribute sensitivity across perturbation spaces and model families.
  </details>

- **[Feature Superposition in Neural Networks: From Theory to Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06862)**  `arXiv:2609.06862`  
  _Dai Shi, Xiaoyu Li, Andi Han, José Miguel Hernández-Lobato_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Superposition refers to neural networks representing more features than they have dimensions. It was proposed to explain polysemantic neurons, which respond to several features, and also other phenomena, such as neural scaling laws and adversarial vulnerability. A feature stored in superposition is useful to the extent that its value can be read back despite interference from the other features. The theory of superposition mainly asks how wide a layer must be for the features to be read back, and by which decoder. In theoretical models, a single linear map that reads back every combination of nonzero features needs a width nearly quadratic in their number, while sparse optimization can succeed with a width linear in that number. In practice, one may hope to interpret a trained network by recovering its features in the same way. However, its features are usually unknown, and thus they are estimated from its activations or weights by recovery methods, such as sparse autoencoders and transcoders. In this work, we organize the theory of superposition by the decoder class and its practice by the strength of the claims about a feature supported by each method's results. On the theory side, we review the lower bounds on the overlap between feature directions, the conditions under which trained toy models share dimensions among features, and the separation between storing features and computing new features. On the practice side, we categorize recovery methods into four families, from probes for a named feature to decompositions of the trained weights. We then compare their evaluations, failure modes, and applications, and grade the evidence on one scale of claims about a feature. Finally, we assess previously stated open problems and identify remaining theoretical and empirical questions about superposition in trained networks. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[When Retain Constraints Conflict: Mitigating Forget-Retain Interference in Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06786)**  `arXiv:2609.06786`  
  _Zijie Liu, Jinhao Duan, Bingqi Shang, Xinming An, Sijia Liu, Tianlong Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of designated training data while preserving model utility, but its behavior on tabular data remains underexplored. This gap is important because tabular prediction is widely used in high-stakes domains and is increasingly adapted to language models through record serialization and schema-aware prompting. We identify a key challenge that distinguishes tabular unlearning from unlearning in free-form text or other modalities: schema-induced forget-retain overlap. In serialized tabular data, records share fixed column-name/value slots, similar attribute ranges, and common output spaces. Consequently, a forget row may have nearby retain rows that rely on the same high-signal attributes, causing retain preservation to oppose the update required for forgetting. Motivated by this failure mode, we propose Conflict-Aware Unlearning (CAU), a schema-aware approach that reduces forget-retain interference by relaxing preservation constraints on retained rows that most conflict with the forget set. Across sample-level and feature-level unlearning on clinical and non-medical tabular tasks, CAU more closely matches a retraining oracle while maintaining predictive utility and retain-region behavior. Our results show that reliable tabular LLM unlearning depends not only on the forgetting objective, but also on how retain constraints are constructed.
  </details>

- **[DrugReason: Dynamic Multi-View Reasoning over Knowledge Graph and Language Evidence for Drug Repurposing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06779)**  `arXiv:2609.06779`  
  _Zijie Liu, Hongxuan Li, Zhen Tan, Jinhao Duan, Baixiang Huang, Zunpeng Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Drug repurposing aims to identify new therapeutic uses for existing compounds and, compared with de novo drug discovery, offers a faster and more cost-effective path to clinical translation. However, the space of candidate drug-disease pairs is enormous and their underlying relationships often depend on complex multi-hop biological mechanisms, making it difficult to reliably predict which pairs represent true therapeutic relationships. Existing approaches tackle this from two directions: knowledge graph-based methods organize curated biomedical evidence into structured relational networks for grounded multi-hop reasoning, while LLM-based methods leverage pretrained knowledge to generate flexible mechanistic rationales. Yet neither is sufficient alone - KGs are confined to observed graph structure while LLMs lack factual grounding and risk hallucination. To address this gap, we propose DrugReason, a multi-view reasoning framework that integrates grounded KG reasoning with LLM-generated mechanistic inference for drug repurposing. DrugReason adaptively routes diverse reasoning paths to specialized experts conditioned on the query context, while a cross-expert distillation objective enables knowledge sharing without sacrificing expert specialization. Experiments on PharmaDB, DDInter, and DrugBank show that DrugReason improves average performance over strong single-view reasoning baselines and achieves competitive or superior results compared with graph-based alternatives, while providing interpretable routing-based predictions.
  </details>

- **[Small-Scale Experiments: Are We There Yet?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11859)**  `arXiv:2608.11859`  
  _Nicholas Lourie, Kyunghyun Cho, Karen Ullrich, Sanae Lotfi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling laws promised cost-effective experiments; six years later, they have yet to fully deliver. Instead, researchers have found them unreliable at small scales (starting at 4M parameters) and concluded that sizable models cannot be avoided. We show this is not the case: the confounding factor is hyperparameters. Small models are highly sensitive, but hyperparameter sensitivity fades with scale. This small-scale sensitivity makes scaling laws easy to miss because they only emerge on the fully tuned frontier, and reaching that frontier requires an extensive search far beyond what most ever run. By ablating the basic scaling law recipe, we show well-tuned hyperparameters matter more than any other ingredient. Further, we reveal why those hyperparameters become easier to find: as scale increases, the hyperparameter loss surface becomes lower dimensional. Nevertheless while scaling laws exist in small models, extrapolation hits statistical limitations. A holistic approach is required. Synthesizing our insights with the recent literature, we develop a new methodology for model-centric research and demonstrate it on a question that once took the field years to settle: where to place normalization layers in the transformer architecture. From small-scale experiments, we recover the large scale result: pre-normalization works better as models grow in size. With the right tools and a better understanding, small-scale experiments can deliver on scaling laws' long-awaited promise.
  </details>

- **[Goal Retrieval versus Action-Value Ordering: Contrastive Critics under Best-of-$K$ Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27422)**  `arXiv:2607.27422`  
  _Ayushman Singh, Siddharth Aphale_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Goal-conditioned action generators can produce many plausible actions. Best-of-\(K\) selection then uses a critic to rank those actions for the current state and goal. Standard contrastive critics are trained and evaluated on a different ranking: distinguishing future goals from unrelated negatives for a fixed state--action pair. The unnormalized contrastive score is monotone in behavior-policy value at the population optimum. Cosine-normalized scores lack this guarantee, and finite trained networks may fail to recover the correct ordering. We audit the resulting gap using shared state--goal queries and frozen candidate pools, so critics differ only in their action ordering. Raw and cosine critics reach retrieval AUCs of \(0.96\)--\(1.00\) across eight OGBench tasks. Their progress ordering on navigation tasks is much weaker and sometimes inverted. In the exact-value controlled problem, all three contrastive selectors have higher mean regret than random selection. Their regret can increase with \(K\). TD-Q stays close to the pool oracle. PointMaze is the only task with a reliable learned value reference. TD-Q gives substantially better fixed-query ordering there, though the differences in executed return are smaller and vary by task. Matched controls suggest that graded value supervision accounts for most of the improvement. Changing score bounds or model capacity alone has much less effect. High retrieval performance does not establish whether a critic ranks actions correctly for best-of-\(K\) selection. That requires a separate action-ordering evaluation before deployment.
  </details>

- **[Inverse RL Helps Align AI by Imitating Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24900)**  `arXiv:2607.24900`  
  _Michał Wiliński, Liu Leqi, Chirag Nagpal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language model alignment aims to make model behavior reflect desirable properties such as helpfulness, safety, and instruction following. Existing methods draw supervision from expert demonstrations, preference judgments, or verifiable outcomes. Demonstrations typically guide supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while preference judgments and verifiers provide rewards for reinforcement learning (RL). This raises a question: can the demonstrations alone be used to derive an explicit, interpretable reward useful in a post-training pipeline? Motivated by inverse reinforcement learning, we introduce Projected Alignment Reward Estimated from Demonstrations (PARED). PARED learns an explicit reward from expert demonstrations over a small set of response-level features, using a lightweight discriminator that separates demonstrations from the policy's own samples in this feature space. Experiments with inference-time reranking and adversarial on-policy RL show improvements both when starting from a base model without an SFT stage and when further optimizing an SFT-trained model. Additionally, we demonstrate that PARED can be used for contextual alignment, in which a single policy learns to adapt its responses to the preferences of different audiences.
  </details>

- **[Conditional Optimal Bridge for Riemannian Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10517)**  `arXiv:2607.10517`  
  _Seyed Arshan Dalili, Ajay Narayanan Sridhar, Vijaykrishnan Narayanan, Mehrdad Mahdavi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering offers a lightweight alternative to fine-tuning for controlling large language models at inference time. Many existing steering methods implicitly optimize a log-density-ratio objective between desired and undesired activation distributions without a principled derivation. Methods with fixed steering directions can also degrade out-of-distribution (OOD) performance. We introduce Conditional Optimal Bridge for Riemannian Activation Steering (\textsc{Cobras}), which casts steering as a Schrödinger Bridge on the residual-stream hypersphere. This formulation yields a principled derivation of the log-density-ratio steering objective from a well-posed optimization problem. We construct a query-adaptive steering field that recovers log-density-ratio steering as a limiting case. The fitted transport also provides a support score for controlling OOD interventions. Across four models, \textsc{Cobras} outperforms prior steering methods in nearly all model--task settings spanning helpfulness, truthfulness, and detoxification, while largely preserving OOD performance. The code is available \href{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>

- **[Mixture-of-Control: State-Aware Fine-Tuning for Transformer-based Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31397)**  `arXiv:2606.31397`  
  _Duc Anh Nguyen, Tien Ngoc Luu, Tung Pham, Toan Tran_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  State-based fine-tuning has emerged as a compelling alternative to weight-based adaptation for transformers, updating lightweight controls into states rather than model weights, offering substantial memory savings while retaining parameter efficiency. However, most existing state-based methods typically apply only per-block control updates, which limits inter-block information exchange and restricts representational adaptation. Meanwhile, prior mechanisms that enable cross-block communication often introduce considerable computational overhead, reducing their practicality for efficient fine-tuning. We introduce Mixture-of-Control (MoC), an efficient cross-layer communication that adaptively integrates local and global control signals to enhance representation learning during adaptation. MoC treats block-wise control states as experts in a sparse mixture-of-experts process, enabling efficient communication across transformer blocks. Experiments on 33 datasets and 10 pretrained models across diverse transformer-based benchmarks demonstrate that MoC consistently outperforms existing state-based fine-tuning methods while maintaining comparable efficiency in memory and compute.
  </details>

- **[InfoQuant: Shaping Activation Distributions for Low-Bit LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26175)**  `arXiv:2605.26175`  
  _Ke Li, Dong An, Xiaoling Zang, Can Ye, Liang Xie, Qibo Qiu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-bit activation quantization remains a major bottleneck in efficient large language model (LLM) deployment. The difficulty is not only that activations contain outliers, but that their distributions are often poorly matched to a low-bit uniform quantizer. Existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods suppress peaks, balance channels, or minimize reconstruction error, yet they rarely specify what activation distribution is actually easy to discretize. As a result, activations may appear numerically smoother while still incurring large quantization error because the quantization range remains wide or most values collapse into a few levels near the mean. We recast activation transformation as quantizer-facing distribution design and analyze quantization error from an information-theoretic perspective. Our analysis shows that quantization-friendly activations should jointly have a smaller numerical range and sufficient dispersion within that range. Guided by this analysis, we propose InfoQuant, a train-free method that employs Peak Suppression Orthogonal Transformation (PSOT) to shape activations into more quantization-friendly distributions. We further introduce adaptive outlier-token selection to improve the robustness of PSOT during optimization. Across multiple LLM families, InfoQuant consistently outperforms prior PTQ and end-to-end training baselines. Under W4A4KV4, it preserves 97% of floating-point accuracy on average and reduces the LLaMA-2 13B performance gap by 42% over the previous state of the art. Code is available at [this https URL](this https URL)
  </details>

- **[Metropolis-Scale Resilient and Trustworthy Traffic Flow Inference Using Multi-Source Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25004)**  `arXiv:2605.25004`  
  _Qishen Zhou, Yifan Zhang, Michail A. Makridis, Anastasios Kouvelas, Yibing Wang, Simon Hu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inferring network-wide traffic states from sparse observations with high accuracy and trustworthy uncertainty quantification is essential for intelligent transportation systems, yet it remains challenging due to the underdetermined nature of the problem, multifaceted disturbances in sensing networks, and the inherent conflicts among multiple inference subtasks when modeled jointly. We propose the Task-Aware Attentive Neural Process (TA-ANP), a unified probabilistic framework for resilient and trustworthy global traffic state inference (GTSI) by fusing floating car data (FCD) with sparse fixed-detector measurements. By casting GTSI as a stochastic process, TA-ANP leverages the meta-learning properties of neural processes to adapt rapidly to changes in sensing configurations without retraining. A task-aware multi-query attention module with distinct spatiotemporal inductive biases is introduced to handle three GTSI subtasks jointly ---real-time estimation at unobserved locations, forecasting at both observed and unobserved locations---while mitigating cross-task interference. For uncertainty quantification, we combine neural processes with Monte Carlo Dropout to capture both aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty. To support metropolis-scale evaluation, we construct the Metropolitan Multi-Source Traffic Dataset (MMTD), integrating fixed-loop sensor measurements, FCD statistics, and OpenStreetMap road-network data over an urban network of 26,166 road segments, including 2,371 fixed-sensor-equipped segments used for quantitative evaluation. Experiments across multiple independently generated sensor configurations show that TA-ANP achieves best performance across all subtasks under deterministic and probabilistic metrics. The resulting well-calibrated uncertainties enable more efficient fixed-sensor placement with fewer sensor deployments.
  </details>

- **[Biological Context Does Not Reliably Improve LLM-Guided Experimental Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15341)**  `arXiv:2605.15341`  
  _Marilyn Zhang, Tianfeng Chen, Mark E. Whiting_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous labs increasingly deploy large language models (LLMs) as closedloop biology optimizers that propose a design, read the outcome, then propose again. This is partly because of an assumption that biological knowledge helps an LLM choose better designs than Bayesian optimization, which has no access to biological context. We test that assumption on 45 biology tasks reconstructed from 2,485 experimental rows across 341 published papers, scored by oracles fitted to those rows, each run over 30 iterations under two prompt conditions. The domain-aware condition provides the published parameter names, units, and labels, and the domain-agnostic renames them so they are not biologically meaningful, with search space and feedback identical. Across 19 LLMs domain-agnostic outperforms domain-aware on 58.7% of tasks by trajectory and 61.9% by endpoint. The failure is specific to biology, reversing on education tasks built the same way. Domain-aware prompting tends to help models propose better initial designs, while making them respond worse to feedback. Part of the reason is anchoring, with models keeping the conventional categorical choice even when the oracle scores a different (published best) value higher. Post-training Llama 3.1 8B to frontier-level raw optimization does not transfer to biology-aware optimization improvements. Biology-aware optimization therefore looks like a separate capability that autonomous laboratory loops should target directly.
  </details>

- **[Language Modeling with Hyperspherical Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11125)**  `arXiv:2605.11125`  
  _Justin Deschenaux, Caglar Gulcehre_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrete Diffusion Language Models progressed rapidly as an alternative to autoregressive (AR) models, motivated by their parallel generation abilities. However, for tractability, discrete diffusion models sample from a factorized distribution, which is less expressive than AR. Recent Flow Language Models (FLMs) apply continuous flows to language, transporting noise to data with a deterministic ODE that avoids factorized sampling. FLMs operate on one-hot vectors whose dimension scales with the vocabulary size, making FLMs costly to train. Moreover, since all distinct one-hot embeddings are equidistant in $\ell_2$, adding Gaussian noise does not have a clear semantic interpretation (unlike images, where Gaussian noise progressively degrades structure). We introduce $\mathbb{S}$-FLM, a latent FLM in the hypersphere. $\mathbb{S}$-FLM generates sequences by rotating vectors in $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ along a velocity field learned with cross-entropy, avoiding the overhead of materializing one-hot vectors. Previous FLMs match AR in Generative Perplexity (Gen.\ PPL), but samples with high likelihood are not necessarily correct in verifiable domains such as math and code. $\mathbb{S}$-FLM substantially improves continuous flow language models on large-vocabulary reasoning and closes the gap to masked diffusion under standard-temperature sampling ($T=1$), while a gap remains under optimized low-temperature ($T=0.1$) decoding.
  </details>

- **[Path-Coupled Bellman Flows for Distributional Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08253)**  `arXiv:2605.08253`  
  _Boyang Xu, Qing Zou, Siqin Yang, Hao Yan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distributional reinforcement learning (DRL) models the full return distribution, but existing finite-support or quantile-based methods rely on projections, while recent flow-based approaches can suffer from \emph{boundary mismatch} at the flow source or from \emph{high-variance} bootstrapping when current and successor noises are independent. We propose Path-Coupled Bellman Flows (PCBF), a continuous-time DRL method that learns return distributions with flow matching using \textbf{source-consistent Bellman-coupled paths}: the current path starts from the required base prior at $t{=}0$, reaches the Bellman target at $t{=}1$, and maintains a pathwise affine relation to the successor flow at intermediate times (without requiring time-$t$ marginals to satisfy a distributional Bellman fixed point for all $t$). PCBF couples current and successor return flows through shared base noise and uses a $\lambda$-parameterized control-variate target: $\lambda{=}0$ recovers an unbiased sample Bellman target, while $\lambda{>}0$ trades controlled bias for variance reduction. Experiments on analytically tractable MRPs, OGBench, and D4RL show improved distributional fidelity and training stability, and competitive offline RL performance.
  </details>

- **[RelAgent: LLM Agents as Data Scientists for Relational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07840)**  `arXiv:2605.07840`  
  _Xingyue Huang, Louis Tichelman, Jinwoo Kim, Krzysztof Olejniczak, İsmail İlkan Ceylan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Relational learning is a challenging problem that has motivated a wide range of approaches, including graph-based models (e.g., graph neural networks, graph transformers), tabular methods (e.g., tabular foundation models), and sequence-based approaches (e.g., large language models), each with its own advantages and limitations. We propose RelAgent, an LLM-based autonomous data scientist for relational learning, which operates in two phases. In the search phase, an LLM agent uses database, validation, and evaluation workspace tools to construct SQL feature programs and select a predictive model. In the inference phase, the resulting program is executed without further LLM calls. The final predictor consists of SQL queries and a classical model, enabling fast, deterministic, and intrinsically interpretable predictions: features are human-readable queries, and predictions depend only on the resulting query-defined feature map, enabling scalable deployment using standard database systems.
  </details>

- **[Rank-Variance Scores for Importance Sampling and Data Pruning under Label Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07551)**  `arXiv:2605.07551`  
  _Csongor Horváth, Ida-Maria Sintorn, Prashant Singh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Variance-optimal importance sampling and magnitude-based pruning prioritize high-loss or large-gradient examples, which under label contamination are often the corrupted ones. Under Huber's $\varepsilon$-contamination model, we characterize this failure exactly: a score sampler's share of visits to corrupted examples is set by their mean score relative to the dataset mean, and a pruned subset's contamination is tightly bounded by the score's corruption-detection AUROC; at every loss-based score we measure, pruning is provably no cleaner than random selection. A useful score must therefore be non-monotone in the loss. We build such a score from the loss ranks of $K$ independently trained proxies: a mislabelled example ranks consistently hardest, a near-boundary example unstably. Its total rank variance (TRV) splits into a between-proxy part, Disagreement-Regularized Importance Sampling (DR-IS), and a within-proxy part; the split predicts that DR-IS is the better sampler and TRV the better pruning rule, both checkable without labels before training. Experiments on CIFAR-10/100 under label noise confirm both roles: the rules substantially outperform random selection where magnitude-based rules collapse, at a proxy cost of 2-4% of training.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Pluralistic Alignment: A pipeline for dynamic artificial democracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01642)**  `arXiv:2605.01642`  
  _Rachel Freedman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prevailing alignment methods target a fixed set of preferences and therefore risk forcing value lock-in as societal norms evolve over time. We introduce Adaptive Pluralistic Alignment (APA), a modular pipeline for updating pluralistically aligned AI systems to track evolving values and avoid value lock-in without repeating costly pretraining or large-scale data collection. APA has three stages: (1) learning compact personalized reward models via low-rank reward basis decomposition, (2) using these models as a jury that collectively selects among candidate outputs through social-choice-theoretic voting, and (3) efficiently adapting the jury over time by fitting new annotator weights over the fixed reward bases as values shift. The resulting system is efficient, explainable, steerable, and modular. We implement a proof-of-concept instantiation using simulated historical annotators, demonstrate how jury composition and the choice of voting rule can substantially affect outcomes, and bound value tracking error given a sliding-window jury. We provide full code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ProMORNA: An End-to-End Generative Framework for Full-Length mRNA Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01513)**  `arXiv:2605.01513`  
  _Zixi Shao, Tao Wang, Ye Shen, Tianyi Huang, Yibei Xiao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing therapeutic messenger RNA (mRNA) requires generating full-length transcripts that optimally balance intracellular stability and translation efficiency. While generative models have advanced mRNA design, existing approaches rely on region-wise generation, expensive inference-time search, or target-specific model updates. To overcome these limitations, we introduce ProMORNA, a zero-shot, end-to-end generative framework for full-length mRNA design. ProMORNA aligns the design process with modern Large Language Model training pipelines: it utilizes a BART-style architecture pre-trained on over 6 million natural protein-mRNA pairs, followed by a two-stage post-training process comprising cold-start supervised fine-tuning and multi-objective reinforcement learning. By standardizing and aggregating relative advantages at the individual metric level with domain-specific stabilization, our post-training stage directly aligns the generative policy with conflicting biological constraints while avoiding direct scalarization of raw objective scores. In silico evaluations demonstrate that ProMORNA pushes the Pareto frontier of predicted stability and translation efficiency beyond standard baselines and GEMORNA, a leading state-of-the-art method. In three independent in vitro Fluc2P experiments, Candidate 2 achieved a 12-72h reporter-activity AUC 3.33 times that of the conventional Benchmark (Holm-adjusted $P=0.01285$), while its estimated 1.29-fold advantage over the reported best GEMORNA candidate remained exploratory. These results support ProMORNA as a promising framework for generating functionally active full-length mRNA candidates.
  </details>

- **[A Mechanistic Study of Algorithmic Generalization in Diffusion Models on Modular Arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17673)**  `arXiv:2604.17673`  
  _Joon Hyeok Kim, Yong-Hyun Park, Mattis Dalsætra Østby, Jiatao Gu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite their empirical success, how diffusion models generalize remains poorly understood from a mechanistic perspective. In this work, we provide a controlled mechanistic account of algorithmic learning in diffusion models. By utilizing the grokking phenomenon on an image-based modular addition task as a tractable testbed, we dissect the model's internal dynamics across two distinct visual regimes. In a single-image regime, mechanistic dissection reveals that the model implements modular addition by encoding operands as periodic representations and composing them at the self-attention--feedforward interface in a way that recovers the trigonometric addition identities. In a diverse-image regime with high intraclass variability, we find that the iterative sampling process partitions the task into an arithmetic computation phase and a subsequent visual refinement phase, with their transition precisely captured by an internal frequency-entropy signal. Together, our analysis clarifies the mechanics of algorithmic generalization of diffusion models in a controlled setting, while leaving open how broadly these mechanisms transfer beyond modular addition.
  </details>

- **[MCLR: Improving Conditional Modeling via Inter-Class Likelihood-Ratio Maximization and Unifying Classifier-Free Guidance with Alignment Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22364)**  `arXiv:2603.22364`  
  _Xiang Li, Yixuan Jia, Xiao Li, Jeffrey A. Fessler, Rongrong Wang, Qing Qu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models achieve strong generative performance but often rely on classifier-free guidance (CFG), an inference-time intervention that modifies the sampling trajectory. Since standard denoising score matching (DSM) should recover the target distribution when the score is learned accurately, the practical necessity of CFG suggests deficiencies in learned conditional scores. We identify weak inter-class separation as one such limitation: samples generated from the same noise under different labels can retain similar class-agnostic structure. To address this deficiency, we introduce MCLR, a principled fine-tuning objective that explicitly maximizes inter-class likelihood-ratios, thereby strengthening class-specific structures under standard sampling. Across diverse models and datasets, MCLR substantially improves guidance-free conditional generation and narrows the gap to CFG. Theoretically, we prove that the CFG-guided score is the unique minimizer of a sample-adaptively weighted, ELBO-approximated MCLR objective, interpreting CFG as an implicit inference-time contrastive alignment procedure. We further compare MCLR with related contrastive objectives, including DPO, CCA, and CFM, clarifying their connections and key differences in an otherwise fragmented literature.
  </details>

- **[Flow Matching Policy Optimization with Mirror Descent and Entropy Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17685)**  `arXiv:2603.17685`  
  _Ting Gao, Stavros Orfanoudakis, Nan Lin, Winnie Daamen, Serge Hoogendoorn, Elvin Isufi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Balancing policy expressiveness with the exploration-exploitation trade-off is a core challenge in online Reinforcement Learning (RL). While Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE)-based diffusion policies can represent complex, multimodal action distributions, they suffer from two critical limitations: their stochastic reverse processes render entropy intractable (necessitating heuristic exploration), and computing policy gradients through long denoising chains is expensive and unstable. In this work, we show that ODE-based flow matching inherently resolves these issues by enabling both simulation-free policy optimization and tractable entropy computation. Building on this, we introduce Flow Matching Policy Optimization with Mirror Descent and Entropy Constraints (FMER). Our framework exploits this insight in three ways. First, we derive an analytic entropy objective that corrects for the density distortion caused by the $\tanh$ transformation (mapping an unbounded latent space to bounded actions), thereby facilitating entropy-constrained policy optimization. Second, we theoretically establish that minimizing an advantage-weighted conditional flow matching loss acts as a simulation-free surrogate for policy mirror descent. This steers the velocity field toward high-value regions while entirely avoiding backpropagation through the ODE solver. Finally, we dynamically tune the mirror descent temperature based on the effective sample size to enforce a robust trust region during training. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that FMER achieves superior performance on the challenging sparse-reward FrankaKitchen environment, while maintaining competitive results across standard dense-reward MuJoCo benchmarks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SYNAPSE: Framework for Neuron Analysis and Perturbation in Sequence Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08424)**  `arXiv:2603.08424`  
  _Jesús Sánchez Ochoa, Enrique Tomás Martínez Beltrán, Alberto Huertas Celdrán_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, Artificial Intelligence has become a powerful partner for complex tasks such as data analysis, prediction, and problem-solving, yet its lack of transparency raises concerns about its reliability. In sensitive domains such as healthcare or cybersecurity, ensuring transparency, trustworthiness, and robustness is essential, since the consequences of wrong decisions or successful attacks can be severe. To overcome these limitations, this work proposes SYNAPSE, a systematic, training-free framework (requiring no retraining of the base model) for understanding and stress-testing the internal behavior of Transformer models across domains. SYNAPSE extracts per-layer [CLS] representations, trains a lightweight linear probe to obtain global and per-class neuron rankings, and applies forward-hook interventions during inference.
